NDA 220152
NEREUS (tradipitant)

I. Executive Summary
1. Overview
1.1. Summary of Regulatory Action
On December 30, 2024, Vanda Pharmaceuticals (“the Applicant”) submitted a new drug
application (NDA) 220152 for tradipitant 85-mg oral capsules under the 505(b)(1) regulatory
pathway. The Applicant’s proposed indication is the “acute prevention of vomiting induced by
motion in adults.” At the time of this review, tradipitant was not approved for marketing in any
country.
The Applicant conducted two phase 3 randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled trials, VPVLY-686-3401 (Trial 3401) and VP-3404 (Trial 3404). The submission also contained data from
a phase 2 randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled trial, VP-VLY-686-2401 (Trial 2401),
and interim data from an ongoing phase 3 open-label, uncontrolled trial, VP-VLY-686-3403
(Trial 3403). Trials 3401 and 3404 evaluated the safety and efficacy of a single dose of
tradipitant for the prevention of vomiting induced by motion. Trial 3403 evaluated as needed
administration of tradipitant for the prevention of vomiting induced by motion. The data from
Trial 3403, the only repeat-dosage trial, was limited to a maximum of 90 dosing days over the
course of the 12-month trial. The Applicant did not have adequate nonclinical safety data to
support more than 90 dosing days in a clinical trial. As a result, at the time of submission of the
NDA, Trial 3403 was on partial clinical hold as there was insufficient information to assess risks
to humans beyond 90 dosing days.
The primary endpoint of Trials 3401 and 3404 was the percentage of subjects who vomited
during the vehicle travel event in the tradipitant 170-mg treatment arm as compared to placebo.
In Trial 3401, there was a statistically significantly lower percentage of subjects who vomited in
the tradipitant 170-mg treatment arm compared to placebo (risk difference [95% CI], -25.9% [37.1, -14.7], p<0.0001). The percentage of subjects who vomited during the vehicle travel event
in the tradipitant 85-mg treatment arm was a secondary endpoint and was nominally significant
(risk difference [95% CI], -24.9 [-36.0, -13.9], p<0.0001).1 Additional secondary endpoints for
other symptoms of motion sickness (e.g., worst nausea severity, Motion Sickness Assessment
Questionnaire [MSAQ], Motion Sickness Severity Scale [MSSS]) were not statistically
significant. In Trial 3404, there was a statistically significantly lower percentage of subjects who
vomited in the tradipitant 170-mg treatment arm compared to placebo (risk difference [95% CI],
-27.4% [-38.3, -16.5], p<0.0001). The percentage of subjects who vomited during the vehicle
travel event in the tradipitant 85-mg treatment arm was a secondary endpoint and was also
statistically significant (risk difference [95% CI], -19.5 [-31.3, -7.6], p=0.0025). Similar to

1

In the prespecified multiple-testing procedure, the Applicant tested the secondary endpoint of worst nausea
severity during the vehicle travel event in the 170-mg tradipitant arm, after the primary endpoint. For this endpoint,
the 170-mg tradipitant arm was not statistically significantly different than placebo. Thus, according to the multipletesting procedure, other endpoints tested after this endpoint are not considered statistically significant due to a lack
of type I error control.

3
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5720419

NDA 220152
NEREUS (tradipitant)
Trial 3401, additional secondary endpoints for other symptoms of motion sickness (e.g., worst
nausea severity, MSAQ, MSSS) were not statistically significant.
Safety data from the single-dose, placebo-controlled Trials 3401 and 3404 adequately
characterized the safety of a single dose of tradipitant for the prevention of vomiting induced by
motion. There were no serious adverse events (SAEs) or adverse events (AEs) that led to
discontinuation reported for Trials 3401 and 3404. The most common AEs were somnolence,
fatigue, and headache for both 170-mg tradipitant and 85-mg tradipitant treatment arms in a
dose-dependent manner. The only source of repeat-dose safety information for the proposed
intended use was from Trial 3403; however, the data for Trial 3403 submitted to the NDA were
limited to a maximum of 90 dosing days over the course of the 12-month trial. Additionally,
although subjects were eligible to use tradipitant for up to 90 dosing days, the majority of
subjects used tradipitant for 30 dosing days or fewer (170-mg tradipitant 67.2% [123/183], 85mg tradipitant 67.8% [135/199]). Overall, the most common adverse events in Trial 3403 were
similar to those in the single-dose trials; however, limitations in the design of Trial 3403 (e.g.,
variability in the timing and frequency of tradipitant administration, infrequent follow-up visits
[i.e., every 4 months], subject e-diaries lacking questions about adverse events, lack of a
comparator arm) resulted in uncertainty attributing the causality of adverse events to tradipitant
versus other factors, as well as an increased likelihood that adverse events were under-reported.
The Applicant also submitted safety data from trials conducted in subjects with gastroparesis;
however, the dosage used (i.e., 85 mg twice daily [BID]) and the patient population differ from
the proposed dosage and intended use population for the prevention of vomiting induced by
motion. Notably, clinical trials in gastroparesis have been limited to 12 weeks in duration.
Additionally, these trials include placebo-controlled data from a phase 2 trial over a 4-week
period in 64 subjects and from a phase 3 trial over a 12-week period in 74 subjects, while the
remaining data are uncontrolled and from either open-label studies2 or individual patient
expanded access protocols. Therefore, although the safety data from subjects with gastroparesis
were considered, these data do not provide information that characterizes safety of tradipitant for
use beyond 90 days, and do not address the limitations of the available safety information for the
prevention of vomiting induced by motion.
To support the marketing of tradipitant for the prevention of vomiting induced by motion, the
Applicant conducted a battery of nonclinical tests with tradipitant, including pharmacology,
safety pharmacology, pharmacokinetic (PK), general toxicology, reproductive toxicology, and
carcinogenicity studies. In addition, the Applicant conducted five human cell-based in vitro
safety pharmacology studies and two special toxicology studies using alternative assays, also
referred to as new approach methodologies (NAMs). At the time of the NDA submission,
general toxicity studies with tradipitant included studies of up to 6 months in rats and up to 3
months in dogs. Because vomiting induced by motion is a condition that is expected to recur
repeatedly each time the motion stimulus is present, the Division had previously recommended
that the Applicant conduct a chronic (e.g., ≥6-month) toxicity study in a nonrodent species to
adequately assess the risk of cumulative toxicity following repeated short-term exposures to
tradipitant and, thus, support the safety of unrestricted use of tradipitant over a lifetime, for the
2

The phase 2 trial had an 8-week open-label treatment period for a total treatment duration of 12 weeks. The phase
3 trial had a separate open-label treatment single arm with a treatment duration of 12 weeks.

4
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5720419

NDA 220152
NEREUS (tradipitant)
indication of the prevention of vomiting induced by motion.3 Based on the review team’s
experience as well as the team’s understanding of the scientific literature regarding the utility of
toxicology studies to support the safety of human drug products, the recommended nonclinical
data (i.e., a 6-month toxicity study in rodents and a ≥6-month toxicity study in a nonrodent
species) are the most appropriate to support the safety of drug products used over a person’s
lifetime, such as tradipitant for the prevention of vomiting induced by motion. However, the
Applicant did not submit a ≥6-month toxicity study in a nonrodent species and did not provide
sufficient scientific justification for why such a study was not warranted to support the safety of
unrestricted use of tradipitant over a lifetime for this indication. Importantly, the relatively long
half-life of tradipitant and its major human metabolites (i.e., ≥ 28 hours) supports that even
single doses spaced days apart could lead to sustained exposure to and even accumulation of
tradipitant. Moreover, the unrestricted use of tradipitant over a lifetime for the prevention of
vomiting induced by motion could expose some individuals to levels of tradipitant over a
duration of use that do not have adequate nonclinical assessment of the toxicological risks (see
Section 7.7). Thus, there is insufficient nonclinical information to adequately characterize the
safety of unrestricted use of tradipitant over a lifetime in human subjects, and, therefore, the
available nonclinical data do not support that tradipitant should be considered safe for
unrestricted use. The review team identified that the limitations of the nonclinical data were an
approvability issue.4
The review team considered the nature of vomiting induced by motion (i.e., generally a selflimited condition characterized by acute episodes that are predictable, infrequent, and in many
cases avoidable) and that clinical use would likely mirror the infrequent use observed in
Trial 3403 (e.g., the majority of patients did not use more than 2 consecutive doses, the median
exposure was 15 and 18 doses over 12 months for the 85-mg arm and 170-mg arm, respectively,
the vast majority of subjects did not approach the 90 dose maximum over the 12 month trial
duration).5 Given that vomiting induced by motion is expected to recur and may result in repeat
administrations of tradipitant on multiple occasions over a lifetime of an individual, the team
also considered the limitations and the uncertainties of the available safety information that could
inform unrestricted use of tradipitant. Based on these considerations, the review team determined
that a favorable risk-benefit assessment may be supported for the prevention of vomiting induced
by motion in adults provided the limitations of the safety data could be adequately
communicated in the label to ensure the safe use of the product. The originally proposed product
labeling was modified to describe the upper limit beyond which safety has not been established
(i.e., more than 90 doses). The clinical review team expects that stating that safety has not been
established for more than 90 total doses will adequately inform prescribers of the safe use of the
drug according to the recommended dosage and administration described in the label. This
3

IND 141315: Advice Letter dated February 28, 2023.
The review team had extensive discussions regarding whether the application should be filed, based on the concern
that the submitted nonclinical information was incomplete for the proposed indication due to the lack of a nonrodent
toxicity study of ≥6 months duration. Although the absence of the nonrodent toxicity study of ≥6 months duration
could be considered a deficiency that results in refusing to file an application, the review team determined that the
proposed indication (i.e., prevention of vomiting induced by motion, a condition that is induced by an external
stimulus with no symptoms in the absence of the stimulus) warranted review of the application to determine whether
the available data are adequate to characterize the safety of tradipitant for the proposed indication and to inform
potential labeling recommendations.

description of the limitations of the available safety data for more than 90 total doses was
supported by the safety data from tradipitant clinical trials in the intended use population and
other populations, which were limited to 90 dosing days or daily dosing for up to 12 weeks.
Therefore, the benefits of tradipitant for this indication outweigh the risks when used according
to the recommended dosage and administration.

1.2. Conclusions on Substantial Evidence of
Effectiveness
Substantial evidence of effectiveness (SEE) was established with two or more adequate and wellcontrolled clinical investigations.


VP-VLY-686-3401 (Trial 3401): A multicenter, randomized, double-blind, placebocontrolled trial to investigate the efficacy and safety of a single dose of tradipitant 85 mg or
170 mg in subjects with a history of motion sickness. Subjects (N=365) were randomized
1:1:1 to tradipitant 85 mg (n=123), tradipitant 170 mg (n=120), or placebo (n=122). Subjects
were administered the investigational product approximately 60 minutes before a planned
vehicle travel event via a boat. During the vehicle travel event, subjects completed nausea
and vomiting questionnaires every 30 minutes, and additional assessments were performed at
the end of the travel event. The primary endpoint was the percentage of subjects with
vomiting during the vehicle travel event in the tradipitant 170-mg treatment arm. The
percentage of subjects with vomiting during the vehicle travel event in the tradipitant 85-mg
treatment arm was a secondary endpoint. The results showed a statistically significant
reduction in the percentage of subjects with vomiting in the 170 mg treatment arm (18.3% vs.
44.3%; risk difference [95% CI] -25.9% [-37.1%, -14.7%], p<0.0001) and a nominally
significant reduction in the 85 mg treatment arm (19.5% vs. 44.3%; risk difference [95% CI]
-24.9% [-36.0%, -13.9%], p<0.0001). These results support the efficacy of tradipitant for the
proposed indication.



VP-VLY-686-3404 (Trial 3404): Trial 3404 was nearly identical to Trial 3401. Trial 3404
was a multicenter, randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled trial to investigate the
efficacy and safety of a single dose of tradipitant 85 mg or 170 mg in subjects with a history
of motion sickness. Subjects (N=316) were randomized 1:1:1 to tradipitant 85 mg (n=104),
tradipitant 170 mg (n=106), or placebo (n=106). Subjects were administered the
investigational product approximately 60 minutes before a planned vehicle travel event via a
boat. During the vehicle travel event, subjects completed nausea and vomiting questionnaires
every 30 minutes, and additional assessments were performed at the end of the travel event.
The primary endpoint was the percentage of subjects with vomiting during the vehicle travel
event in the tradipitant 170-mg treatment arm. The percentage of subjects with vomiting
during the vehicle travel event in the tradipitant 85-mg treatment arm was a secondary
endpoint. The results showed a statistically significant reduction in the percentage of subjects
with vomiting in the 170 mg treatment arm (10.4% vs. 37.7%; risk difference [95% CI]
27.4% [-38.3%, -16.5%], p<0.0001) and in the 85 mg treatment arm (18.3% vs. 37.7%; risk
difference [95% CI] -19.5% [-31.3%, -7.6%], p=0.0025). These results support the efficacy
of tradipitant for the proposed indication.

Trials 3401 and 3404 were two adequate and well-controlled clinical investigations assessing a
single dose of tradipitant prior to a travel event that provide SEE to support the approval of
tradipitant for the proposed indication of prevention of vomiting induced by motion in adults.

2. Benefit-Risk Assessment
2.1. Benefit-Risk Framework

 Some individuals are at risk for developing vomiting induced by
condition
motion.
 In affected individuals, symptoms are typically triggered by lateral
and vertical motion (e.g., travel by sea, virtual simulator motion),
which signal afferents from the vestibular apparatus, visual system,
and proprioceptive systems to trigger the vestibular nuclei of the
brainstem. In response, efferent projections to multiple brain and
brainstem regions trigger autonomic reactions and the “vomiting
center,” leading to vomiting.
 Vomiting induced by motion is one manifestation of the complex and
multisystem disorder commonly referred to as “motion sickness.”
Other signs and symptoms may include nausea, confusion, fatigue,
dizziness, malaise, headache, pallor, and diaphoresis.
 Vomiting induced by motion is expected to recur in susceptible
individuals upon reintroduction of the triggering motion stimulus.
 Vomiting induced by motion may have significant impacts on quality
of life and the ability to perform tasks during or following the motion
event.
 Previously affected or susceptible individuals often seek treatment
to prevent the occurrence of these symptoms prior to onset or prior
to encountering an inducing event.
Current
treatment
options





Behavioral modifications may be used to manage symptoms of
nausea and vomiting induced by motion, including avoidance of
triggering travel events, positional changes (e.g., sitting in the front
seat of a car), avoiding conflicts between vestibular and visual cues
(e.g., reading), habituation, and distraction techniques.
There are several prescription products (i.e., Phenergan and
Transderm Scop) and therapies available over-the-counter for the
prevention and treatment of the nausea, vomiting, or dizziness
associated with motion sickness, including cyclizine hydrochloride,

Taken together, the safety of more than 90 doses of
tradipitant has not been established in clinical trials.

with a higher tradipitant exposure; therefore, tradipitant
should be taken without food. The Applicant has agreed to
conduct a thorough QT study to evaluate the effect of
tradipitant on QT prolongation in the fed state.

2.2. Conclusions Regarding Benefit-Risk
The data submitted in the NDA demonstrated a favorable benefit-risk profile for the indication of
the prevention of vomiting induced by motion in adults with a description that the safety of more
than 90 total doses has not been established. Vomiting induced by motion is one of several
heterogeneous symptoms of motion sickness. Motion sickness is a generally self-limited
condition characterized by episodes of symptoms associated with motion, which are generally
predictable, and in many cases, avoidable. Vomiting induced by motion is expected to recur in
susceptible individuals upon repeat exposure to motion; however, in most individuals, travel
events that are anticipated to cause vomiting induced by motion that require preventative
treatment are infrequent, as supported by the tradipitant use data from the repeat-dose trial, Trial
3403, in which tradipitant was administered on an as-needed basis.
Two adequate and well-controlled single-dose trials (Trials 3401 and 3404) provided substantial
evidence of effectiveness, showing statistically significant reductions in the proportion of
subjects with vomiting compared to placebo for the tradipitant 170-mg arms, a nominally
significant reduction in the proportion of subjects who vomited in the tradipitant 85-mg arm
compared to placebo in Trial 3401, and a statistically significant reduction in the proportion of
subjects who vomited in the tradipitant 85-mg arm compared to placebo in Trial 3404. Notably,
while tradipitant was effective in preventing vomiting induced by motion, it did not demonstrate
efficacy for the prevention of nausea induced by motion. Thus, the indication of “prevention of
vomiting induced by motion” is appropriate based on the trial results. In addition, the results of
the worst nausea score secondary endpoint are included in the label to inform prescribers of the
lack of efficacy for the prevention of nausea induced by motion as nausea is a common symptom
of motion sickness.
The safety of tradipitant for the prevention of vomiting induced by motion was primarily
informed by the results of the randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled, single-dose trials
(Trials 2401, 2401, and 3404). The most common adverse reactions in these trials were
somnolence, headache, and fatigue. There were no notable serious adverse events, adverse events
that led to discontinuation, laboratory abnormalities, or vital sign changes; however, the trials
evaluated only a single dose of tradipitant. Safety information for repeated administration of
tradipitant in the intended use population was provided by the open-label, repeat-dose, as needed
trial, Trial 3403. Overall, the adverse events reported during Trial 3403 were similar to those of
Trials 3401 and 3404; however, the interpretation of the safety information from the trial was
limited by several factors (e.g., infrequent safety assessments, variability in timing/frequency of
tradipitant administration, lack of a comparator arm). The Applicant also submitted safety data
from trials conducted in unapproved populations, which included up to 85 mg twice daily for 12
weeks. The available clinical safety data submitted to the application were limited to 90 dosing
days or 12 weeks because, at the time of the NDA submission, clinical trials with a longer
duration were on partial clinical hold. The nonclinical data remained insufficient in this NDA
submission. The Applicant did not submit sufficient nonclinical data to support unrestricted use
of tradipitant over a lifetime. Additionally, the relatively long half-life of tradipitant and its major
human metabolites (i.e., ≥ 28 hours) supports that even single doses spaced days apart could lead
to sustained exposure to and even accumulation of tradipitant. Furthermore, the unrestricted use
of tradipitant over a lifetime for the prevention of vomiting due to motion would expose some
individuals to levels of tradipitant over a duration of use that does not have adequate nonclinical
13
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5720419

NDA 220152
NEREUS (tradipitant)
assessment of the toxicological risks (see Section 7.7). Therefore, the available nonclinical data
do not support that tradipitant is safe for unrestricted use over a lifetime.
Based on the available safety data, tradipitant is recommended for approval with a description in
the label that the safety of tradipitant has not been established for more than 90 total doses to
adequately inform prescribers of the safe use of tradipitant.
The following postmarketing requirements will be issued:


Tradipitant is anticipated to be used by women of childbearing potential and it is not known
whether tradipitant may affect maternal, fetal, and infant outcomes. Therefore, postmarketing
requirements (PMRs) will be issued under 505(o) to collect data from a 1) prospective
pregnancy registry, 2) a retrospective pregnancy cohort study, and 3) a lactation study in
lactating women.



Tradipitant is a new molecular entity and vomiting induced by motion is experienced by
pediatric subjects. Therefore, PMRs will be issued under PREA to 1) evaluate the PK of an
age-appropriate liquid formulation of tradipitant and 2) conduct a randomized, double-blind,
placebo-controlled clinical trial to evaluate the safety and efficacy of tradipitant in pediatric
patients 2 to <18 years of age.

The Applicant agreed to the following postmarketing commitments:


A clinical pharmacology study to evaluate the effects of hepatic impairment on the PK of
tradipitant and its major metabolites.



A clinical pharmacology study to evaluate the effects of severe renal impairment on the PK
of tradipitant and its major metabolites.



An in vitro assessment to evaluate the contribution of all major CYP enzymes to the
metabolism of tradipitant and its active metabolites, and to assess the relative contribution of
specific CYP enzymes to tradipitant metabolism. The results of the in vitro study will
determine the need for a subsequent drug-drug interaction study(ies).



A thorough QT study to evaluate the effects of tradipitant on QT intervals in the fed state.

II. Interdisciplinary Assessment
3. Introduction
Vanda Pharmaceuticals submitted NDA 220152 for tradipitant 85-mg oral capsules. Tradipitant
is a neurokinin-1 (NK-1) receptor antagonist, which has a proposed mechanism of action of
blocking substance P from binding to NK-1 receptors in the brain and gastrointestinal tract, and
subsequently produces anti-emetic effects.
Tradipitant is currently not approved for marketing in any country. The Applicant proposes to
market tradipitant 85 mg or 170 mg, administered orally without food approximately 60 minutes
prior to the desired antiemetic effect, for the following indication: “[TRADENAME] is a
(b) (4) substance P/neurokinin 1 (NK1) receptor antagonist indicated for the acute prevention
of vomiting induced by motion in adults.” Reviews of three proposed proprietary names were
conducted during the course of the review of this application. The first proposed proprietary
(b) (4) was determined to be unacceptable on April 14, 2025. 6 The second proposed
name,
(b) (4) was determined to be unacceptable on September 24, 2025. 7 The third
proprietary name,
proposed proprietary name, NEREUS, was determined to be acceptable on December 5, 2025.8

3.2. Approach to the Clinical Review
As described in Section 6.2, the efficacy review focused on controlled data from the phase 3
randomized placebo-controlled trials, Trials 3401 and 3404. As described in Section 7.4, the
safety review focused on data from the phase 2 and phase 3 randomized, double-blind, placebocontrolled trials, Trials 2401, 3401, and 3404. In addition, data from the phase 3 open-label
uncontrolled phase 3 trial, Trial 3403, were considered.

5. Pharmacologic Activity, Pharmacokinetics,
and Clinical Pharmacology
5.1. Nonclinical Assessment of Potential
Effectiveness
Tradipitant is a selective, competitive inhibitor of the human cell membrane NK-1 receptor in
vitro. Tradipitant completely inhibited ligand binding to the human NK-1 receptor in vitro at a
concentration of 10μM (5.9 μg/mL). The half-maximal inhibitory concentrations (IC50 values) of
tradipitant for binding at the NK-1 receptor were 0.40 and 3.3nM (0.24 and 1.9 µg/mL,
respectively) in two separate studies. Tradipitant was also shown to inhibit substance P-mediated

18
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5720419

NDA 220152
NEREUS (tradipitant)
calcium release in human embryonic kidney (HEK) 293 cells with an IC50 value of 0.63nM
(0.37 µg/mL).
The four major human metabolites of tradipitant, M2, M3, M4, and M8, have been shown or are
presumed to be pharmacologically active based on available data. All four metabolites inhibited
ligand binding to the NK-1 receptor in vitro by ≥99.0% at a concentration of 10µM. M8 was
shown to inhibit binding at the receptor with an IC50 of 0.49nM and to inhibit substance Pmediated calcium release in vitro with an IC50 of 6.3nM. M2, M3, and M4 were reported by the
Applicant12 to inhibit binding to the NK-1 receptor with IC50 values of 1.55, 0.38, and 2.23nM,
respectively; the functional activity of these metabolites was not tested.
In a study in male Beagle dogs, a single oral dose of tradipitant at 0.3, 1.0, or 3.0 mg/kg (less
than the clinical dose of tradipitant of 170 mg/day, based on human equivalent dose [HED])
administered 2 hours prior to challenge with the pro-emetogenic drug, apomorphine, resulted in
significantly decreased incidence of emesis compared to no treatment.

12

These values were reported by the Applicant in Module 2.6.2 Pharmacology Written Summary (based on Studies
AB28300-1190601, AB28300-1190602, AB28300-1190603, and AB28300-1190604). However, IC50 values for
M2, M3, and M4 were not stated in these study reports.

5.2. Clinical Pharmacology/Pharmacokinetics
This submission consists of 11 phase 1 trials in healthy subjects, one phase 2 trial, and two completed phase 3 trials in subjects with
history of a motion sickness.
Table 5. Summary of Clinical Pharmacology and Pharmacokinetics
Characteristic
Drug Information
Pharmacologic Activity
Established pharmacologic class Substance P/ NK-1 receptor antagonist.
(EPC)
Mechanism of action
Tradipitant is a competitive inhibitor of the NK-1 receptor.
Active moieties
Tradipitant is the active moiety. Its metabolites M2, M3, M4, and M8 are also pharmacologically active as NK1 inhibitors, the IC50 of M8 was 6.7-fold lower than tradipitant but the IC50 values of other metabolites were not
determined. In the mass balance study, based on AUCinf, tradipitant, M2, M3, and M4 accounted for
approximately 9%, 4%, 5%, and 8% of plasma radioactivity, respectively (Study H8R-MC-HJAK). Following a
single dose of 85 mg in healthy subjects under fasted condition, the AUCinf ratio between M2, M3, M4, and M8
to tradipitant was 40%, 33%, 43%, and 42%, respectively (Trial VP-VLY-686-1002).
QT prolongation
No thorough QT (TQT) study was conducted. The Applicant submitted an integrated assessment of cardiac
safety. The integrated assessment included Trial H8REW-HJAC, which was considered the primary study
supporting QTc assessment, as it included placebo and serial time-matched ECG/PK collection. The results of
the trial support that tradipitant did not cause clinically relevant QTc prolongation. However, the risk of QTc
prolongation has not been characterized for tradipitant when administered with food, which results in a 4.7-fold
increase in Cmax for the 85 mg dose and 7.0-fold increase in Cmax for the 170 mg dose. The Applicant agreed
to conduct a thorough QT study as a postmarketing commitment to address the potential for QTc prolongation
when administered with food. Refer to Section 24.
General Information
Bioanalysis
Concentrations of tradipitant and its metabolites (M3, M4, M6, and M8) in human plasma samples have been
measured using adequately validated LC-MS/MS assays in clinical studies. All clinical samples were analyzed
within the established stability period. Overall, the bioanalytical method validation and performance is
acceptable.
Healthy subjects versus patients The general population of patients experiencing motion sickness is not expected to have significantly different
systemic exposure compared to healthy subjects.

Drug Information
While population PK analysis was unable to adequately predict the exposure of tradipitant and its metabolites
due to limitations in the dataset and analysis methodology, disease condition was not identified as a covariate
affecting pharmacokinetics in subjects with motion sickness. Refer to Section 14.5 for additional details.

Range of effective dose(s) or
exposure

In the randomized, placebo-controlled phase 3 trials (Trials 3401 and 3404), a single dose of 85-mg and 170mg tradipitant was evaluated in subjects with a history of motion sickness during vehicle travel. In Trial 3401,
the percentage of subjects who vomited was significantly lower in both the 85-mg tradipitant arm (19.5%,
p<0.0001) and the 170-mg tradipitant arm (18.3%, p<0.0001) as compared to the placebo arm (44.3%). In
Trial 3404, the percentage of subjects who vomited was significantly lower in both the 170-mg tradipitant arm,
(10.4%, p<0.0001) and the 85-mg tradipitant arm (18.3%, p=0.0014) as compared to the placebo arm
(37.7%).
In healthy subjects, single dose up to 800 mg given under fasted condition was well tolerated. The 800 mg
single dose administered in Trial H8R-EW-HJAC was a different formulation than the proposed TBM
formulation. The mean Cmax and AUC0-inf of tradipitant are 277 ng/mL and 4620 ng∙h/mL, respectively (Trial
H8R-EW-HJAC). While no relative bioavailability study was conducted, cross-study comparison showed that
tradipitant Cmax and AUC0-inf after single dose of 800 mg are 3.3-fold and 2.5-fold of those with the proposed
to-be-marketed (TBM) formulation at 85 mg single dose, respectively, and 2.5-fold and 1.3-fold higher than
those at 170 mg, respectively (Trial 1002). The PK parameters for total drug-related concentrations (tradipitant
and its metabolites M2, M3, M4, and M8) are not available.
In healthy subjects, following a single dose of 85 mg and 170 mg under fasted condition, tradipitant Cmax
increased less than dose proportionally (1.3-fold), and AUCinf increased approximately dose-proportionally
(1.9-fold) (Trial 1002).
The Applicant has not provided the accumulation ratio following repeated dosing in subjects with a history of
motion sickness.
Time to reach steady state was not determined in the intended population.
The proposed TBM formulation was used in phase 2 and phase 3 trials conducted in subjects with history of
motion sickness (Trials 3401 and 3404). No formulation bridging is needed.

NEREUS (tradipitant)
Characteristic
Mass balance results

Drug Information
Elimination
In the mass balance study, following a single 25 mg (100 μCi) oral dose administration of 14C-radiolabelled
tradipitant, about 88% of the administered radioactivity was recovered mainly as metabolites over 312 hours
postdose, in which 80% and 7% was in feces and urine, respectively. The administered radioactivity was
mostly (85%) recovered within 168 hours postdose.
In feces, tradipitant and 10 metabolites were detected, in which unchanged tradipitant was minimal and 4
metabolites (M17, M35, M58, and M59) were the major components, accounting for 9%, 9%, 9%, and 6% of
the dose, respectively. In urine, tradipitant was not detected and 10 metabolites were detected with M60 as
the major urinary metabolite, accounting for <2% of the dose (Trial H8R-MC-HJAK).

Clearance
Half-life

Metabolic pathway(s)

Primary excretion pathways (%
dose)

In plasma, within 24 hours postdose, tradipitant, M2, M3, M4, and M8 were the main circulating entities
identified. At 4 hours postdose, tradipitant, M2, M3, M4, and M8 accounted for 24%, 11%, 13%, 16%, and
13% of the radioactivity in the plasma sample, respectively. Based on AUC0-inf, tradipitant, M2, M3, and M4
each accounted for approximately 9%, 4%, 5%, and 8% of radioactivity in plasma, respectively while AUC 0-inf
for M8 was not assessed in the mass balance study. The mean t1/2 of total radioactivity was 113 hours
compared to mean t1/2 of 20 to 30 hours of tradipitant, M2, M3, or M4, indicating that additional metabolites
with longer t1/2 may be circulating in plasma. The clinical relevance of unaccounted radioactivity in human
plasma is unknown.
The apparent oral clearance of tradipitant was 41.7 L/hr in healthy subjects (Trial 1102).
In healthy subjects, following a single dose of 85-mg tradipitant under fasted condition, the mean elimination
half-life of tradipitant was 31.6 hours, and the mean elimination half-lives of M2, M3, M4, and M8 were 43.7,
30.2, 32.4, and 36.3 hours, respectively (Trial 1002).
Tradipitant is extensively metabolized; however, the metabolism pathway and the contribution of a specific
metabolizing enzyme to tradipitant elimination was not fully characterized. Based on in vitro studies, the
formation of M3 is mainly by CYP3A4, CYP2C19, and to a lesser extent CYP2C8 while the formation of M2
appears to be by non-CYP450-mediated processes (unspecified). Tradipitant is also glucuronidated by
UGT1A4 and UGT2B7 to form M8. Tradipitant and metabolites M2, M3, and M4 only account for 26% of
radioactivity in plasma based on AUC0-inf, and additional metabolites may not have been identified. Refer to
Section 8.2.
Hepatobiliary excretion is the main elimination pathway of tradipitant. In the mass balance study, about 80% of
the radioactivity was recovered in feces, mainly as metabolite forms following oral administration. In Beagle
dogs, after intravenous dosing, 77% of the administered radioactive dose was recovered in feces suggesting
biliary excretion as the primary excretion pathway (Report 008D04).

Intrinsic Factors and Specific Populations
Body weight
The population PK model failed to incorporate body weight as a significant covariate
Age
Thel population PK model failed to incorporate age as a significant covariate
Renal impairment
Renal excretion is not a major elimination pathway for tradipitant and its metabolites. No dedicated renal
impairment study was conducted. Based on population PK analysis, EBE of CL/F were similar in subjects with
mild renal impairment (n=79) and normal renal function (n=366). A 23% lower EBE of CL/F were observed in
subjects with moderate renal impairment (n=11) compared to subjects with normal renal function; however,
the difference is not considered clinically significant. Tradipitant was not studied in subjects with severe renal
impairment.
Hepatic impairment
While hepatobiliary excretion is the main elimination pathway of tradipitant and its metabolites, the effect of
hepatic impairment on tradipitant pharmacokinetics has not been studied and is unknown.
Drug Interaction Liability (Drug as Perpetrator)
Inhibition/induction of metabolism In vitro, tradipitant inhibits and induces CYP3A4. Oral midazolam exposure was increased 16% when
coadministered with tradipitant 85 mg BID. Tradipitant did not inhibit CYP1A2, CYP2B6, CYP2C8, CYP2C9,
CYP2C19, CYP2D6, nor induce CYP1A2, CYP2B6, CYP2C8, CYP2C9, CYP2C19.

6. Efficacy (Evaluation of Benefit)
6.1. Assessment of Dose and Potential
Effectiveness
Dose Rationale for Phase 3 Studies
The doses for the two phase 3 trials were selected initially based on the data obtained from a
phase 1 trial (Trial H8R-EW-HJAD) in healthy subjects, and subsequently, a phase 2 trial
(Trial 2401) in subjects with history of motion sickness.
Trial H8R-EW-HJAD assessed frontal cortex NK-1 receptor occupancy following multiple
ascending doses in healthy subjects using positron emission tomography (PET). In this trial,
healthy subjects received oral once-daily administration of tradipitant for 7 days at escalating
doses (1, 10, 30, and 100 mg), with maximum frontal cortex NK-1 receptor occupancy of 93%
±4% observed 24 hours after the final 100 mg dose (Figure 1). The Applicant estimated that
approximately 85 mg daily would achieve 90% brain NK-1 receptor occupancy at steady state.
However, the clinical relevance of this receptor occupancy assessment in proposed indication is
unclear.
Figure 1. Relationship of Plasma Tradipitant AUCτ,ss (Left Panel) or Ctrough,ss (Right Panel) and %
Receptor Occupancy in the Frontal Cortex

Source: Adapted from Figures HJAD.11.2 and HJAD.11.3 of Study H8R-EW-HJAD CSR
Dose was escalated every 7 days within subjects from 1 mg to 100 mg.
Abbreviations: AUCτ,ss, area under the concentration-time curve for a dosing interval at steady state; CSR, clinical study report;
Ctrough,ss, trough concentration at steady state

Trial 2401, the phase 2 trial, evaluated a single 170-mg dose of tradipitant in subjects with a
history of motion sickness during a 4-hour boat trip. The percentage of subjects who vomited
was lower in the tradipitant arm compared to placebo (17.5% versus 39.7%, respectively).
Dose Response in Phase 3 Trials
The efficacy of tradipitant 85 mg and 170 mg for the prevention of motion-induced vomiting in
adults was evaluated in two randomized, placebo-controlled phase 3 trials (Trial 3401 and Trial
3404). In Trial 3401 (N=365), both tradipitant doses demonstrated similar treatment effect, with
25
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5720419

NDA 220152
NEREUS (tradipitant)
vomiting rates of 18.3% for the 170-mg dose and 19.5% for the 85-mg dose compared to 44.3%
for placebo. In Trial 3404 (N=316), tradipitant 170 mg appeared to demonstrate lower vomiting
rate compared to the 85-mg dose, with vomiting rates of 10.4% and 18.3%, respectively,
compared to 37.7% for placebo.
In Trial 3401, the percentage of subjects who vomited was lower in both the tradipitant 170-mg
arm (18.3%, p<0.0001) and the tradipitant 85-mg arm (19.5%) compared to the placebo arm
(44.3%) (Table 9). However, due to the hierarchical testing procedure, the tradipitant 85 mg
result is considered nominally significant since the preceding secondary endpoint (worst nausea
score for tradipitant 170 mg) did not achieve statistical significance. No apparent dose-response
relationship was observed between the two doses, as both demonstrated similar vomiting rates.
Neither dose demonstrated efficacy for the worst nausea score secondary endpoint, with results
numerically similar across all treatment arms (Table 9). Additionally, neither dose showed
superiority to placebo for MSAQ subscales or Patient Global Impression of Severity
Questionnaire (PGI-S) scores, with the tradipitant 85-mg arm performing nominally worse than
placebo on the sopite-related MSAQ subscale (Table 10).
In Trial 3404, the percentage of subjects who vomited was lower in both the tradipitant 170-mg
arm (10.4%, p<0.0001) and the tradipitant 85-mg arm (18.3%, p=0.0025) compared to the
placebo arm (37.7%) (Table 13). While the 170-mg dose demonstrated a lower vomiting rate
than the 85-mg dose, the applicant did not conduct a formal comparison, and this trend was not
observed in the other trial. Neither dose demonstrated efficacy for nausea-specific endpoints
such as worst or average nausea scores (Table 14), MSAQ subscales (Table 15), or PGI-S scores
(Table 15), with results numerically similar across all treatment arms.
Refer to Section 6.2 and Section 6.3 for more details.

6.2. Clinical Studies/Trials Intended To
Demonstrate Efficacy
The Applicant submitted two randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled phase 3 trials to
support the efficacy of tradipitant: Trial 3401 and Trial 3404. In both trials, the Applicant
reported a statistically significant difference between tradipitant and placebo for the primary
endpoint of percentage of vomiting during the trip in the tradipitant 170-mg arm compared to
placebo as assessed by the vomiting assessment questionnaire.

6.2.1. Trial 3401
6.2.1.1. Design, Trial 3401
Trial 3401 was a multicenter, randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled phase 3 trial to
assess the efficacy of tradipitant for the prevention of vomiting induced by motion. The trial
included subjects with a history of motion sickness and consisted of administration of a single
dose of trial drug approximately 60 minutes prior to a travel assessment (i.e., a boat trip) lasting
120 to 300 minutes. Eligible subjects were randomized in a 1:1:1 ratio to receive tradipitant
85 mg, tradipitant 170 mg, or placebo.
26
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5720419

NDA 220152
NEREUS (tradipitant)
At the screening visit, potential subject eligibility for the trial was assessed based on the Motion
Sickness Eligibility Questionnaire (MSEQ), interview, physical examination, vital signs,
electrocardiogram (ECG), clinical laboratory tests, drug and alcohol screen, and medical history.
Subjects who fulfilled all of the inclusion-exclusion criteria continued into the evaluation phase
of the trial (travel assessment, Visit 2).
For each travel assessment visit, an assessment was made 24 to 48 hours in advance of the visit
based on National Oceanic and Atmospheric Administration sea conditions to determine if the
travel assessment visit would take place. This was determined based on the wave height: if the
wave height was predicted to be above 1 meter, then the travel assessment would proceed. If the
wave height was predicted to be below 1 meter, the travel assessment would be rescheduled and
subjects who were not able to proceed with a rescheduled visit would have the option to
withdraw consent and be documented as having failed the subject screening. During Visit 2,
subjects were randomly assigned to 1 of 3 treatment arms stratified by port: 85-mg tradipitant,
170-mg tradipitant, or placebo. Subjects were assigned to take 85-mg tradipitant, 170-mg
tradipitant, or placebo approximately 60 minutes prior to entering the boat. Throughout the
duration of boat travel, the vomiting assessment (VA) and nausea assessment (NA) would be
completed approximately every 30 minutes. Immediately following completion of the boat
travel, subjects were instructed to complete several questionnaires to assess their travel: the
MSAQ to assess the symptoms associated with motion sickness, and the PGI-S.
6.2.1.2. Eligibility Criteria, Trial 3401
Key eligibility criteria for Trial 3401 included:


History of symptoms consistent with motion sickness



Men or women between 18 to 75 years, inclusive



Body mass index (BMI) of ≥18 and <40 kg/m2



Males, non-fecund females (i.e., surgically sterilized, if procedure was done 6 months before
screening or participant is postmenopausal for at least 2 years), or females of child-bearing
potential using an acceptable method of birth control (i.e., condoms, diaphragm, spermicidal
agents, cervical cap, Copper intrauterine device) for a period of 35 days before the first
dosing, during the study and for one month after the last dose and must have a negative
pregnancy test at the screening visit
6.2.1.3. Statistical Analysis Plan, Trial 3401

Analysis Populations
Intent-to-Treat Population
The intent-to-treat (ITT) population included any subject randomized that received a dose of trial
drug and had at least one post baseline vomiting assessment. The ITT population analyzed
subjects as they were randomized, regardless of which treatment a subject received. The primary
efficacy analyses were performed in the ITT population.

27
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5720419

NDA 220152
NEREUS (tradipitant)
In general, the Agency recommends against defining the primary efficacy analysis population
based on post-randomization outcomes. As post-randomization, such as requiring a postbaseline
assessment, could be related to treatment assignment, the inclusion of subjects in the efficacy
analysis based on post-randomization outcomes could bias efficacy results. Excluding subjects
who did not receive any trial drug is generally reasonable in a double-blind trial, as treatment
administration should be independent of treatment assignment. In Trial 3401, all randomized
subjects who received trial drug had at least one postbaseline vomiting assessment; therefore, the
criterion for inclusion in the ITT population did not impact efficacy conclusions.
Safety Population
The safety population included any subject randomized that received a dose of trial drug. The
safety population was analyzed based on the actual treatment received. Safety summaries were
based on the safety population.
Per-Protocol Population
The per-protocol (PP) population included any subject who was randomized and received the
protocol-required trial drug exposure and required protocol processing. Blinded clinical data
were reviewed prior to data lock to finalize the list of the PP population.
Sample Size
A sample size of 100 subjects per arm (300 in total) was planned to provide around 96% power
to detect a 26% difference in vomiting between tradipitant and placebo assuming 50% of
subjects on placebo would vomit and 24% of subjects on tradipitant would vomit, given a twosided significance level of 0.05.
Efficacy Assessments
Vomiting Assessment
The VA was a 1-item questionnaire to objectively measure the incidence of emesis. Subjects
would indicate whether or not they had vomited.
Nausea Assessment
The NA was a 1-item questionnaire to assess nausea severity as reported by the subjects. The
subjects would rate the worst severity of their nausea on a 5-point scale in which 0 – none, 1 –
mild, 2 – moderate, 3 – severe, and 4 – very severe. Subjects were instructed to select the number
that most represents the severity of their nausea symptoms every 30 minutes.
Motion Sickness Assessment Questionnaire
The MSAQ was a 16-statement scale to assess motion sickness, which could be sub-divided by
affected body systems (gastrointestinal: sick to stomach, queasy, nauseated, vomit; central: faintlike, lightheaded, dizzy, spinning, disoriented; peripheral: sweaty, clammy/cold sweat, hot/warm;
sopite-related: annoyed/irritated, drowsy, tired/fatigued, uneasy). Each response was rated 1 to 9
with 1 representing low severity and 9 representing high severity. The overall motion sickness
score was obtained by calculating the percentage of total points scored: (sum of points from all
28
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5720419

NDA 220152
NEREUS (tradipitant)
items/144) ×100. Subscale scores were obtained by calculating the percentage of points scored
within each factor: (sum of gastrointestinal items/36) ×100; (sum of central items/45)×100; (sum
of peripheral items/27)×100; (sum of sopite-related items/36) × 100.
Patient Global Impression of Severity Questionnaire
The PGI-S is a general assessment tool used by subjects to rate the overall severity of their
disease or condition at a specific point in time. In Trial 3401, the PGI-S assessed motion sickness
severity at the end of the travel. The subjects rated the severity of their motion sickness
symptoms on a 0 to 4 scale in which 0 = none, 1 = mild, 2 = moderate, 3 = severe, and 4 = very
severe.
Efficacy Endpoints
The primary efficacy endpoint was the percentage of subjects with vomiting during the trip in the
tradipitant 170-mg arm as assessed by the VA questionnaire.
The secondary endpoints included:


Worst nausea score in the tradipitant 170-mg arm



Percentage of subjects with vomiting in the tradipitant 85-mg arm



Worst nausea score in the tradipitant 85-mg arm

The secondary efficacy endpoints were tested in a fixed sequence as specified above.
Other secondary efficacy endpoints that were not under multiplicity control for each tradipitant
treatment arm included:


Total score of MSAQ



Subscale scores of MSAQ



PGI-S

Pooling Port
According to the statistical analysis plan (SAP), low enrollment ports were pooled for analysis. If
a port had <3 subjects in any treatment arm or <9 in a total of subjects, the port (herein known as
a small port) was pooled with another small port until the resulting pooled port has reached the
threshold. The pooling process was as the following: the largest small port was pooled with the
smaller port starting with the smallest until a non-small port was formed. If at the end one pooled
port still did not meet the threshold, it would be pooled to the smallest port which has met the
threshold.
Analysis of Efficacy Endpoints
The percentage of vomiting was assessed in the ITT population by the Cochran-Mantel-Haenszel
(CMH) test adjusting for the pooled port. The proportion of subjects vomiting and the p-values
for the CMH test were reported.
The worst nausea was analyzed in the ITT population, using an analysis of covariance
(ANCOVA) model with the main effects of pooled port, peak wave height and treatment arm. A
29
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5720419

NDA 220152
NEREUS (tradipitant)
summary of each endpoint, by treatment arm, was presented together with the least squares (LS)
mean and standard error in each treatment arm, the difference in LS means between treatment
arm and placebo and its 95% CI, and the p-value for the difference in LS means.
Based on the datasets submitted by the Applicant, it appeared that pooled site rather than pooled
port was used as the covariate in both the CMH test and the ANCOVA model. On March 14,
2025, an information request was issued to clarify the rationale for using pooled site instead of
pooled port. In a response dated March 25, 2025, the Applicant stated that each site corresponded
to a unique port; however, the review team identified that both sites 113 and 117 in the dataset
were listed as having the same port, “Port 07 – Fort Myer.” A follow-up information request for
clarification was sent on March 25, 2025, and on April 4, 2025, the Applicant explained that
each site number represented a unique physical location in Trials 3401 and 3404. Specifically,
sites 113 and 117 in Trial 3401 corresponded to Tampa, FL and Fort Myers, FL, respectively. In
the response received on April 4, 2025 (SDN 10), the Applicant stated,
participants’ randomization was stratified by port in this study. Based on the enrollment
plan and timeline, a maximum of ten ports were originally designed in the IRT/IWRS
system. However, due to enrollment difficulties, more than ten ports were explored to be
open during the study. Adding more ports to the IRT/IWRS system would take a couple
of weeks. To avoid delaying some scheduled boat trips, we assigned sites 113 and 117 to
the same port in the IRT/IWRS system. The ADSL dataset directly used this information
from the unblinded randomization list.

The review team conducted sensitivity analyses using the prespecified port pooling algorithm
(based on the listed port in the Subject-Level Analysis Dataset [ADSL]) and found that efficacy
conclusions were not impacted.13 Therefore, the choice of pooling by port versus site, as reflected
in the ADSL dataset, does not appear to impact the efficacy results. Unless specified otherwise,
all analyses in the review adjusting for location used pooled site as the covariate, consistent with
the Applicant’s implementation.
The continuous secondary endpoints, the total score and subscale scores of MSAQ, and PGI-S,
were analyzed in the ITT population, using an ANCOVA model with the main effects of pooled
port, wave high and treatment arm. A summary of each endpoint, by treatment arm, was
presented together with the least-squared mean and standard error in each treatment arm, the
difference in LS means between treatment arm and placebo and its 95% CI, and the p-value for
the difference in LS means.
Estimands and Intercurrent Events
The protocol and SAP did not specify an estimand of interest; however, based on the analyses,
the impact of potential intercurrent events appears minimal. Treatment discontinuation was not
applicable due to the single-dose design for efficacy assessment. The protocol listed medications
prohibited from 1 week before randomization until the end of the treatment period. On
March 14, 2025, an information request was issued asking for a list of subjects who used
prohibited medications between 1 week prior to randomization and the end of treatment period.
On March 25, 2025, the Applicant responded, indicating only three subjects used prohibited
13

Data not shown.

30
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5720419

NDA 220152
NEREUS (tradipitant)
medications during this period, which the review team confirmed. A sensitivity analysis was
performed using nonresponder imputation for subjects who used prohibited medication.14 These
subjects were considered to have experienced vomiting during the travel for this analysis. The
results of the sensitivity analysis were consistent with the primary analysis, indicating that
intercurrent events appear to have minimal impact on the efficacy conclusions.
Handling of Missing Data
No imputation was performed for primary or secondary endpoints. In the Applicant’s primary
analysis, vomiting assessments with missing values were excluded from the calculation of the
primary endpoint. Refer to Section 6.2.1.4 for a discussion of the amount of missing data and its
impact.
6.2.1.4. Results of Analyses, Trial 3401
Demographic characteristics are summarized by treatment arm in Table 7 for Trial 3401, within
the ITT population. Overall, the baseline demographic characteristics were balanced between
treatment arms and were representative of the population anticipated in motion sickness. Most
subjects were White (85%), female (67%), and not Hispanic or Latino (84%). The mean age was
approximately 49 years with a range from 19 to 75 years, and the majority (86%) of subjects
were younger than 65 years of age.

A summary of the analysis populations for Trial 3401 is presented in Table 8. Of the 366
randomized subjects, only one subject, although randomized to tradipitant 170-mg arm, did not
receive the trial drug, and was excluded from the ITT population. No subjects received the
incorrect trial drug according to their assigned randomization arm and all subjects who took trial
drug had a postbaseline vomiting assessment. Therefore, the ITT population and the safety
population were consistent with no discrepancies.

For subject disposition in Trial 3401, only one subject in the tradipitant 170-mg arm
discontinued the trial due to withdrawal of consent. The data indicated that this subject withdrew
from the trial after 60 minutes of the boat trip. There was no dropout in the other arms.
Treatment discontinuation was not applicable to the ITT population as the ITT population
included subjects who received the treatment, and the trial only had a single dose for efficacy.
The Applicant’s primary efficacy results were confirmed by the statistical review team, and the
results demonstrate superiority of the tradipitant 170-mg dose compared to placebo in percentage
of subjects with vomiting during the boat travel. The percentages of subjects with vomiting
during the travel were 18.3%, 19.5%, and 44.3% in the tradipitant 170 mg, tradipitant 85 mg, and
placebo arms, respectively, as shown in Table 9, which also presents the results for the secondary
endpoint of the worst nausea score during travel.

Per the multiple testing hierarchy, the comparison of tradipitant 85 mg versus placebo for the
percentage of subjects with vomiting was to be conducted only after the comparison of
tradipitant 170 mg versus placebo on worst nausea score. Since the testing did not demonstrate
statistical significance for the latter comparison, the result for tradipitant 85 mg on the vomiting
endpoint is considered nominally significant.
For the primary endpoint, the SAP did not specify which summary statistics would be reported.
In the clinical study report, the Applicant presented the unadjusted treatment difference along
with its 95% CI. However, since sites were used as a stratification factor at randomization, and
the p-value was based on the CMH test, the review team also examined the adjusted treatment
difference that accounts for the stratification factor. The unadjusted treatment differences
between each tradipitant arm and placebo were consistent with the adjusted treatment
differences, indicating the use of pooled site in the primary analysis did not have an impact on
efficacy conclusions.
The proportion of subjects with missing vomiting assessments was balanced across treatment
arms. A sensitivity analysis considering subjects with missing assessments as treatment failures
(i.e., as having experienced vomiting) resulted in only two subjects in the tradipitant 170-mg arm
and one subject in the placebo arm changing their status from no vomiting to vomiting. This
small amount of missing data did not impact the efficacy conclusion.15
For the secondary endpoint of worst nausea score during the boat travel, neither the tradipitant
170-mg nor the 85-mg arm demonstrated a statistically significant difference compared to
placebo and the average of the worst nausea score was similar across arms, indicating

15

Data not shown.

Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5720419

NDA 220152
NEREUS (tradipitant)
insufficient evidence to support the effectiveness of tradipitant in reducing nausea for this patient
population.
The results for other secondary endpoints by treatment arm are summarized in Table 10. Neither
the tradipitant 170-mg arm nor the tradipitant 85-mg arm demonstrated nominal superiority to
placebo for any of the endpoints listed in Table 10. Some endpoints were numerically worse in
the tradipitant arms compared to placebo. In fact, for the “sopite-related” score of the MSAQ, the
tradipitant 85 mg performed nominally worse than placebo (p-value=0.0155). This indicates that
despite the reduction in vomiting observed with tradipitant, there is no evidence of prevention of
symptoms other than vomiting, and tradipitant may even worsen some motion-sickness
symptoms (i.e., sopite-related symptoms showed a nominally significant difference in favor of
placebo compared to tradipitant 85-mg dose). Furthermore, for the PGI-S, the results were nearly
numerically identical across treatment arms, indicating that when subjects were asked to rate
their overall motion sickness severity, they provided similar ratings across different treatment
arms.

NEREUS (tradipitant)

6.2.2. Trial 3404
6.2.2.1. Design, Trial 3404
Trial 3404 was a multicenter, randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled phase 3 trial to
assess the efficacy of tradipitant in the prevention of vomiting induced by motion. The trial
included subjects with a history of motion sickness and consisted of administration of a single
dose of trial drug approximately 60 minutes prior to a travel assessment (i.e., a boat trip) lasting
120 to 300 minutes. Eligible subjects were randomized in a 1:1:1 ratio to receive tradipitant
170 mg, tradipitant 85 mg, or placebo. Subjects received an offer to participate in an optional PK
assessment at the end-of-trial visit. Trial 3404 used the same trial design as Trial 3401. Refer to
Section 6.2.1.1 for details.
6.2.2.2. Eligibility Criteria, Trial 3404
The key eligibility criteria for Trial 3404 were the same as those for Trial 3401. Refer to
Section 6.2.1.2 for details.
6.2.2.3. Statistical Analysis Plan, Trial 3404
Analysis Populations
The analysis populations and corresponding definitions for Trial 3404 were the same as those
described for Trial 3401. Refer to Section 6.2.1.3.
Sample Size
The sample size determination was the same in Trial 3404 as described for Trial 3401. Refer to
Section 6.2.1.3.
Efficacy Assessments
Vomiting Assessment
The VA was the same in Trial 3404 as described for Trial 3401. Refer to Section 6.2.1.3.
Nausea Assessment
The NA was the same in Trial 3404 as described for Trial 3401. Refer to Section 6.2.1.3.
Motion Sickness Assessment Questionnaire
The MSAQ was the same in Trial 3404 as described for Trial 3401. Refer to Section 6.2.1.3.
Patient Global Impression of Severity Questionnaire
The PGI-S was the same in Trial 3404 as described for Trial 3401. Refer to Section 6.2.1.3.

35
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5720419

NDA 220152
NEREUS (tradipitant)
Efficacy Endpoints
The primary efficacy endpoint was the percentage of subjects with vomiting during the trip in
tradipitant 170-mg arm as assessed by the VA questionnaire.
The secondary endpoints included:


Percentage of subjects with vomiting in the tradipitant 85-mg arm



Percentage of subjects with vomiting and severe nausea defined as worst nausea ≥3 in each
tradipitant treatment arms

The secondary efficacy endpoints were tested in a fixed sequence as specified above. However,
for the percentage of subjects with vomiting and severe nausea, it was not explicitly specified
whether the tradipitant 170-mg arm was tested prior to the 85-mg arm.
Other secondary efficacy endpoints that were not under multiplicity control for each tradipitant
treatment arm included:


Worst nausea score



Average nausea score



Total score of MSAQ



Subscale scores of MSAQ



PGI-S

Pooling Port
Subjects were enrolled from five ports. The algorithm for pooling port was the same in
Trial 3404 as described for Trial 3401. Refer to Section 6.2.1.3.
Analysis of Efficacy Endpoints
The percentage of vomiting was assessed in the ITT population by the CMH test adjusting for
the pooling port. The proportion of subjects with vomiting and the p-values for the CMH test
were reported. The secondary endpoints were assessed in the same way as the primary endpoint.
Based on the datasets submitted by the Applicant, it appeared that pooled site rather than pooled
port was used as the covariate in both the CMH test and the ANCOVA model. However, since
each site in Trial 3404 corresponded to a unique port, pooling by site or port yields the same
result.
The continuous secondary endpoints, including worst nausea score, average nausea score, the
total score and subscale scores of MSAQ, and PGI-S, were analyzed in the ITT population, using
an ANCOVA model with the main effects of pooled port, wave height and treatment arm. A
summary of each endpoint, by treatment arm, was presented together with the least-squared
mean and standard error in each treatment arm, the difference in LS means between treatment
arm and placebo and its 95% CI, and the p-value for the difference in LS means.

36
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5720419

NDA 220152
NEREUS (tradipitant)
Estimands and Intercurrent Events
The protocol and SAP did not specify an estimand of interest; however, based on the analyses,
the impact of potential intercurrent events appears minimal. Treatment discontinuation was not
applicable due to the single-dose design for efficacy assessment. The protocol listed medications
prohibited from 1 week before randomization until the end of the treatment period. On
March 14, 2025, an information request was issued asking for a list of subjects who used
prohibited medications between 1 week prior to randomization and the end of treatment period.
On March 25, 2025, the Applicant responded, indicating no subjects used prohibited medications
during this period, which the review team confirmed.
Handling of Missing Data
No imputation was performed for primary or key secondary endpoints. Refer to Section 6.2.2.4
for a discussion of the amount of missing data and its impact.
6.2.2.4. Results of Analyses, Trial 3404
Demographic characteristics are summarized by treatment arm in Table 11 for Trial 3404.
Overall, the baseline demographic characteristics were balanced across treatment arms and
considered representative of the motion sickness population. The mean age was 46 years with a
range from 18 to 75 years, and the majority (88%) of subjects were younger than 65 years of age.
Most subjects were White (78%), female (56%), and not Hispanic or Latino (81%).

Regarding subject disposition for Trial 3404, only one subject in the tradipitant 170-mg arm
discontinued the trial due to loss to follow-up. This subject completed all vomiting and nausea
assessments during the boat trip. The loss to follow-up occurred after travel had ended, and thus
did not affect the efficacy evaluation for this subject.
The review team verified the Applicant’s primary efficacy results, which demonstrated that both
tradipitant 170-mg and 85-mg doses were superior to placebo in reducing the percentage of
subjects who experienced vomiting during the travel event, as presented in Table 13. The
observed percentages of subjects with vomiting were 10.4% for tradipitant 170 mg, 18.3% for
tradipitant 85 mg, and 37.7% for placebo.

The SAP did not specify the summary statistics to be reported for the binary endpoints. In the
clinical study report, the Applicant presented unadjusted treatment differences with
corresponding 95% CIs. However, because ports were used as the stratification factor at
randomization and the p-values were derived from a CMH test, the review team also examined
the adjusted treatment differences. The adjusted and unadjusted treatment differences between
each tradipitant arm and placebo were consistent, indicating that the inclusion of pooled site/port
in the primary efficacy analysis did not have an impact of efficacy conclusions.
The number of subjects with missing vomiting assessments was balanced across treatment arms,
with two in the tradipitant 85-mg arm, one in the placebo arm, and none in the tradipitant 170mg arm. Given the minimal amount of missing data, there was no impact on the overall efficacy
conclusions.
For the endpoint assessing the percentage of subjects with vomiting and severe nausea, both
tradipitant arms demonstrated a statistically significant difference compared to placebo.
However, the treatment difference for this endpoint was driven by vomiting, and there was no
distinct difference for nausea. Specifically, the percentage of subjects with a worst nausea score
≥3 was 52.9%, 35.8%, and 45.3% for tradipitant 85 mg, tradipitant 170 mg, and placebo,
respectively, which was similar across treatment arms. None of the unadjusted risk differences
for the percentage of subjects with worst nausea score ≥3 compared to placebo achieved nominal
significance. Thus, the significance observed in the multi-component endpoint was driven by the
vomiting component. Therefore, this endpoint analysis does not provide additional information
to characterize the effect of treatment beyond the information provided by the primary endpoint
analysis. See Table 14 for a summary of the secondary endpoints related to nausea assessments.
Table 14 presents the results for secondary endpoints related to nausea assessments, which were
not subject to multiplicity control. Neither the tradipitant 170-mg nor the tradipitant 85-mg
treatment arms demonstrated a nominally statistically significant difference from placebo for
either the worst nausea score or the average nausea score. In fact, the endpoint results were

6.3. Key Efficacy Review Issues
6.3.1. Benefit-Risk Assessment of Tradipitant 85-mg
and 170-mg Doses
Issue
The primary sources of efficacy data submitted were obtained from two phase 3 randomized,
placebo-controlled clinical trials, Trials 3401 and 3404, conducted in subjects with a history of
motion sickness. Due to the lack of statistical significance for a preceding secondary endpoint in
the testing hierarchy (i.e., worst nausea score in the tradipitant 170-mg arm), Trial 3401 did not
demonstrate statistical significance for the percentage of subjects with vomiting in the tradipitant
85-mg arm compared to placebo under the prespecified fixed-sequence multiple testing
procedure for secondary endpoints. However, the tradipitant 85-mg arm achieved nominal
statistical significance for the prespecified analysis of the percentage of subjects with vomiting in
Trial 3401 and the nominal p-value was well below the 0.05 threshold used to evaluate statistical
significance in the trial (i.e., p<0.0001). Additionally, the results were robust against missing
42
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5720419

NDA 220152
NEREUS (tradipitant)
data and intercurrent events, demonstrating the consistency of the treatment effect across
different statistical analyses. Trial 3404 provided confirmation that the tradipitant 85-mg dose
was effective in reducing the percentage of subjects who vomited compared to placebo by
demonstrating statistical significance on the prespecified analysis.
The overall evidence supports the efficacy of both the tradipitant 85-mg and 170-mg arms on
vomiting. While the tradipitant 170-mg arm had a numerically lower percentage of subjects
experiencing vomiting during travel, the overall efficacy appears similar between the two doses.
Further exploratory analyses were performed to evaluate the benefit-risk profile of both doses.
Background
During the pre-NDA meeting held on November 8, 2023, the Applicant asked whether the
Agency would consider Trials 2401 and 3401 sufficient to support NDA filing and review of
tradipitant for the prevention of vomiting induced by motion, while Trial 3404 was still ongoing.
The clinical study report and statistical analysis plan were submitted after the meeting request.
The Agency expressed concern that in Trial 3401, the worst nausea score in the tradipitant 170mg arm was not statistically significantly different from placebo. As a result, per the fixedsequence testing procedure, subsequent hypotheses including the percentage of vomiting in the
tradipitant 85-mg arm were not controlled for type I error. Additionally, since Trial 2401 only
evaluated the 170-mg dose, data supporting the 85-mg dose appeared insufficient without results
from Trial 3404. When the Applicant asked whether the results from the analyses of the
tradipitant 85-mg dose arm on the percentage of vomiting would not be considered by the
Agency. The Agency indicated that any efficacy data submitted as part of a future NDA would
be considered but that the type I error control was an important aspect of the assessment of
efficacy.
In the NDA submission, the primary sources of efficacy data were from two phase 3 trials,
Trials 3401 and 3404. Although the percentage of vomiting in the tradipitant 85-mg arm reached
only nominal statistical significance in Trial 3401, it achieved statistical significance in Trial
3404. The efficacy results were robust with respect to the handling of missing data and
intercurrent events, demonstrating SEE for the tradipitant 85-mg dose. Given both treatment
arms demonstrated SEE and there appeared to be some dose-related safety outcomes, the review
team conducted additional analysis to assess the benefit-risk profile of the two dose arms to
determine whether there was sufficient evidence to recommend the use of one dose over the
other.
Assessment
To further evaluate the benefit-risk profile between the two doses, the review team conducted an
exploratory responder analysis evaluating the percentage of subjects with vomiting during travel
or one of the following treatment-emergent adverse events (TEAEs): headache, somnolence, or
fatigue. This responder analysis was conducted for both Trials 3401 and 3404, as well as a
pooled analysis of both trials. This analysis was designed to assess both efficacy and safety
endpoints simultaneously to better characterize the overall benefit-risk profile of each dose. The
safety signals included in the responder analysis were TEAEs with a frequency ≥5% that
occurred with greater frequency in the tradipitant arms compared to placebo. Therefore, the
selected TEAEs are likely related to tradipitant. Furthermore, these TEAEs represent unfavorable
treatment outcomes for subjects, and subjects who experienced these TEAEs would likely have
43

NDA 220152
NEREUS (tradipitant)
experienced limited, if any, benefit from treatment. The results of the responder analysis are
shown in Table 18.

Results from the pooled analysis of Trials 3401 and 3404 showed that 35.7% of subjects in the
85-mg arm experienced vomiting or one of the specified TEAEs compared to 37.2% in the 170mg arm and 49.1% in the placebo arm. Overall, the response rates for this analysis were
numerically better, though similar, for the tradipitant 85-mg arm compared to the tradipitant 170mg arm. While the percentage of subjects who vomited was slightly lower for the tradipitant
170-mg arm compared to the 85-mg arm, this responder analysis suggests that the 85-mg dose
may have a slightly more favorable benefit-risk profile after accounting for TEAEs that appear to
be related to tradipitant. It should be noted that these data exclusively represent the effects of a
single administration of tradipitant and there are no clinical trials with a treatment duration of
more than 90 doses. Furthermore, there are no randomized controlled data to support repeated
doses of tradipitant for this indication, and there are substantial limitations to the data provided to
support the safety of repeated use of both proposed doses (see Section 7.6.2).
Conclusion
While the percentage of vomiting in the tradipitant 85-mg arm reached only nominal statistical
significance in Trial 3401, it achieved statistical significance in Trial 3404. The consistently
lower percentage of vomiting observed in analyses for Trials 3401 and 3404 supports that the
superiority of tradipitant 85 mg over placebo is robust and persuasive. The tradipitant 170-mg
arm had a slightly lower percentage of subjects who vomited compared to the 85-mg arm;
however, a responder analysis evaluating the percentage of subjects who either vomited or
experienced the TEAEs of headache, somnolence, or fatigue numerically favored the 85-mg
dose, though the results were generally similar for the two arms. Despite some AEs occurring at
a higher rate in the 170-mg arm, there was a numerically lower rate of vomiting in the 170-mg
44
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5720419

NDA 220152
NEREUS (tradipitant)
arm and some patients may benefit from the higher dose. The review team recommends the
approval of both doses given the data support a favorable risk-benefit profile for both doses. It
should be noted that the results of this analysis were based on TEAEs from a single dose. The
safety profile of repeated intermittent dosing over a lifetime has not been established. It is
possible there are additional unknown safety risks from unrestricted, repeated dosing over a
lifetime that will occur more frequently when using the 170-mg dose compared to the 85-mg
dose. Therefore, the review team recommends use of the lowest effective dose (i.e., the
tradipitant 85-mg dose).

6.3.2. Impact of Variable Travel Duration on Efficacy
Issue
As outlined in the protocols for Trials 3401 and 3404, the expected duration of the boat travel
ranged from approximately 120 to 300 minutes. This wide variability in travel time could affect
the efficacy assessment if, for example, a longer travel event increases the likelihood of
vomiting. Imbalanced travel duration across treatment arms could impact the symptoms and
severity of motion sickness experienced by subjects, potentially complicating the results.
Background
According to the protocols for Trials 3401 and 3404, travel durations ranged from 120 to 300
minutes. Vomiting was assessed every 30 minutes during the trip to evaluate the percentage of
subjects with vomiting. Longer exposure to motion could increase the chance of vomiting. There
may also be unidentified potential confounders associated with a longer travel duration (e.g., a
travel event takes longer due to rough seas). Although site was the randomization stratification
factor for both trials, boat trips from the same site could still differ in duration. As a result,
potential imbalances in travel time across treatment arms may complicate the interpretation of
the percentage of subjects with vomiting. The review team conducted analyses to assess the
potential impact of variable travel duration on efficacy results.
Assessment
Based on the datasets submitted by the Applicant, the actual travel durations ranged from 125 to
265 minutes in Trial 3401 and from 123 to 245 minutes in Trial 3404. Kaplan-Meier plots of
travel duration by treatment arms for Trials 3401 and 3404 are presented in Figure 2 and Figure
3, respectively. Around 85% and 90% of subjects in each treatment arm had a travel duration of
at least 180 minutes in Trials 3401 and 3404, respectively. The majority of subjects had a travel
duration between 180 minutes and 240 minutes. Overall, travel durations were comparable
across treatment arms in both trials.

The review team conducted supplementary analyses where the endpoint was defined as the
percentage of subjects with vomiting by specific timepoints during the travel, ranging from 30 to
270 minutes in 30-minute increments. The results are presented in Table 19 and Table 20 for
Trials 3401 and 3404, respectively. The percentages at 210, 240, and 270 minutes were identical
within each treatment arm for both trials. The treatment differences began to stabilize when
censoring the data at 120 minutes for Trial 3401 and at 180 minutes for Trial 3404.

Conclusion
The distribution of travel duration was balanced across treatment arms in both Trials 3401 and
3404, supporting that the observed superiority of tradipitant over placebo in prevention of
vomiting was not driven by differences in travel duration. Additionally, the supplementary
analyses censoring data at specific timepoints suggest that the impact of variable travel duration
on efficacy was minimal.
