7. Safety (Risk and Risk Management)
7.1. Potential Risks or Safety Concerns Based on
Nonclinical Data
7.1.1. Overall Risks and Safety Concerns
Notable risks and safety concerns based on completed nonclinical studies include the following:


The submitted nonclinical information is insufficient to adequately characterize the safety
profile of tradipitant for unrestricted use over a lifetime.
­ The Applicant has not conducted a chronic (e.g., ≥6 months in duration) toxicity study in
a nonrodent species.
­ Although the Applicant conducted additional in vitro nonclinical tests intended to support
the safety of tradipitant, this information does not substantially contribute to assessing the
safety of unrestricted use over a lifetime (see Section 13.2.5).
­ As such, the safety of unrestricted use of tradipitant over a lifetime has not been
adequately addressed by nonclinical data (see further discussion in Section 7.7.1).



A risk of hepatoxicity was identified in a subchronic toxicity study16 in Han Wistar rats (see
Section 13.1.5) and was further explored in short-duration in vitro safety pharmacology
studies with tradipitant and its major human metabolites (see Section 13.2.3). Based on the
available nonclinical data, a risk for liver injury with tradipitant and its major metabolites has
been identified. This safety concern was not identified in clinical trials with tradipitant up to
90 doses.

Completed nonclinical studies with tradipitant include an in vitro human ether-a-go-go-related
gene (hERG) assay, an in vitro study in dog Purkinje fibers, four in vitro safety pharmacology
studies using cultured 3D human hepatocyte spheroids, one in vitro safety pharmacology study
using a human liver co-culture microphysiological system (MPS), and single-dose in vivo
cardiovascular (dog), respiratory (rat), and central nervous system (mouse) safety pharmacology
studies; repeat-dose toxicity studies in mice (13 weeks), Fisher rats (1, 3, and 6 months), Han
Wistar rats (3 months), and dogs (1 and 3 months); a complete battery of genetic toxicity studies
including an Ames assay, an in vitro chromosomal aberration assay in human lymphocytes, and
an in vivo micronucleus assay in mice; a complete battery of reproductive toxicity studies
including a combined fertility, early embryonic development, and embryo-fetal development
study in rats, an embryo-fetal development study in rabbits, and a pre- and postnatal
development study in rats; a juvenile toxicity study in Han Wistar rats; carcinogenicity studies of
26 weeks in transgenic mice and 104 weeks in Han Wistar rats; two special toxicology studies
16

Although a 13-week study is not generally considered pivotal to support the safety of a drug for chronic or
repeated intermittent use, the 13-week Han Wistar rat study is considered pivotal in this NDA. This study
demonstrated new and adverse toxicities with tradipitant administration that were not observed in the 26-week
Fisher rat study. In alignment with the principles of 3R’s to reduce animal use when possible, an additional 26-week
study in Han Wistar rats was not requested. Therefore, both rat studies are considered integral in determining the
safety of tradipitant for the prevention of vomiting induced by motion.

48
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5720419

NDA 220152
NEREUS (tradipitant)
using a four-organ MPS comprised of human liver, cardiac, renal, and neuronal cells; and an
assessment of phototoxic potential. As previously stated, a chronic toxicity study in a nonrodent
species has not been conducted.

7.2. Potential Risks or Safety Concerns Based on
Drug Class or Other Drug-Specific Factors
Tradipitant is a substance P/NK-1 receptor antagonist. Other substance P/NK-1 receptor
antagonists have been approved, including aprepitant (oral), fosaprepitant (intravenous [IV]), and
rolapitant (oral or IV). Some substance P/NK-1 receptor antagonists have been approved as
fixed-combination products, such as netupitant and palonosetron (oral) and fosnetupitant and
palonosetron (IV). The approved indications for these substance P/NK-1 receptor antagonists are
prevention of chemotherapy-induced nausea and vomiting (CINV) and postoperative nausea and
vomiting (PONV). These substance P/NK-1 receptor antagonists are approved for patients who
are either undergoing chemotherapy treatment for an oncologic diagnosis or undergoing a
surgical procedure. The currently approved substance P/NK-1 receptor antagonists also have a
limited administration schedule with at least some of the exposure occurring in a healthcare
setting. For example, the recommended dosage regimen for the fixed-dose combination
aprepitant/palonosetron is a 3-day course of oral capsules prior to highly emetogenic
chemotherapy or moderately emetogenic chemotherapy. The clinical trials that supported the use
of the currently approved substance P/NK-1 receptor antagonists were in alignment with the
intended use of the products. Additionally, marketing applications for approved substance P/NK1 receptor antagonists have included data from 6-month rodent and 9-month nonrodent toxicity
studies.
Due to differences in the intended use population between tradipitant and the approved substance
P/NK-1 receptor antagonists, the known safety concerns for the drug class may not be directly
relevant to tradipitant, and the benefit-risk assessment for tradipitant differs from other drugs in
its class. As described above, the approved in-class medications are indicated for specific
populations undergoing treatments (i.e., chemotherapy and surgery) for underlying and often
life-threatening disease. This contrasts with the proposed population with a history of motion
sickness, a non-life-threatening physiologic response to stimuli that is self-limited and often
avoidable. The proposed population includes generally healthy individuals, which may lower the
acceptability of risk compared to other in-class drugs. In addition, due to the physiologic nature
of motion sickness, the proposed population may experience motion sickness in response to a
variety of stimuli (e.g., sea travel, car travel, air travel), under no healthcare supervision, and
repeatedly over a prolonged period.
The currently approved substance P/NK-1 receptor antagonists have several associated safety
concerns addressed in their approved labels. Aprepitant (Cinvanti, NDA 209296 and Emend,
NDAs 021549, 022023, and 207865) labeling includes Warnings and Precautions for
hypersensitivity reactions including anaphylaxis, and drug-drug interactions (DDIs) due to
aprepitant’s properties as a substrate, weak-to-moderate inhibitor, and inducer of cytochrome
P450 (CYP)3A4. Rolapitant (Varubi, NDA 206500 and 208399) labeling includes Warnings and
Precautions for DDIs due to rolapitant’s properties as a moderate inhibitor of CYP2D6. These
medications’ labels describe adverse events that occurred in CINV clinical trials including but
not limited to fatigue, dizziness, dehydration, abdominal pain, nausea, diarrhea, dyspepsia,
49
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5720419

NDA 220152
NEREUS (tradipitant)
gastritis, stomatitis, anemia, neutropenia, decreased appetite, hiccups, and urinary tract infection.
Adverse events described in the labeling that occurred in PONV clinical trials include (but are
not limited to) bradycardia, hypotension, and constipation. Some of these adverse events also
occurred in the tradipitant safety database; however, the patient population and intended use of
tradipitant differ from the approved substance P/NK-1 receptor antagonists.
There are potential safety concerns for substance P/NK-1 receptor antagonists that are not
marketed in the United States. Casopitant mesylate (Rezonic, NDA 022336) is a drug with this
mechanism of action for which a marketing authorization application was filed with FDA in May
2008, that received a complete response in June 2009, due to safety concerns and was
subsequently withdrawn by the Applicant. A similar marketing authorization application was
filed with the European Medicines Agency in 2008, and it was withdrawn by the Applicant in
2009. Repeat-dose toxicity studies in rats (6 months) and in dogs (9 months) showed evidence of
cardiomyopathy. These changes occurred with increased levels of biomarkers for cardiomyocyte
injury (i.e., troponin I and creatine kinase muscle and brain mass isoenzyme). There were also
ultrastructural changes in the hearts of both preclinical species detected on transmission electron
microscopy, suggestive of accumulation of phospholipids (Pagliarusco et al. 2011). In shorterduration nonclinical studies, these findings were not observed. These long-term nonclinical
studies of casopitant were important in identifying safety concerns that differed from other drugs
of the same class and highlight the importance of obtaining appropriate and complete toxicology
data for each unique drug candidate.17

7.3. Potential Risks or Safety Concerns Identified
Through Postmarket Experience
Not applicable. Tradipitant is not commercially available in any country.

7.4. FDA Approach to the Safety Review
The clinical safety review primarily focused on data from the two randomized, double-blind,
placebo-controlled phase 3 trials, Trial 3401 and Trial 3404, and the randomized, double-blind,
placebo-controlled phase 2 trial, Trial 2401. All three trials were conducted in subjects who had
a history of motion sickness who were otherwise healthy.18 In each trial, subjects received a
single dose of tradipitant approximately 60 minutes prior to a vehicle travel assessment
consisting of a boat trip lasting 123 to 265 minutes, during which safety data were collected. In
Trials 3401 and 3404, subjects were randomized to receive a single dose of either tradipitant
85 mg, tradipitant 170 mg, or placebo before the travel event. In Trial 2401, subjects were
randomized to receive a single dose of either tradipitant 170 mg or placebo before the travel

17

See Remand Response from Dr. Julie G. Beitz, FDA, to Mr. Birznieks, Vanda Pharmaceuticals, Inc. (April 26,
2019). Also see Memorandum to File from Dr. David Joseph, FDA, under NDA 022336 (June 15, 2009).
18
Trials 2401, 3401, and 3404 included the following exclusion criterion: “current clinically significant
cardiovascular, respiratory, neurologic, hepatic, hematopoietic, renal, gastrointestinal or metabolic dysfunction
unless currently controlled and stable.”

50
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5720419

NDA 220152
NEREUS (tradipitant)
event. The review team determined that pooling of safety data from Trials 2401, 3401, and 3404
was appropriate as the patient population and trial design for each trial were sufficiently similar.
The Applicant also submitted an interim report and 120 Day Safety Update for Trial 3403, which
was an ongoing open-label trial wherein subjects received either tradipitant 85 mg or 170 mg
approximately 60 to 120 minutes prior to a travel event anticipated to induce motion sickness19
for up to 90 dosing days over the course of 12 months. Trial 3403 was the only source of clinical
safety data for repeat dosing of tradipitant in the intended use population. The Division had
previously recommended that the Applicant include a concurrent comparator arm to increase the
likelihood of Trial 3403 resulting in interpretable data; however, the Applicant did not alter the
trial design.
The Applicant’s safety database also included all trials in which tradipitant was administered,
including trials with indications other than vomiting induced by motion (i.e., gastroparesis, social
anxiety disorder, irritable bowel syndrome, alcohol use disorder, atopic dermatitis). The
generalizability of these data is limited due to differences in pathophysiology, chronicity, and
associated symptoms of these conditions as compared to the intended use population (i.e.,
vomiting induced by motion).
The Applicant defined TEAEs as adverse events that were “newly occurring or worsening from
treatment dosing until 3 days after the final dose of study medication.” Tradipitant has a reported
half-life of approximately 34 hours (2 half-lives are approximately 3 days, and 5 half-lives are
approximately 7 days). Therefore, within the Applicant’s selected 3-day timeframe, it is
anticipated that 25% of tradipitant is expected to remain in the body. The review team considered
TEAE definitions that included a duration of up to 7 days after the last dose (i.e., 5 half-lives),
but these definitions did not meaningfully change the safety analyses. Therefore, the safety
analyses described in this review use the TEAE definition described in the trial protocols.

7.5. Adequacy of the Clinical Safety Database
The Applicant provided safety data from a total of 516 subjects (including 227 who received
85 mg and 289 who received 170 mg) with a history of motion sickness who were exposed to a
single dose of tradipitant in Trials 2401, 3401, and 3404. In addition, in their original
submission, the Applicant provided interim safety data for 382 subjects (including 199 who
received 85 mg and 183 who received 170 mg) with a history of motion sickness who received
repeated dosing of tradipitant in the ongoing Trial 3403. Additional safety data from Trial 3403
were provided for 143 subjects (525 subjects total) in the 120-Day Safety Update report. Table
21 shows the total number of subjects exposed to each dose of tradipitant in trials for the
proposed intended use.

Travel events anticipated to induce motion sickness were defined as “any vehicle travel (car, plane, train, boat,
etc.) historically known to induce motion sickness in the participant.”

The submitted clinical safety database in the intended use population along with the available
nonclinical data support the safety of tradipitant for up to 90 doses. However, the available safety
data are inadequate to support the safety of unrestricted use. The single-dose clinical trials (i.e.,
Trials 2401, 3401, 3404) support the safety of a single dose of tradipitant, while the multipledose trial (i.e., Trial 3403) provides additional safety data with several limitations that limit the
interpretability of the results (e.g., limited to 90 doses, infrequent safety assessments). These
limitations of the data from Trial 3403 are discussed in detail in Section 7.6.2.
The Applicant also submitted data from clinical trials of tradipitant in other conditions (e.g.,
gastroparesis). Notably, no clinical trials have had a duration of more than 12 weeks (or 90
dosing days). Additionally, in many of the clinical trials, the dosage of tradipitant differed from
the dosages assessed for vomiting induced by motion and there may be differences in diseasespecific risks for these other indications. Thus, the safety data from other indications does not
address the lack of data for the prevention of vomiting induced by motion.
To address the limitations of the clinical and nonclinical safety data, the review team determined
that the label should include a description that the safety of tradipitant beyond 90 doses has not
been established, to ensure the safe and effective use of tradipitant for the prevention of vomiting
induced by motion. Refer to Section 7.7.3 for additional discussion.

7.6. Safety Results
7.6.1. Safety Results, Pooled Analyses, Trials 3401,
3404, and 2401
Pooled analyses of the double-blind, placebo-controlled data from the single-dose Trials 3401,
3404, and 2401 were conducted. The main limitation to pooling data from these trials is that
Trial 2401 only compared tradipitant 170 mg to placebo, whereas Trials 3401 and 3404 included
an additional tradipitant 85-mg arm. In order to preserve randomized comparisons and guard
against potential bias, pooled data were assessed separately for the 85-mg arm versus placebo
and the 170-mg arm versus placebo.

tradipitant 85 mg versus placebo (i.e., Trials 3401 and 3404), and Table 23 provides a summary
of TEAEs for tradipitant 170 mg versus placebo (i.e., Trials 2401, 3401, and 3404). No SAEs
were reported in the tradipitant or placebo arms. No subjects withdrew due to TEAEs, and there

The TEAEs of headache, somnolence, and fatigue occurred more commonly in both tradipitant
arms compared to placebo. Among the trials that assessed 170 mg (Trials 2401, 3401, and 3404),
and trials that assessed 85 mg (Trials 3401 and 3404), somnolence occurred at a higher rate for
the pooled 170-mg arms versus pooled placebo arms (12.5% versus 5.8%, risk difference 6.7%)
compared to the pooled 85-mg arms versus pooled placebo arms (6.2% versus 3.9%; risk
difference 2.3%). There were also greater risk differences in the 170-mg arms versus placebo for
headache (9.7% versus 5.8%; risk difference 3.9%) and fatigue (8.3% vs. 2.1%; risk difference
6.2%) compared to the 85-mg arms for headache (7.0% vs. 5.3%; risk difference 1.7%) and
fatigue (6.2% versus 2.6%; risk difference 3.6%). Of note, the TEAEs reported by subjects may
be a result of motion sickness, as motion sickness may present with a variety of symptoms (e.g.,
nausea, vomiting, headache, somnolence, fatigue, dizziness, belching, diaphoresis). However, the
review team determined that the TEAEs described above were likely related to tradipitant, as
they occurred more frequently in at least one of the tradipitant arms than in the placebo arm and
are consistent with the mechanism of action of tradipitant (i.e., centrally-acting NK-1 receptor
antagonist).

7.6.1.3. Laboratory Findings, Pooled Analyses, Trials 3401,
3404, and 2401
During the single-dose trials, no notable trends in changes from baseline were observed on
routine clinical laboratory parameters (e.g., chemistry, hematology, urinalysis).
7.6.1.4. Assessment of Drug-Induced Liver Injury, Pooled
Analyses, Trials 3401, 3404, and 2401
No potential cases were identified for cholestasis or Hy’s Law (i.e., AST or ALT >3x ULN, total
bilirubin >2x ULN and ALP <2x ULN), or Temple’s Corollary (i.e., AST or ALT >3x ULN)
among subjects receiving tradipitant. There was one subject who met criteria for Temple’s
Corollary in the placebo arm (1/291, 0.3%).
In the pooled tradipitant 85-mg arms, there were few subjects (4/227, 1.8%) whose AST or ALT
rose to ≥2x ULN at a postbaseline trial visit. There was one subject in the placebo arm (1/228,
0.4%) with such an elevation. In the pooled tradipitant 170-mg arms, there were two subjects
(0.7%) whose AST or ALT rose to ≥2x ULN at a postbaseline trial visit. There were three
subjects in the placebo arm (3/291, 1.0%) with such an elevation. Overall, there were no clear
trends for elevated liver enzymes among the generally healthy individuals enrolled in these
single-dose trials.
7.6.1.5. Vital Sign Analyses, Pooled Analyses, Trials 3401,
3404, and 2401
No notable trends were observed during this trial for vital sign parameters, including blood
pressure, pulse rate, respiratory rate, and body temperature.
7.6.1.6. Electrocardiogram Analyses, Pooled Analyses, Trials
3401, 3404, and 2401
Subjects underwent 12-lead ECGs at the screening visit and at the end-of-trial visit. One subject
who received tradipitant 85 mg (1/227, 0.4%) experienced a QTc interval >500 ms, as well as an
increase in QTc interval of ≥60 ms. No other subjects among the three trials experienced such
prolongation of the QTc interval. There were no other notable trends in ECG abnormalities.

Hispanic or Latino (39.3% versus 20.0%) or not Hispanic or Latino (28.0% versus 16.0%). The
numbers of subjects whose race was other than White or Asian, and whose ethnicities were not

7.6.2. Safety Results, Open-Label Trial, Trial 3403
Subjects in Trial 3403 were randomized to either tradipitant 85 mg or tradipitant 170 mg to be
taken as needed 60 to 120 minutes prior to a travel event anticipated to induce motion sickness
symptoms during the 12-month evaluation period.
Trial 3403 was the only trial that assessed multiple doses of tradipitant for the indication of
prevention of vomiting induced by motion. The trial duration was limited to 90 dosing days,
which does not allow for an assessment of clinical safety of tradipitant beyond 90 doses for the
proposed indication. Thus, adverse effects arising from long-term usage of tradipitant cannot be
assessed.
The design of this trial included several limitations that affect the interpretability of the results:


The trial included infrequent safety assessments, occurring every 4 months. Between the
clinical site visits, subjects were expected to report adverse events to the Investigator, but
neither the travel event e-diary nor the weekly e-diary used in the trial queried subjects about
adverse events, which may have results in underreporting of adverse events. The potential of
an underreporting of adverse events is further suggested by the fact that there was a similar
rate of TEAEs reported for Trial 3403 (32.7% for tradipitant 85 mg; 34.4% for tradipitant
170 mg), as compared to the rate of TEAEs in the single dose Trials 3401 (26.8% for
tradipitant 85 mg; 28.3% for tradipitant 170 mg) and 3404 (31.7% for tradipitant 85 mg;
34.9% for tradipitant 170 mg). It would generally be assumed that the rate of TEAEs in Trial
3403 would be greater than in the single-dose trials, due to the subjects’ mean exposure of
approximately 24 doses of tradipitant over the course of 12 months in Trial 3403, and due to
57

Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5720419

NDA 220152
NEREUS (tradipitant)
the Applicant’s definition of TEAE that includes adverse events that are newly occurring or
worsening from baseline until 3 days after the last dose of trial medication (i.e., rather than
the most recent dose of trial medication). Thus, any adverse event occurring between the first
dose and 3 days following the final dose (e.g., occurring during Month 12) would be
considered a TEAE. While the Applicant states that this definition was selected as a
conservative approach, the rates of TEAEs reported in single-dose trials were similar to rates
of TEAEs in Trial 3403. Therefore, due to the infrequent safety assessments, there may have
been underreporting of adverse events.


Subjects were permitted to use tradipitant prior to a variety of travel events, which could
differ in durations and intensities. Approximately half of reported travel events were travel
by car (1142 out of 2302 events in the 85-mg arm, 1107 out of 2198 events in the 170-mg
arm), with the remainder of events distributed among boat, bus, plane, train, and other modes
of travel with similar proportions between the two treatment arms. The variety of travel
conditions permitted in the trial may have introduced additional variability in the effects of
motion among different subjects and among travel events for each subject. Additionally,
subjects may have chosen alternative types of travel to reduce the likelihood of anticipated
motion sickness, or used methods to alleviate symptoms that are specific to the mode of
travel (e.g., sitting in the front seat of a car, sitting in a forward-facing seat on a train).
Without standardization of the travel conditions and without a placebo control, it is
challenging to assess whether reported AEs were due to tradipitant or due to motion sickness.



The two methods utilized by the Applicant to determine the number of doses of tradipitant
used by each subject were found by the review team to have some discrepancies. One method
was to subtract the number of capsules returned from the number of capsules dispensed. The
second method was based on subjects self-reporting when they used tradipitant in an e-diary.
When determining the total number of doses with the first method (counting returned
capsules), a majority of subjects (272 out of 382 [71.2%]) used more doses of tradipitant than
using the second method (subject self-reporting). Conversely, there were some subjects who
used more doses according to their self-reporting than the total calculated based on the
number of capsules dispensed and returned (66 out of 382 [17.3%]). This discrepancy in the
methods for determining exposure limits the interpretation of use patterns observed in this
trial. Specifically, subjects’ overall use of tradipitant may be over-estimated as these data
were calculated based on the number of capsules returned compared to those dispensed, and
capsules/bottles of medication that were “missing” were presumed to be utilized by subjects.
Additionally, the number of doses determined to be used during time periods shorter than the
full duration of the trial (i.e., maximum use in any 30-day period, maximum consecutive
dosing days) may be under-estimated, as these exposure data are determined based on
subjects’ e-diary records, which may be incomplete depending on subjects’ utilization of the
e-diary.



Subjects did not strictly adhere to their assigned dosing regimens. Among the trips taken by
subjects in the 85-mg arm, a different dose was used (i.e., a subject self-administered 170 mg
rather than 85 mg) in approximately 9% of trips. Among trips taken by subjects in the 170mg arm, a different dose was used (i.e., a subjects self-administered 85 mg rather than
170 mg) in approximately 5% of trips. These deviations in dosing from the assigned
treatment arms limits the interpretability of the data for each treatment arm.
58

Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5720419

NDA 220152
NEREUS (tradipitant)


The trial did not include a placebo arm or concurrent comparator; therefore, it is challenging
to determine which adverse events were attributable to tradipitant rather than other factors
(e.g., symptoms of motion sickness, concomitant medications) in the intended use
population. This is particularly relevant for tradipitant, as the most common adverse events in
the single-dose trials (i.e., somnolence, headache, and fatigue) overlap with symptoms of
motion sickness.

returned.

tradipitant 170-mg arm) used tradipitant for 30 or fewer days throughout the duration of the 12month trial. This indicates that most subjects used the drug infrequently, consistent with the
typical sporadic occurrence of motion sickness symptoms. However, the remaining subjects,
which amount to nearly one-third of the trial population, used the drug for greater than 30 days,

Subjects in Trial 3403 were dispensed tradipitant in either one or two 36-count bottles every four months during
the 12-month trial. Despite the protocol specifying that tradipitant capsules should be dispensed such that subjects

7.6.2.2. Overview of Treatment-Emergent Adverse Events,
Summary, Trial 3403
Table 31 below provides a summary of TEAEs reported in Trial 3403. Overall, TEAEs were
reported by a similar proportion of subjects in the tradipitant 85-mg (32.7%) and tradipitant 170mg (34.4%) arms. A higher proportion of subjects had TEAEs leading to permanent
discontinuation of trial drug in the tradipitant 170-mg arm than the tradipitant 85-mg arm (4.4%
versus 2.5%, respectively), as described in Section 7.6.2.5 below.

7.6.2.4. Serious Treatment-Emergent Adverse Events, Trial
3403
There was one subject in the tradipitant 85-mg arm who experienced an SAE (1/199, 0.5%), and
three subjects in the tradipitant 170-mg arm who experienced an SAE (3/183, 1.6%). Eleven
SAEs occurred in one subject in the tradipitant 170-mg arm, and an additional 2 SAEs (i.e.,
femur fracture and sinus node dysfunction) each occurred in different subjects. No SAE occurred
in more than one subject.
The narratives for the SAEs in the tradipitant 85-mg arm and tradipitant 170-mg arm are
described below:






(b) (6) , a 72-year-old female with history of motion sickness as well as
Anemia: Subject
left hip osteoarthritis was randomized to receive tradipitant 85 mg as needed. On trial Day
164, 87 days following her most recent dose of tradipitant, the subject experienced
symptomatic anemia and was hospitalized for 5 days. She presented to an emergency
department immediately following an outpatient left hip total arthroplasty, due to
symptomatic anemia associated with hypotension as a result of surgical bleeding. She
received intravenous normal saline, albumin, and a unit of packed red blood cells. Her
symptomatic anemia and hypotension resolved. The event was determined by the investigator
to be unrelated to the trial medication, and she remained enrolled in the trial, in which she
used a total of 7 doses of tradipitant. The clinical context suggests that the TEAE of anemia
was secondary to a complication of the surgery that the subject underwent for a pre-existing
condition.
(b) (6) , a 62-year-old female with history of motion sickness was
Femur fracture: Subject
randomized to receive tradipitant 170 mg as needed. On trial Day 106, 57 days following her
most recent dose of tradipitant, the subject experienced an accidental slip and fall while at
home, which resulted in a left proximal femur fracture. She was hospitalized for 5 days and
underwent surgery for a rod implantation. The event was determined by the investigator to be
unrelated to the trial medication, and she remained enrolled in the trial, in which she used a
total of 30.5 doses of tradipitant.21
(b) (6) , a 63-year-old male with history of motion
Sinus node dysfunction: Subject

following a dose of tradipitant, the subject visited an emergency department for syncope and
stayed overnight for diagnostic tests and observation. Tests that were performed included
computerized tomography scan of the head without contrast, and a chest radiograph. He was
diagnosed with sick sinus syndrome as the etiology for his syncopal event. He underwent
placement of a pacemaker and was discharged from the hospital in stable condition 2 days
after his initial presentation. The event was determined by the investigator to be unrelated to

(b) (6) , a 69-year-old female with
stenosis, dysarthria, and essential hypertension: Subject
history of motion sickness was randomized to receive tradipitant 170 mg as needed. On trial
Day 256, 41 days following her most recent dose of tradipitant, the subject experienced an
initial cerebrovascular accident prompting hospitalization, and diagnosis was performed via
magnetic resonance imaging of the brain. The investigator was made aware of these events
87 days after the onset of the SAEs, when trying to schedule an interim trial visit. The
investigator considered all of these events to be unrelated to tradipitant and were instead
attributed to obesity as the primary precipitating factor. She remained enrolled in the trial, in
which she used a total of 29 doses of tradipitant. The constellation of TEAEs suggests that
the subject experienced multiple sequelae of a cerebrovascular accident, which may have
been precipitated by underlying obesity and hypertension.



Asthenia, glucose tolerance impaired, influenza-like symptoms, myalgia, urinary tract
(b) (6) , a 69-year-old female with history of motion sickness as well as
infection: Subject
a recent cerebrovascular accident (see above), was randomized to receive tradipitant 170 mg
as needed. On trial Day 303, 6 weeks after her hospitalization for cerebrovascular accident
(see above), and 3 days following her most recent dose of tradipitant, she presented with
nausea and chills upon returning home from a cruise. She was hospitalized for 2 days and
underwent a diagnostic evaluation that included a chest radiograph, transthoracic
echocardiogram, computed tomography (CT) scan of the head, magnetic resonance imaging
of the head, electrocardiogram, and laboratory evaluations, all of which did not reveal
evidence of a new stroke or a specific unifying etiology for her symptoms. She underwent
CT angiography of the head and neck, which showed chronic left vertebral artery stenosis.
She was diagnosed with generalized weakness and myalgia, flu-like symptoms, and possible
urinary tract infection with secondary diagnoses of hypertension and prediabetes. She
received anticoagulants, antihypertensive medication, and statins. She reported ongoing
symptoms of shaking hands, difficulties with problem solving, generalized weakness, and
dizziness. The investigator considered this event to be unrelated to tradipitant, and more
likely a result of underlying disease and pre-existing conditions, including obesity. She
remained enrolled in the trial and ultimately received a total of 29 doses of tradipitant. The
constellation of TEAEs suggests that the subject experienced a likely infectious illness in the
context of multiple pre-existing conditions.
7.6.2.5. Treatment-Emergent Adverse Events Leading to
Treatment Discontinuation, Trial 3403

Table 32 below shows TEAEs leading to discontinuation from Trial 3403. The most common
TEAE leading to discontinuation in the tradipitant 85-mg arm was somnolence, which occurred
in two subjects and was the only TEAE that led to discontinuation in more than one subject in
that treatment arm. There was one TEAE leading to interruption of the trial drug in the
tradipitant 85-mg arm, which was a rise in serum transaminase levels. In the tradipitant 170-mg
arm, the most common TEAEs that led to trial discontinuation were somnolence, dizziness, and
fatigue, which each occurred in two subjects.

(b) (6) , a 52-year-old male with history of motion
Somnolence and dry mouth: Subject
sickness was randomized to receive tradipitant 170 mg as needed. The subject had a pertinent
past medical history of hearing loss in the left ear and benign premature ventricular
contractions. Concomitant medications and supplements included naproxen, B complex
vitamins, vitamin C, Vitamin D3, omega complex, magnesium, glucosamine chondroitin, and
a multivitamin. On Day 1, the subject experienced mild dry mouth and mild somnolence,
which improved on Day 30. The last dose of tradipitant prior to the event was earlier on
Day 1. The subject was discontinued due to TEAEs on Day 56 of the trial at which time he
had used a total of 6.5 doses of tradipitant. The Investigator determined that the TEAEs were
certainly related to the trial drug and not serious. The Investigator coded the AE outcomes as
resolved for dry mouth, and unknown for somnolence, both without treatment. The
constellation and timing of TEAEs suggests that the subject’s symptoms were related to
tradipitant.

and syncope as resolved, and fractured sacrum as not resolved, with continuing use of
concomitant medication. The constellation and timing of TEAEs suggests that the subject
possibly experienced hyperhidrosis and syncope related to tradipitant.

(b) (6) , a 43-year-old male with history of motion sickness
Elevated liver tests: Subject
was randomized to receive tradipitant 85 mg as needed. The subject had a pertinent past
medical history of nonalcoholic steatohepatitis, and he used no concomitant medications. At
Visit 4 on Day 230, the subject experienced elevated ALT of 132 U/L (ULN =40 U/L),
elevated from a baseline value of 56 U/L. The most recent dose prior to the start of the TEAE
was on Day 228, by which time he had e-diary entries reporting that he had used 9 doses of
tradipitant. He underwent an unscheduled visit on Day 246 showing reduced ALT to
109 U/L. Although AST was elevated from 23 U/L at baseline to 51 U/L at Visit 4
(ULN =43 U/L), this improved to 37 U/L at the unscheduled visit. He did not experience
elevated alkaline phosphatase or total bilirubin levels. The TEAE resolved without treatment,
and the TEAE was documented as having ended on Day 246, despite the ALT remaining
elevated as it declined to 92 U/L at Visit 5 on Day 336. The subject’s treatment was
documented as interrupted due to this TEAE, despite e-diary documentation of the subject
taking tradipitant 2 times during the period between TEAE onset and resolution. He
documented using tradipitant another 13 times after resolution of the TEAE. By the end of
the trial, he had used 58 doses of tradipitant.22 The case narrative indicates that the site
reported that the subject could continue the trial without interruption due to the potential for
the subject’s underlying nonalcoholic steatohepatitis serving as an alternative explanation for
the laboratory abnormalities. The constellation and timing of TEAEs indicates that the
subject’s adverse events were possibly related to tradipitant.

7.6.2.6. Treatment-Emergent Adverse Events, Trial 3403
Table 33 below describes TEAEs occurring in ≥2% of subjects. The most common TEAEs for
subjects receiving tradipitant were somnolence, fatigue, and dizziness. The TEAEs that occurred
at a higher rate in the tradipitant 170-mg arm compared to the tradipitant 85-mg arm included
somnolence (12.0% versus 10.6%), fatigue (8.7% versus 5.0%), dizziness (3.8% versus 2.5%),
and nausea (2.2% versus 1.0%).

Although the TEAEs of somnolence, fatigue, dizziness, and nausea appeared to occur more
frequently in the tradipitant 170-mg arm than in the 85-mg arm, the TEAEs reported by subjects
could be due to motion sickness, as motion sickness may present with a variety of symptoms
(e.g., belching, diaphoresis, dizziness, headache, fatigue, and somnolence). Based on Trial 3403

data alone, without a concurrent comparator arm, it is challenging to determine whether the
TEAEs described above were related to tradipitant; however, given that somnolence, fatigue, and
dizziness occurred at higher rates in the tradipitant arms than the placebo arms in the pooled
single-dose trials (see Section 7.6.1.2), it is likely that these adverse events are attributable to
tradipitant.
7.6.2.7. Laboratory Findings, Trial 3403
Overall, there were no clinically relevant trends in the laboratory data. However, interpretation of
the laboratory data is limited due to the design of the open-label trial. Laboratory data were
collected at trial visits, which occurred every 4 months, and the visits occurred irrespective of the
timing of the subjects’ exposure to tradipitant. Thus, the submitted data may not accurately
reflect the potential effects of recent tradipitant exposure on laboratory results.
7.6.2.8. Assessment of Drug-Induced Liver Injury, Trial 3403
No potential cases were identified for Hy’s Law (i.e., AST or ALT >3x ULN, total bilirubin >2x
ULN and ALP <2x ULN). There was one subject in the tradipitant 85-mg arm (1/165; 0.6%)
who met criteria for Temple’s Corollary with ALT ≥3x the ULN at Visit 4. There was one
subject in the tradipitant 170-mg arm who met criteria for cholestasis with bilirubin ≥2x the ULN
at Visit 4 (1/149; 0.7%).
7.6.2.9. Vital Sign Analyses, Trial 3403
There were no substantial changes over time or differences between arms in vital sign parameters
during this trial, including weight, blood pressure, pulse rate, respiratory rate, and body
temperature.
7.6.2.10. Electrocardiogram Analyses, Trial 3403
Subjects underwent 12-lead ECGs at the screening visit (Visit 1) and at the end-of-trial visit
(Visit 5). There was an increase in the QTcB interval aggregate of ≥30 ms relative to baseline in
5.8% of subjects in the tradipitant 85-mg arm, and in 5.7% of subjects in the tradipitant 170-mg
arm. There were no other abnormalities exceeding 5% of subjects in either treatment arm.
7.6.2.11. Subgroup Analyses, Trial 3403
Table 34 describes TEAEs by demographic subgroups (sex, age group, race, ethnicity) in those
receiving tradipitant 85 mg and tradipitant 170 mg. Adverse event rates were higher in the
tradipitant 170-mg arm compared to the 85-mg arm for subjects who were male (40.0% versus
26.7%) and of White race (35.5% versus 29.9%). Such a difference was not observed in female
subjects or subjects of Asian race. The number of subjects who were races other than White or
Asian was small, precluding a clear determination regarding whether there is possible doseresponse in these demographic subgroups.

7.6.2.12. Safety Update Report, Trial 3403
A 120-day safety update report was submitted during the review, which included additional
safety data for Trial 3403, including data for an additional 69 subjects in the tradipitant 85-mg
arm (268 subjects total) and 74 subjects in the tradipitant 170-mg arm (257 subjects total),
resulting in a total safety population of 525 subjects. The demographics among subjects included
in the safety update are generally similar compared to the population originally submitted for
review. The update included a mean duration of exposure (determined by cumulative dosing
days, excluding days between doses) of 24.5 days in the tradipitant 85-mg arm (previously 23.8
days) and 24.3 days in the tradipitant 170-mg arm (previously 23.9 days). There were 2
additional trial discontinuations due to TEAEs. These included one TEAE of breast cancer in the
tradipitant 85-mg arm, and one TEAE of migraine in the tradipitant 170-mg arm. There was one
additional SAE, which was a case of ureterolithiasis in the tradipitant 170-mg arm. There were
no deaths among subjects included in the safety update. Overall, the safety update report did not
identify new safety signals or affect statements of contraindications, warnings, precautions, and
adverse reactions in the draft labeling.

NEREUS (tradipitant)

7.7. Key Safety Review Issues
7.7.1. Adequacy of Nonclinical Information To Assess
the Toxicological Effects of Tradipitant
Issue
The Applicant proposed for tradipitant to be used for the prevention of vomiting induced by
motion on an as-needed basis, with no restriction on the total duration of use. However, the
Applicant has neither conducted a chronic toxicity study in a nonrodent species with tradipitant,
nor have they provided adequate scientific justification that such a study is not warranted to
support unrestricted use of tradipitant over a lifetime for this indication. Additionally, the
Applicant has not provided any data or scientific justification to suggest that the cumulative
toxicity of tradipitant will be different whether dosing occurs consecutively or as needed (e.g.,
intermittently). Although tradipitant will be used intermittently at the discretion of the individual
for the prevention of vomiting induced by motion (i.e., for prevention of discrete episodes on an
as-needed basis), this use is expected to be repeated over a lifetime, as such episodes are
expected to occur whenever the motion stimulus is present. Therefore, the adequacy of the
nonclinical information submitted to the current NDA to support the proposed unrestricted use
for this indication, i.e., repeated intermittent use over a lifetime, was identified as a review issue.
Assessment
An NDA submission is required to contain nonclinical studies of the toxicological effects of the
drug as they relate to the drug’s intended clinical uses, including, as appropriate, studies
assessing the drug’s acute, subacute, and chronic toxicity (21 CFR 314.50(d)(2)(ii)). As vomiting
induced by motion may occur whenever the motion stimulus is present, any pharmacologic
intervention for this indication is expected to be used repeatedly over a lifetime. Nonclinical
programs are designed to assess and inform the risks of a new drug with the most intensive use
(i.e., worst-case scenario). Repeated, intermittent use of tradipitant over a lifetime (i.e., whenever
an individual is trying to prevent vomiting induced by motion) is such a scenario for this
indication, as clinical data with tradipitant in Trial 3403 have demonstrated that a subset of
individuals are likely to use the product frequently (see Section 7.6.2.1). Additionally, the
relatively long half-life of tradipitant and its major human metabolites (28+ hours) supports that
even single doses spaced days apart could lead to sustained exposure to and even accumulation
of drug-related material.
While the nonclinical review team acknowledges that high doses of tradipitant (up to 1500
mg/kg/day) were tested in the completed nonclinical studies, this does not provide additional
assurance of safety in lieu of adequate nonclinical studies, and it is inappropriate to base a safety
determination on the dose level alone. Plasma exposure to tradipitant-related compounds did not
increase proportionally to the increasing dose administered in either rats or dogs. Based on
human equivalent dose, the highest tested dose of 1500 mg/kg/day23 was 85- and 171-fold the
23

While the highest tested dose of 1500 mg/kg/day was the NOAEL in the 6-month repeat-dose toxicity study in
Fischer rats, the NOAEL in the 3-month study in dogs was 275 mg/kg/day (31-fold the MPHD based on HED).

71
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5720419

NDA 220152
NEREUS (tradipitant)
MPHD of 170 mg/day in rats and dogs, respectively. However, based on the AUC of tradipitant
alone,24 this dose was only 0.4/1.1- and 12/24-fold25 the MPHD in male/female rats and dogs,
respectively. Thus, the unrestricted use of tradipitant over a lifetime for the prevention of
vomiting due to motion would expose some individuals to physiological levels of tradipitantrelated material over a duration of use without adequate nonclinical assessment of the
toxicological risks.
The Applicant has neither conducted a chronic repeat-dose toxicity study in a nonrodent species,
nor have they provided a suitable alternative, to adequately characterize the safety of unrestricted
use of tradipitant over a lifetime (see Section 12). Completed repeat-dose toxicity studies with
tradipitant include studies of up to 3 months in mice, 6 months in rats, and 3 months in dogs.
FDA, in concert with other international regulatory agencies, generally considers subchronic
(e.g., 3-month) toxicity studies to be insufficient in predicting the long-term systemic toxicity of
a drug in humans (DeGeorge et al. 1999; Ledwith and DeGeorge 2011; Jacobs and Hatfield
2013; Roberts et al. 2015; Galijatovic-Idrizbegovic et al. 2016; Monticello et al. 2017). More
specifically, a chronic toxicity study in a nonrodent species can identify new toxicities or
demonstrate the progression of minor or adaptive events to adverse effects that may be missed or
unreported in shorter duration toxicity studies. Moreover, testing in a minimum of two
mammalian animal species (i.e., one rodent and one nonrodent) provides adequate assurance of
detecting toxicities that may be relevant to humans, as unique toxicities may occur in one species
versus another (Broadhead et al. 2000; Olson et al. 2000; Bailey et al. 2014; Monticello et al.
2017). Thus, chronic toxicity studies in two species are important to support the safety of a drug
such as tradipitant for marketing approval (i.e., a drug for which there will be unrestricted use
over a lifetime, without clinical trial safeguards in place). In the absence of a chronic toxicity
study in a nonrodent species or a suitable alternative, the available nonclinical data are
inadequate to support marketing approval for unrestricted use of tradipitant over a lifetime.
The Applicant proposed26 that their additional in vitro studies with tradipitant (i.e., in vitro liver
safety pharmacology and special toxicology studies using a four organ MPS), as part of a totality
of evidence approach, adequately supported the safety of unrestricted use of tradipitant over a
lifetime in the absence of a chronic toxicity study in a nonrodent species. Following review of
these in vitro studies, the review team determined that these studies did not substantially
contribute to the characterization of the long-term safety of tradipitant, based on limitations
including lack of technical characterization, lack of established biological relevance, and short
duration (see Section 13.2.3 and Section 13.2.6). As such, the review team concluded that a
chronic toxicity study in a nonrodent species, or a suitable alternative, was still warranted to
support the safety of unrestricted use of tradipitant over a lifetime.
As real-world use of an approved drug is not highly controlled like in a clinical trial, the
recommended duration of nonclinical data to support marketing is longer than for clinical trials,
with the intention of identifying as many risks as possible to inform safe use of a new product
prior to approval. In the case of tradipitant for the prevention of vomiting induced by motion,
24

A comparison of total tradipitant-related exposure (i.e., tradipitant + M2 + M3 +M4 + M8) in animals versus
humans could not be made for the 6-month study in Fischer rats or the 3-month study in dogs, as the metabolites
were not measured in these studies.
25
With the NOAEL in dogs being 275 mg/kg/day (9.5/7.8-fold the MPHD in males and females, based on AUC of
tradipitant alone).
(b) (4)

72
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5720419

NDA 220152
NEREUS (tradipitant)
unrestricted, repeat intermittent use over a lifetime should be supported by chronic toxicity
studies in two species (i.e., a 6-month study in a rodent species and a ≥6-month study in a
nonrodent species), or a suitable alternative. As discussed, the Applicant has not adequately
assessed the safety of tradipitant for unrestricted, lifetime use.
Thus, the nonclinical data are insufficient to support that tradipitant is safe for unrestricted use
over a lifetime.
Conclusion
The nonclinical review team acknowledges that the clinical trial data demonstrate that tradipitant
is effective at preventing vomiting induced by motion, and clinical safety data with tradipitant
are available for up to 90 doses. We also acknowledge that the prescribing information will
describe the limitations of the available safety data. However, there is insufficient nonclinical
information to adequately support the safety of unrestricted use of tradipitant over a lifetime in
human subjects, and the lack of adequate nonclinical data to support the unrestricted use of
tradipitant over a lifetime is an approvability issue.

7.7.2. Adequacy of Nonclinical Information To Assess
the Toxicological Effects of Metabolites M2, M4, and
M8
Issue
The Applicant has identified four major metabolites of tradipitant in human plasma: M2, M3,
M4, and M8. These metabolites have been investigated in a series of nonclinical studies, and
their plasma levels were measured in select toxicity studies conducted in rats and mice. Based on
available data, these metabolites are considered to be pharmacologically active, toxicologically
relevant, and present at significant levels compared to the parent drug, tradipitant. These factors
support that the safety of levels of these metabolites should be qualified based on nonclinical
data. Whether the nonclinical data are sufficient to qualify the safety of these metabolites was
identified as a review issue.
Background
Based on PK studies in humans, the Applicant identified “four major metabolites” of tradipitant:
M2, M3, M4, and M8.27 Nonclinical and clinical data to assess the safety of the four major
human metabolites were limited during clinical development. At the time that phase 3 trials with
tradipitant were initiated (i.e., the point at which qualification of metabolites is typically
expected), the Applicant’s available nonclinical data supported that M2, M4, and M8 were likely
to be present at significantly lower levels in tested nonclinical species compared to humans (i.e.,
that these were disproportionate metabolites).28 However, at no point during development were
the available clinical pharmacology data sufficient to definitively determine whether any
metabolite exceeded the typical threshold of 10% of drug-related exposure at steady state29 at the
27

See Module 2.6.4 Pharmacokinetics Written Summary, pg. 9.
See Module 2.6.4 Pharmacokinetics Written Summary, Table 26, pgs. 43 to 44.
29
FDA guidance for industry Safety Testing of Drug Metabolites Revision 2 (March 2020).
28

73
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5720419

NDA 220152
NEREUS (tradipitant)
proposed clinical doses that would warrant qualification by nonclinical data. Additionally,
clinical trials with tradipitant have not exceeded 12 weeks or 90 dosing days for any indication.
Therefore, the potential for unqualified metabolites was not considered a safety issue for
tradipitant prior to the submission of this marketing application.
Assessment
Typically, “human metabolites that can raise a safety concern are those present at greater than 10
percent of total drug-related exposure at steady state,” based on area under the concentrationtime curve (AUC).29 In this case, clinical pharmacology data with tradipitant were previously
limited such that it could not be determined whether these metabolites meet the criteria for
further evaluation. However, in this NDA submission,30 the available data supported that the
levels of all four metabolites represent a notable proportion of exposure in humans relative to
tradipitant itself. Additionally, based on information submitted to the current NDA, these
metabolites are considered pharmacologically active (Section 13.2.1) and were shown to induce
hepatocyte death in vitro using human donor-derived cells (Section 13.2.3). As such, the review
team determined that the potential toxicity of these major human metabolites should be
adequately evaluated in nonclinical studies, despite the unclear contribution of individual
metabolites to total drug-related exposure at steady state.
Metabolites M2, M3, and M4 were measured and/or directly evaluated in select nonclinical
pharmacology, pharmacokinetics, in vitro liver safety pharmacology, repeat-dose toxicity (rats
only), genetic toxicology (in vitro), reproductive toxicity (pre- and postnatal development study
only), carcinogenicity, special toxicology, and/or in silico studies (see Sections 13.1.4, 13.1.5,
13.1.7, 13.1.8, 13.2.1, 13.2.2, 13.2.3, 13.2.4, 13.2.5, 13.2.6, and 13.2.7). M8, a glucuronide
conjugate, was evaluated for binding and activity in vitro as well as in secondary pharmacology,
PK, in vitro safety liver pharmacology, and in silico studies (see Sections 13.1.4, 13.2.1, 13.2.2,
13.2.3, and 13.2.7).
Qualification Based on In Vivo Nonclinical Data
As levels of M3 were similar31 in rats compared to humans, the safety of M3 is considered
adequately qualified by nonclinical data.32
In rats and mice, M2 and M4 were not present or were observed at lower levels compared to the
single-dose or steady-state levels in humans, such that under no condition was at least 50% of
human M2 or M4 exposure achieved in animals. M2 and M4 were not measured in any study in
a nonrodent species, while M8 was not measured in any nonclinical animal study. Based on the

30

218489 or previously submitted to any IND for tradipitant. See discussion of the timeline for the review of
metabolite data in the Nonclinical Supplementary NDA Review dated December 30, 2025.

Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)

NDA 220152
NEREUS (tradipitant)
available information, metabolites M2, M4, and M8 are disproportionate human metabolites that
have not been qualified based on the available in vivo nonclinical data.
Qualification Based on Toxicological Similarity to Tradipitant
Safety qualification of metabolites is generally established by either 1) dosing of the parent drug
in a nonclinical species determined to produce the metabolite(s) at sufficient levels compared to
the human exposure; or 2) direct dosing of the unqualified metabolite(s) at doses sufficient to
evaluate toxicity at exposures similar to or greater than levels formed in humans. In the absence
of such data, the review team considered an alternative approach for NDA 220152, specifically,
a Weight of Evidence (WoE) analysis to support that metabolites M2, M4, and M8 were not
expected to be toxicologically different from tradipitant itself, thereby allowing for qualification
based on total tradipitant drug-related exposure rather than on an individual basis. While this
approach had been suggested previously, during early clinical development of tradipitant with
the originating sponsor, the review team notes that this approach was only feasible because of
data that were submitted recently (i.e., between September 2023 and October 2025). Pivotal new
data included primary pharmacology (binding and/or functional activity), secondary
pharmacology, liver safety pharmacology, in vitro distribution (protein binding), and in vitro
genotoxicity studies with the metabolites, as well as population PK modeling supporting the
relevance of healthy volunteer PK parameters for the motion sickness population and in silico
analyses using various computational tools and databases.
Full discussion of the WoE analyses for each metabolite can be found in the Nonclinical
Supplementary NDA Review dated December 30, 2025. Briefly, Quantitative Structure-Activity
Relationship ([Q]SAR) analyses of the di-aryl alcohol and/or N-oxide moieties present on M2,
M3, and M4 compared to tradipitant were conducted by FDA/CDER’s Division of Applied
Regulatory Science (DARS) Computational Toxicology Consultation Service (CTCS),33 and
comparative computational prediction analyses were conducted by the Applicant.34,35,36,37 These
analyses supported that the structural differences would not result in new or exaggerated
toxicities with the metabolites compared to tradipitant itself (Table 35). Analysis of the recently
provided in vitro pharmacology, pharmacokinetic, and genotoxicity data further supported the
toxicological similarity of tradipitant and all four major metabolites (Table 36). Therefore, the
WoE supported that qualification of the disproportionate human metabolites (M2, M4, and M8)
using total drug-related exposure, rather than on an individual basis, was scientifically
appropriate.

Reference ID: 5720419

NDA 220152
NEREUS (tradipitant)
Comparison of the total tradipitant drug-related exposure (i.e., tradipitant plus all measured
metabolites) between animals and humans demonstrated sufficient exposure margins such that
metabolites M2, M4, and M8 were considered qualified based on the available nonclinical data.
Conclusion
Based on all data available at the time of this review, it was determined that adequate nonclinical
assessment of the major human metabolites, M2, M3, M4, and M8, was warranted to support the
safety of tradipitant for its intended clinical use. Only M3 was considered qualified based on the
levels measured in repeat-dose toxicity studies in animals compared to those in humans, while
M2, M4, and M8 were considered disproportionate human metabolites. A WoE approach,
including in silico toxicology analyses of the structural differences between tradipitant and its
metabolites as well as recently submitted in vitro pharmacology, pharmacokinetic, and
genotoxicity data, supported that tradipitant and the disproportionate human metabolites were
expected to be toxicologically similar. This conclusion allowed for the qualification of these
metabolites based on total tradipitant drug-related exposure, rather than individually. Thus, all
identified major human metabolites of tradipitant are considered qualified for safety based on
available nonclinical data. However, as the nonclinical data supporting the safety of unrestricted
use of tradipitant over a lifetime are considered inadequate, the safety of the metabolites is
likewise not considered to be adequately supported by nonclinical data for unrestricted use of
tradipitant over a lifetime.
Notably, it is not generally expected that such a WoE approach will be appropriate for the
qualification of metabolites in other development programs, as each situation must be evaluated
on its own merits based on the totality of data and information. Although in silico analyses are
highly valuable in certain situations, they are unlikely to routinely address safety concerns in lieu
of typical in vivo toxicity studies.

7.7.3. Adequacy of the Clinical Safety Database To
Characterize Safety of Tradipitant for Vomiting
Induced by Motion
Issue
Tradipitant is proposed for the indication of the prevention of vomiting induced by motion,
which is a condition that is likely to occur repeatedly over the course of an individual’s lifetime.
The Applicant proposes unrestricted use of tradipitant with no limitation on the total duration of
use. Thus, understanding the safety of long-term intermittent repeated dosing is important to
inform the safe use of this product as a preventive medication for vomiting induced by motion.
Background
Vomiting induced by motion is one manifestation of the complex and multisystem disorder
commonly referred to as “motion sickness.” Motion sickness is expected to recur in susceptible
individuals upon reintroduction of the triggering motion stimulus, notwithstanding the potential
for habituation that typically occurs when the triggering motion stimulus is experienced
repeatedly and predictably. Generally, for conditions such as vomiting induced by motion (i.e.,
conditions that are likely to require repeated intermittent use over the long-term – e.g., migraine,
81
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5720419

NDA 220152
NEREUS (tradipitant)
erectile dysfunction, asthma), the safety evaluation of a drug is expected to characterize the
safety profile for a duration of time consistent with the intended long-term use. However,
depending on the frequency of the need for treatment for the condition, it may not be feasible for
a clinical trial to collect data in a sufficient number of subjects with long-term exposure in the
intended use population, as would be expected for chronic conditions that require daily
treatment. Thus, the characterization of safety for vomiting induced by motion is supported by
the clinical safety data, which serves to characterize safety in the anticipated use conditions, and
the nonclinical data, which serves to characterize the toxicological risks for the proposed
duration of use.
The Applicant provided safety data from three placebo-controlled trials assessing the effects of a
single dose of tradipitant (Trials 2401, 3401, and 3404) and a fourth trial assessing multiple
doses (Trial 3403). Trial 3403, the only repeat-dose trial in the intended use population, was an
open-label, uncontrolled trial in which subjects could take tradipitant as needed prior to any
motion sickness-inducing event for up to 90 total doses over the 12-month trial duration.
Notably, Trial 3403 had infrequent safety assessments, potential under-reporting of adverse
events, variability in the type of travel events, and no concurrent comparator arm. Therefore, it is
difficult to attribute AEs to tradipitant versus other factors. Additional safety information was
provided from clinical trials assessing the use of tradipitant for other indications, including
gastroparesis; however, the longest duration of controlled trials in these other indications was 12
weeks. As described in Section 7.7.1, the Applicant has not provided sufficient nonclinical
information to adequately characterize the safety of unrestricted use of tradipitant over a lifetime
in human subjects.
Assessment
Among the data submitted from trials conducted in the intended use population, placebocontrolled clinical safety data are available from 516 subjects who received a single dose of
tradipitant; 227 received the 85-mg dose and 289 received the 170-mg dose. The remaining
safety data were from open-label, uncontrolled, as-needed use of tradipitant in 382 subjects
enrolled in Trial 3403 (up to 90 doses over the course of 12 months, see Section 7.6.2). The
review team’s safety analysis of the placebo-controlled clinical safety data identified that the
TEAEs that occurred most commonly and at higher rates in subjects receiving tradipitant
compared to placebo were somnolence, headache, and fatigue. Each of these TEAEs occurred at
a higher rate for the pooled 170-mg arms versus pooled placebo arms compared to the pooled 85mg arms versus pooled placebo arms. For example, somnolence occurred at a rate of 12.5% in
the pooled 170-mg am versus 5.8% in the pooled placebo arm, resulting in a risk difference of
6.7%, whereas the rate of somnolence in the pooled 85-mg arm was 6.2% versus 3.9% in the
pooled placebo arm, resulting in a risk difference of 2.3%. Although there are several limitations
that affect the interpretability of the results of Trial 3403, the safety data from Trial 3403 showed
similar adverse events and did not identify any new safety signals (see Section 7.6.2).
Additionally, Trial 3403 showed that tradipitant usage was intermittent (e.g., the maximum
number of doses in a 30-day period for the majority of subjects was 4 or fewer; the maximum
number of consecutive dosing days for the majority of subjects was 2 or fewer) and infrequent
(e.g., the majority of subjects did not exceed 30 total dosing days during the 12 month trial)
suggesting that individuals with motion sickness are unlikely to take a drug for the prevention of
vomiting induced by motion frequently (see Section 7.6.2.1).
82
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5720419

NDA 220152
NEREUS (tradipitant)
The analysis of the data from the pooled single-dose trials and the multiple-dose open-label trial
shows that the 170 mg dose is associated with higher rates of adverse events compared to the 85
mg dose. However, this increased risk is small, and the overall benefit-risk assessment is
favorable for both the 85 mg and 170 mg doses.
The clinical safety database also includes data from trials conducted in other patient populations,
all with controlled trial durations of no more than 12 weeks. In the clinical program evaluating
tradipitant for treatment of gastroparesis, subjects received tradipitant 85 mg twice daily in one
phase 2 (4-week placebo-controlled phase) and one phase 3 trial (12-week placebo-controlled
duration). The safety data from these trials were considered as part of the review. While the data
from these trials provides some assurance of safety for up to 12 weeks, tradipitant was not
approved for treating gastroparesis, and the generalizability of the safety information in subjects
with other conditions to the safety in individuals with motion sickness is low due to differences
in pathogenesis, intended use, comorbidities, and concomitant treatments.
Taken together, the available safety data support the safe use of tradipitant in the intended use
population for 90 total doses; however, the safety of the unrestricted use of tradipitant has not
been adequately characterized. There are no clinical trial safety data for the use of tradipitant for
greater than 90 doses or 12 weeks, and there are insufficient nonclinical data to characterize the
safety of unrestricted, lifelong use of tradipitant (see Section 7.7.1). Thus, a description in the
label that the safety of more than 90 doses has not been established is warranted to ensure the
safe and effective use of tradipitant for the proposed indication.
Conclusion
The data provided in the submission do not support unrestricted use of tradipitant, due to
insufficient data to adequately characterize such use for a lifetime. The clinical review team
determined that the available safety data support the safe use of tradipitant for up to 90 doses.
Although some individuals may use a preventative treatment for vomiting induced by motion for
more than 90 doses over their lifetime, the typical individual is anticipated to use such a
treatment infrequently due to the intermittent and predictable nature of vomiting induced by
motion (as evidenced by the use data from Trial 3403). Thus, the label will describe the upper
limit beyond which safety has not been established and will state in the dosing and
administration section that the safety of tradipitant for more than 90 doses has not been
established in clinical trials. This information is warranted to inform prescribers of the safe use
of the product because some individuals would be expected to exceed the duration of use
supported by the available safety data.
Additionally, the overall benefit-risk assessment is favorable for both the 85-mg and 170-mg
doses. In the clinical trials, the 170-mg dose was associated with slightly higher rates of adverse
events compared to the 85-mg dose. Because of this dose-dependent increase in adverse events,
both doses of tradipitant should be approved with a recommendation that individuals use the
lowest effective dose.

Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5720419

NDA 220152
NEREUS (tradipitant)

8. Therapeutic Individualization
8.1. Intrinsic Factors
Pharmacokinetic difference between subjects with history of motion sickness and healthy
subjects were not identified based on the available data.
Hepatic Impairment
Use of tradipitant in subjects with mild, moderate, or severe hepatic impairment is not
recommended. In the mass balance study, following a single 25 mg (100 μCi) oral dose
administration of 14C-radiolabeled tradipitant, about 80% of the radioactivity was recovered
mainly as metabolite forms in feces within 312 hours postdose. While hepatobiliary excretion in
the main elimination pathway of tradipitant and its metabolites, no dedicated study in hepatic
impaired subjects have been performed.
The Applicant’s proposal to not recommend tradipitant for patients with mild, moderate, or
severe hepatic impairment is acceptable. However, given the main elimination pathway is
hepatobiliary excretion, the review team recommends a hepatic impairment study be completed
as a postmarketing commitment.
Renal Impairment
Based on a mass balance study, renal excretion is not a major elimination pathway of tradipitant
and its metabolites. No dedicated study in renally impaired subjects has been conducted. Based
on the Applicant’s population PK analysis, the estimated apparent clearance (CL/F) was similar
between subjects with mild renal impairment (N=79) and normal renal function (N=366) while
23% lower in subjects with moderate renal impairment (N=11) compared to subjects with normal
renal function. However, this difference is not considered clinically significant. Tradipitant was
not studied in subjects with severe renal impairment. The review team recommends a severe
renal impairment study be completed as a postmarketing commitment.
Age, Race, and Sex
In clinical trials supporting motion sickness indication, the impact of age, race and sex on
pharmacokinetics of tradipitant and its metabolites was not assessed.
CYP3A4*22 Genotype
Tradipitant undergoes extensive metabolism through several pathways including CYP3A4. The
Applicant evaluated the impact of CYP3A4*22 (a decreased function allele, as reviewed in Pratt
et al. (2023)) on the pharmacokinetics of tradipitant and its metabolites. A total of 57%
(292/515) of subjects in Trial 3403 and 48% (82/170) of subjects in Trial 3404 had genotyping
data available. Because of the large fraction of subjects with missing data, the Applicant did not
include CYP3A4 genotype into the population PK models. Therefore, the impact of CYP3A4*22
genotype on tradipitant pharmacokinetics remains unclear.

84
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5720419

NDA 220152
NEREUS (tradipitant)

8.2. Extrinsic Factors
Food Effect
Tradipitant should be administered without food.
In Trial 1002, when a single oral dose of 85-mg tradipitant was administered in healthy subjects
within 30 minutes of consumption of a high-fat high-calorie meal, tradipitant Cmax and AUC0-inf
was increased by 4.74-fold and 2.63-fold, respectively, and the median time to maximum
concentration (Tmax) was delayed by 2 hours (from 2 hours to 4 hours) as compared to tradipitant
administered under the fasted condition.
In the same study, following administration of a single oral dose of 170 mg of tradipitant under
similar fed conditions, tradipitant Cmax and AUC0-inf was increased by 7.03-fold and 3.81-fold,
respectively, and the median Tmax was delayed by 2.5 hours (from 1.5 hours to 4 hours)
compared to the fasted state. For the active metabolites M2, M3, M4, and M8, a high-fat highcalorie meal increased the Cmax by 4.08, 3.70, 3.40, 4.29-fold, respectively, and increased
AUC0-inf by up to 2.39, 2.64, 2.39, 2.73-fold, respectively, as compared to the fasted condition
for the single 85-mg dose arm. On the other hand, for the single 170-mg dose arm, for the active
metabolites M2, M3, M4, and M8, a high-fat high-calorie meal increased the Cmax by 6.54, 5.26,
5.47, 5.6-fold, respectively, and increased AUC0-inf by up to 3.5, 3.56, 3.2, 3.53-fold,
respectively, compared to the fasted condition.
In the phase 2 and 3 randomized controlled clinical trials supporting the indication of prevention
of vomiting induced by motion, subjects were instructed to take tradipitant approximately 60
minutes prior to boat travel on empty stomach.
Potential Effects of Other Drugs on Tradipitant
Tradipitant is extensively metabolized. The in vitro studies were conducted to identify the
enzymes responsible to form some known metabolites. These in vitro studies were reviewed
during NDA218489 review. No new in vitro drug interaction assessment was provided. The
Applicant’s in vitro studies showed non-CYP450-mediated processes (unspecified) and CYP3A4
were involved in tradipitant metabolism to M2 and M3, respectively and M8, a glucuronide
metabolite can be formed by uridine glucuronosyltransferase [UGT]1A4 and UGT2B7. Notably,
M8 is a more potent active metabolite with a 6.7-fold lower IC50 than tradipitant. However, the
relative contribution of a specific metabolizing enzyme and potential contribution of other CYP
enzymes to overall tradipitant metabolism have not been fully characterized. In vitro studies to
measure disappearance tradipitant in presence of recombinant CYP enzymes and/or inhibitors of
a specific CYP enzyme can provide such information. Therefore, it is recommended that the
Applicant conducts additional in vitro assessment as a post-marketing commitment to evaluate
the potential roles of other CYP enzymes and the relative contribution of specific CYP enzymes
to inform the major metabolizing enzymes of tradipitant and its active metabolites and assess the
potential for metabolism-mediated drug interactions. Based on the in vitro study results the need
for clinical drug- drug interaction studies should be determined.

85
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5720419

NDA 220152
NEREUS (tradipitant)
Potential Effects of Tradipitant on Other Drugs
Although tradipitant inhibits and induces CYP3A4 in vitro, when coadministered with multiple
doses of tradipitant 85 mg BID, oral midazolam (a sensitive CYP3A substrate) exposure (Cmax
and AUCinf) increased up to 16% as compared to when midazolam was administered alone. The
change of the systemic exposure for midazolam is unlikely to be clinically significant. This in
vivo drug interaction study was also reviewed under NDA 218489.

8.3. Plans for Pediatric Drug Development
An Agreed Initial Pediatric Study Plan letter was issued December 23, 2024, for the proposed
deferral of pediatric studies until additional safety and effectiveness data from adult studies have
been collected, as well as for a partial waiver for patients less than 2 years of age. Motion
sickness symptoms, including vomiting induced by motion, are very uncommon in individuals
less than 2 years of age, which would make the necessary studies impossible or highly
impracticable, and thus warrants a partial waiver for assessment of tradipitant in this age group.
(b) (4)

These plans for pediatric drug development of tradipitant were determined to be acceptable, and
were used to develop post-marketing requirements (PMRs) further described in Section 24.

8.4. Pregnancy, Lactation, and Females/Males of
Reproductive Potential
Pregnancy
There are limited human data available from clinical trials during drug development related to
pregnancy, which are insufficient to inform the risk of use during pregnancy. Based on animal
reproduction studies, oral administration of tradipitant did not demonstrate developmental
toxicity in rats or rabbits. The Division of Pediatrics and Maternal Health (DPMH) recommends
including information about the available clinical data under the Risk Summary and the
nonclinical data under the Risk Summary and Animal Data sections.
DPMH recommends issuing a pregnancy exposure registry and complementary database study as
PMRs for tradipitant based on following rationale: (1) the drug is systemically absorbed and will
likely transfer to the fetus, (2) motion sickness is common in females of reproductive potential,

86
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5720419

NDA 220152
NEREUS (tradipitant)
(3) motion sickness may be made worse during pregnancy, and (4) there are no human
pregnancy data available. See Section 24.
Lactation
Tradipitant is present in rat milk. DPMH recommends a statement in labeling about the presence
of tradipitant in animal milk. There are no available lactation data in humans. DPMH
recommends stating in labeling that there are no available data about the presence of the
proposed drug in human milk, its effects on milk product or its effects on the breastfed infant.
Due to the potential for breastfed infants to experience adverse reactions due to exposure to
tradipitant through breast milk, DPMH recommends including a statement in labeling that
breastfed infants should be monitored for adverse reactions that occur in adults, such as
somnolence.
DPMH recommends that a PMR be issued for a clinical lactation study because tradipitant is
expected to be used by females of reproductive potential, including lactating women. Based on
animal data and drug characteristics, tradipitant is likely to be transferred to human milk. A
clinical lactation study is needed to assess the degree to which tradipitant is transferred to human
milk and to assess the potential effects on the breastfed infant. See Section 24.
Females and Males of Reproductive Potential
DPMH recommends omitting subsection 8.3 as there is no information on the drug’s effects on
human fertility or contraception to relay to the healthcare provider.

9. Product Quality
The applicant of this 505(b)(1) new drug application has provided sufficient CMC information to
assure the identity, strength, purity, and quality of the drug substance, tradipitant and the drug
product, NEREUS (tradipitant) Capsules, 85 mg at release and throughout its proposed
expiration dating period of 48 months. The proposed expiration dating period is supported by the
stability data provided in the application. Therefore, this application is recommended for
approval from the OPQ perspective with an expiration dating period of 48 months.

Considerations

III. Additional Analyses and Information
12. Summary of Regulatory History
On October 2, 2018, IND 141315 was submitted by Vanda Pharmaceuticals Inc, the Applicant,
for the development of VLY-686 (tradipitant),38 an orally administered NK-1 receptor antagonist,
for motion sickness. IND 141315 was opened with Trial VP-VLY-686-2401 (Trial 2401),
“Motion Sifnos: A Randomized, Double-Blind, Placebo-Controlled Study To Investigate The
Efficacy Of Tradipitant In Subjects Affected by Motion Sickness During Vehicle Travel.” The
trial was found safe to proceed on November 1, 2018, following the Applicant’s agreement to
limit enrollment to subjects with normal baseline liver biochemistry results and the addition of
trial-stopping criteria.
On December 19, 2018, the Division issued an advice/information request letter regarding
Trial 2401. Specifically, the Division provided the following comments and recommendations:

The Division sought clarification from the Applicant whether they intend to seek an
