elastomeric connectors (1 mm wide by 18.2 mm long by 9mmtall) were incorporated
into each system to create an interface between the MEA and a commercially available
60-electrode amplifier. A stimulus generator was used to stimulate the cells (800 mV
rectangular pulse, 0.5 to 3.0 Hz in 0.25 Hz increments). The multichannel systems
software suite was used to control both the amplifier and stimulator and was used to
record action potentials (APs).
In this study, cardiomyocyte function was evaluated based on cardiac force, beat
frequency, and conduction velocity. As previously described,20 cardiac velocity and
spontaneous beat frequency were extracted from the data using Clampfit (Axon
Instruments). Spontaneous beat frequency was obtained from spontaneous recordings
of cardiac activity 30 s in length. Stimulated conduction was initiated by stimulation
using 40 ms pulses at 1 Hz for 15 s. For each recording, the frequency was calculated
as the number of measured APs divided by the length of time between the first and last
APs during the recording interval. Cardiac velocity was measured by determining the
length of time for an AP to travel from an electrically stimulated electrode along the
patterned cardiomyocyte path, following the method described previously.22
Proximal Tubular Epithelial Cells
(b) (4)
Human primary proximal tubular epithelial cells were purchased from
No information on the donor
characteristics was included in the vendor-provided COA. Human kidney tissue was
obtained from specimens obtained during surgical resection of renal cell carcinoma.
Renal proximal tubule epithelial cells were grown in monolayer cultures following
isolation from renal cortical tissue. Microfluidic technology allowed media perfusion,
which exposed cells to fluid shear force at the apical surface and delivered nutrients
continuously under normoxic conditions.29 Renal proximal tubule epithelial cells were
confirmed to have γ-glutamyl transferase activity.
In this study, renal proximal tubule cell function was evaluated based on kidney-injury
molecule (KIM)-1 release. KIM-1 protein in the supernatant was quantified by ELISA.
Cortical Neurons
The iPSC cell line utilized for cortical neuron differentiation was purchased from the
(b) (4)

iPSCs were generated by
four-factor (OCT4, SOX2, KLF4, CMYC) retroviral vector-mediated reprogramming from
fibroblasts derived from a healthy, 64-year-old male donor.
Differentiation of cortical neurons from iPSCs consisted of a timeline of three stages,
starting out with the use of small molecules, and ending with an array of neuron-specific
factors. iPSCs were grown to 90% confluence in the manufacturer-specified culture
29 Adler M, Ramm S, Hafner M, Muhlich JL, Gottwald EM, Weber E, Jaklic A, Ajay AK, Svoboda D,

Auerbach S, Kelly EJ, Himmelfarb J, Vaidya VS. A Quantitative Approach to Screen for Nephrotoxic

80
Reference ID: 5719891

NDA # 220152

Sarah J. Morgan, PhD

medium on a surface coated with matrigel matrix. To induce the differentiation, cells
received a full medium change and were then fed every other day with differentiation
medium I, differentiation medium II, and differentiation medium III, according to the
differentiation timeline.
In this study, cortical neuron function was evaluated based on half-peak width activity,
spike amplitude, and firing rate as previously described.30 Whole-cell patch clamp
recordings were performed on the 37°C heated stage of an upright microscope. The
intracellular recording solution (140 mM potassium gluconate, 4 mM NaCI, 0.5 mM
CaCI2, 1 mM MgCI2, 1 mM EGTA, 5 mM HEPES acid, 5 mM HEPES base, and 5 mM
Na2ATP) was loaded into borosilicate glass patch pipettes produced with a pipette
puller. Patch pipettes with a resistance in the range of 6–10 MΩ were used for all
recordings. Voltage-clamp and current-clamp experiments were performed with a
Multiclamp 700B amplifier. Signals were filtered at 2 kHz and digitized at 20 kHz with an
Axon Digidata 1322A interface. Offset potentials were nulled before formation of a
gigaΩ seal and fast and slow capacitance was compensated for in all recordings.
Membrane potentials were corrected by subtraction of a 15 mV tip potential, calculated
using Axon’s pCIamp 10 software (Axon Instrument, Foster City, CA, USA). Recordings
with an access resistance of less than 22 MΩ were not included as a high access
resistance indicates poor patch quality/insufficient access to the interior of the cell. To
generate a single AP, a 3 ms depolarizing current pulse of sufficient intensity (1-2 nA)
was applied. Inward and outward currents were evoked by a series of 250 ms
depolarizing steps from −70 to +170 mV with +10 mV increments. Depolarizationevoked repetitive firing was recorded in current clamp mode. Gapfree recordings of
spontaneous activity of patched neurons were performed for up to 5 m while holding the
cell membrane at optimal threshold (between −45 and −50 mV). Cells which required
more than 200 pA of current to maintain voltage clamp were excluded as excessive
application of current is indicative of poor patch quality or membrane integrity. All
recordings and analysis were performed using the pCIamp 10 software suite. Action
potential rise times were calculated as the time taken to reach 90% maximum AP
amplitude from 10% of the maximum amplitude. Decay slope and time were calculated
from 90% to 10% of the action potential. Full width at half maximum was calculated as
the width of the action potential (in ms) at half the maximum amplitude. Action potential
rise time and FWHM were calculated during the flattest portion of the AP to allow for
easy comparison between conditions.

Results
Metabolism of Tradipitant in Acellular and Liver Systems
Due to the lipophilicity of tradipitant, it was necessary to evaluate the maximum
solubility of the compound in the system as well as the amount absorbed onto the
PDMS device material. The targeted “plasma” concentrations of tradipitant were 500
and 5000 ng/mL. However, a concentration of 5000 ng/mL could not be consistently
achieved due to the high lipophilicity of the drug (data not provided), and the maximum
targeted concentration was reduced to 3500 ng/mL. The dosing concentration of
tradipitant that resulted in sustained “plasma” concentrations of 500 ng/ml and 3500
ng/ml were 3 μM and 100 μM, respectively.
Tradipitant was stable over a 7-day period in acellular systems. Metabolism of
tradipitant in hepatocytes from Donor 1 resulted in 7.2% parent drug, 91.6% M2, 0.1%
M3, 1.2% M4 after 24 hours.

Reviewer note: The drug and metabolite pharmacokinetic profiles measured in this in
vitro study (using either Donor 1 or Donor 2) are not consistent with human clinical or in
vivo animal study data. Following a single dose of 25 mg radiolabeled tradipitant in
healthy human volunteers, drug-related exposure measured over 24 hours was 9%
parent drug, 4% M2, 5% M3, and 8% M4 (i.e., the parent drug and major identified
human metabolites accounted for only 26% of drug-related exposure; see Trial H8RMC-HKAK and 2.4 Nonclinical Overview, pg. 20). In healthy volunteers administered 85
mg tradipitant BID for 12 days, drug-related exposure measured over 24 hours was
84
Reference ID: 5719891

NDA # 220152

Sarah J. Morgan, PhD

composed of 50% parent drug, 17% M2, 16% M3, and 17% M4 (see Trial VP-VLY-6861102, pgs. 60-63). Additionally, although data are limited, M2 represented < 1% of drugrelated exposure in nonclinical toxicology studies. Overall, cells in the MPS were
overexposed to M2 and underexposed to tradipitant and/or its other major metabolites
during this study compared to what has been observed in animals and humans. Due to
the differences in in vitro and in vivo metabolism, the clinical relevance of any findings
related to toxicity in this assay is unclear.
Tissue-Specific Endpoints
The study report states that no significant effects of tradipitant were observed on
selected measures of hepatocyte function (albumin production, CYP3A4 activity, liver
cell viability), cardiomyocyte function (cardiac force, cardiac beat frequency, and cardiac
conduction velocity), kidney injury (KIM-1, a proposed biomarker of acute kidney
injury31), or cortical neuron function (half-peak width, spike amplitude, and firing rate)
through 7 days of treatment. In light of the limited scope of the toxicity evaluation
(including lack of scientific justification for the specific parameters tested), observed
differences in metabolism, lack of positive controls, and lack of compliance with US FDA
GLP regulations, we disagree that any conclusions can be made based on the
information available in this study report.

10.2 HSTOX 2008-002: Safety Evaluation of Supraphysiological Tradipitant Dosing
under Differential Liver Metabolism
HSTOX 2008-002

Methods
(b) (4) multiThe potential toxic effects of tradipitant were evaluated using
organ MPS. This custom, human-based multi-organ platform was composed of liver
(primary biopsy), cardiac (induced pluripotent stem cell [iPSC]-derived), kidney (primary
biopsy), and neuronal (iPSC-derived) “organ” modules and was referred to as the “fourorgan system.”

Based on the unexpected drug metabolism observed in Study HSTOX-7619 (Donor 1,
(b) (4)
purchased from the
this study used multiple donors for the liver construct in order
to evaluate the effects of differences in metabolism on the potential toxicity of

88
Reference ID: 5719891

NDA # 220152

Sarah J. Morgan, PhD

tradipitant. Three donors were identified as having low, moderate, and high levels of
metabolic activity, as defined by CYP3A4 activity, respectively (see Results).
All cell types utilized in this study were cultured independently to allow for complete
maturation prior to integration into the four-organ system. Following assembly, systems
were maintained for 3 days before the start of the experiment.
To evaluate the toxicity of tradipitant, the system was treated once daily for 14 days with
vehicle or tradipitant (Lot # RS-FP-VDDE21019) to achieve a final concentration of 3,
30, or 100 µM. On Days 1-13, systems received daily medium changes amounting to
30% of the total system volume, with the new medium containing the appropriate
concentration of tradipitant to maintain target concentrations in the full system.
Concentrations of 3 and 100 µM were previously shown to achieve “plasma” exposures
of approximately 500 and 3500 ng/mL, respectively (see Study HSTOX-7619). DMSO
(0.1%) was used as the vehicle/negative control, and two positive controls were
included for each endpoint (see results below).
Reviewer note: Based on the information provided by the Applicant, Solubility issues
were observed at the highest administered concentration of 100 M tradipitant, as the
compound precipitated out of solution when added to culture medium. The degree of
precipitation was not quantified.
Reviewer note: As stated previously, the low and high concentrations (plasma
exposures) used in this study represent tradipitant exposure at approximately 1.5- and
11-fold the clinical Cmax of 327 ng/mL, respectively, as determined in healthy volunteers
following 12 days of dosing at 85 mg BID. The Applicant did not conduct IVIVE analyses
to determine how the in vitro concentration relates to that observed in humans. Thus,
the stated effect margins should be interpreted with caution, and all observed effects
are discussed in terms of absolute concentration.
Unless otherwise noted, endpoints evaluating “organ” function were measured pretreatment (with tradipitant being added to the culture immediately after baseline testing)
and following 1, 4, 7, 10, and 14 days of treatment. In addition to organ-specific
endpoints, the quantities of tradipitant, M2, M3, and M4 were measured in medium
collected from systems on Days 1, 4, 7, 10, and 14 post-dose. On Day 14, the cells
were lysed and the quantities of tradipitant, M2, M3, and M4 in both media and cell
lysate samples were measured by UHPLC-MS/MS.
Unless otherwise stated, each condition was tested in duplicate per experiment, and
four biological replicates each were used for the low, moderate, and high metabolizing
donors. Two-way ANOVA/Fisher’s LSD was utilized to compare vehicle control to the
various tradipitant concentrations for each four-organ system with either the high,
medium, or low metabolizing liver construct.

measured using a commercially available ELISA (Thermo Fisher Scientific: Waltham,

Chlorpromazine and losartan were used as positive controls for measurement of
albumin production. Chlorpromazine is a phenothiazine that can cause mild and
transient serum enzyme elevations and is a known cause of clinically apparent acute
and chronic cholestatic liver injury. Chlorpromazine administered orally at 75 mg/day for
21 days is associated with compound-induced liver injury with a latency of 2 weeks.32
The chlorpromazine concentration used in this study, 0.5 µM (the clinical Cmax), was
selected to recapitulate the clinically observed liver injury. Losartan is an angiotensin 1
(AT1) blocker prescribed for the treatment of hypertension that has been reported to
induce mild hepatotoxicity including marked elevation of aminotransferases.33 The
losartan concentration used in this study, 300 ng/mL, was selected based on the high
end of the range of clinical Cmax,ss values following the recommended dosing regimen.

concentration.43 Nimodipine is known to have an inhibitory effect on neuronal activity.

concentration-response curve, designed using a Cmax of 1 µM, over a 14-day
longitudinal study) as this concentration produced a significant inhibitory effect by Day 4
of treatment.44

GLP Compliance
No information on GLP compliance was provided in this study report. Based on the
available information,45 it appears that this study was not conducted in compliance with

Results
Reviewer note: Although not included in the figures below, statistical testing of all
treated conditions compared to the vehicle control was provided by the Sponsor. P
values can be viewed in the full study report. For the purposes of this review, all p
values < 0.05 were considered statistically significant.
Metabolism of Tradipitant in the Four-Organ System
Following 14 days of treatment with tradipitant at 3 µM, no difference in the media levels
of tradipitant was observed between the low, moderate, and high metabolizer systems.
Metabolites M2, M3, and M4 were undetectable at this concentration.
At 30 µM, no difference in the media levels of tradipitant was observed between the low,
moderate, and high metabolizer systems. Tradipitant levels were approximately 5-fold
higher at 30 µM compared to 3 µM, despite the 10-fold difference in concentration.
“Low” levels of metabolite M2 were observed at this concentration, similarly with no
difference between the low, moderate, and high metabolizer systems. M3 and M4 were
undetectable at this concentration.
At 100 µM, tradipitant levels were highest in the moderate metabolizer system, followed
by the low metabolizer system; levels in the high metabolizer system were notably lower
than the other two systems. Tradipitant levels were approximately 2- and 3-fold higher
in the low and moderate metabolizer systems at 100 µM compared to 30 µM for a 3.3fold increase in concentration, while levels in the high metabolizer system were lower (a
small fraction) at 100 µM compared to 30 µM. Levels of M2 were “low” compared to the
parent drug but followed a similar pattern in that they were significantly higher in the
moderate metabolizer system compared to the high metabolizer system. M3 and M4
were undetectable at this concentration.

Hepatocytes
Identification of Donors with High, Moderate, and Low CYP3A4 Levels
Various donor sources were investigated to identify physiological representations of
high, moderate, and low metabolizing hepatocytes, based on CYP3A4 activity.
(b) (4) were selected as the high metabolizer, and
(b) (4)
Hepatocytes from
hepatocytes from separate donors were selected as the moderate and low metabolizers
for further study.
Reviewer note: Based on nonclinical and clinical data submitted by the Applicant,
CYP3A4 has a role in the metabolism of tradipitant, but it is not the predominant or only
pathway responsible (see NDA Integrated Review, Sections 13 and 14, for review of
nonclinical and clinical data, respectively). Tradipitant is metabolized to M2 by nonCYP-mediated pathways; to M3 by CYP3A4 (major), CYP2C19 (minor), and CYP2C8
(minor); and to M8 by glucuronidation. M2 and M3 are further metabolized to M4 by
CYP-mediated and non-specified pathways, respectively. According to the current study
report, previous work (Study HSTOX-2008.001, not submitted to the Agency)
demonstrated that the M2 metabolite is “spontaneously generated” and not CYPdependent. Induction of CYP enzymes was observed following repeated dosing in rats
and dogs, and clinically relevant induction of CYP3A is possible but was not definitively
demonstrated. Apparent clearance of tradipitant was 33% lower in subjects with the
heterozygous CYP3A4*22 allele compared to wild type (CYP3A4*1), and the Applicant
suggests that CYP3A4*22 subjects may be at higher risk of adverse events; however,
the impact of this finding on human PK was of unclear clinical significance. Additionally,
no scientific justification or biological rationale was provided for the criteria used to
define a low, moderate, or high CYP3A4 donor, and it is not clear that these criteria
were pre-specified. Overall, the available data do not clearly demonstrate that
endogenous/baseline CYP3A4 activity has a significant impact on tradipitant
metabolism, and the clinical relevance of using donors with three different levels of
CYP3A4 activity in this study has not been justified.
Untreated hepatocytes (in isolated culture, not part of a four-organ system) displayed
healthy morphology over 14 days. CYP3A4 activity was maintained over the 14-day
study for all three donors; notably, no induction of CYP3A4 activity was observed. No
differences in albumin levels were observed between medium and low metabolizer
hepatocytes throughout the study. Albumin levels for the high metabolizer hepatocytes
were higher compared to other donors on Days 1 and 7; however, by Day 14, albumin
levels were similar for all donors.

Effects of Tradipitant on Viability in Hepatocytes from Different Donors
Hepatocytes in isolated culture were monitored over 14 days to assess changes in
morphology attributable to parent and metabolite exposure. Observations of excessive
vacuole formation or detachment of hepatocytes resulting in an inconsistent monolayer
were considered evidence of toxicity.
No morphological evidence of toxicity was observed in low, moderate, or high
metabolizer hepatocytes at a tradipitant concentration of 3 µM during the 14-day study
when compared to vehicle control cells. At 100 µM, vacuoles were observed in low and
moderate metabolizer hepatocytes (see red arrows in Figure 41), but these findings
were similar to those observed in vehicle controls. However, substantial cell detachment
(see blue arrows) was observed in the high metabolizer hepatocytes at Day 14,
indicating toxicity with tradipitant treatment. Toxicity under these conditions may be
attributable to prolonged exposure to metabolite(s) and/or metabolite byproduct(s) that
98
Reference ID: 5719891

NDA # 220152

Sarah J. Morgan, PhD

were not present or were present at lower levels in the low and moderate metabolizer
hepatocytes; however, no direct association was suggested by the Applicant.

Reference ID: 5719891

Over the course of 14 days, treatment with tradipitant at 3 µM resulted in some
accumulation of the parent drug and metabolites in hepatocytes from all donors, though
variability prevents any firm conclusions of metabolic patterns between the different
metabolizing conditions at this concentration. The final concentration of tradipitant on
Day 14 was loosely inversely related to the level of CYP3A4 activity, although the
absolute differences were small. Production of M2 over the course of the study was
highest in the moderate metabolizer hepatocytes, followed by the low and lastly the high
metabolizers. Production of M3 was below the limit of detection (not provided) in all
donors on Day 1 but detectable thereafter, with the highest levels observed in the
moderate metabolizer hepatocytes followed by the low metabolizer hepatocytes; M3
remained undetectable in the high metabolizer hepatocytes throughout the study.
Production of M4 was below the limit of detection (not provided) in all hepatocytes on
Day 1 but detectable thereafter. M4 levels were similar in the low and moderate
metabolizer hepatocytes, while the high metabolizer hepatocytes had similar levels as
other donors on Days 4 and 7 but higher levels on Days 10 and 14.
Treatment with tradipitant at 100 µM resulted in relatively stable levels of parent drug in
low and moderate metabolizer hepatocytes over 14 days but lower exposure in high
metabolizer hepatocytes on Day 14 compared to Day 1. The final concentration of
tradipitant on Day 14 was loosely inversely related to the level of CYP3A4 activity,
although the absolute differences were small. Production of M2 decreased over the
course of the study, with levels similar among the three different metabolizers on Day
14. Production of M3 was below the limit of detection (not stated) in all donors at all
timepoints. Production of M4 was highest in the moderate metabolizer hepatocytes, with
lower levels observed in the low and high metabolizers.
Overall, quantified levels of M2 were similar to or higher than tradipitant in all donors.
Levels of M3 relative to tradipitant were lower (10% to 20% those of tradipitant at 3 µM
and undetectable at 100 µM) and were not clearly related to CYP3A4 activity. Levels of
M4 relative to tradipitant were highly variable (undetectable to approximately 40% at 3
µM and undetectable up to approximately 20% at 100 µM).
Reviewer note: As discussed for Study HSTOX-7619, the drug and metabolite
pharmacokinetic profiles measured in this in vitro study are not consistent with human
clinical or in vivo animal study data. Following a single dose of 25 mg radiolabeled
tradipitant in healthy human volunteers, drug-related exposure measured over 24 hours
was 9% parent drug, 4% M2, 5% M3, and 8% M4 (i.e., the parent drug and major
identified human metabolites accounted for only 26% of drug-related exposure; see
Study H8R-MC-HKAK and 2.4 Nonclinical Overview, pg. 20). In healthy volunteers
administered 85 mg tradipitant BID for 12 days, drug-related exposure measured over
24 hours was composed of 50% parent drug, 17% M2, 16% M3, and 17% M4 (see
Study VP-VLY-686-1102, pgs. 60-63). Additionally, although data are limited, M2
represented < 1% of drug-related exposure in nonclinical toxicology studies. Due to the
differences in in vitro and in vivo metabolism, any findings related to toxicity in this
assay may not be biologically relevant to humans. Finally, these results call into
question the role and importance of CYP3A4 in the metabolism of tradipitant. As stated

101
Reference ID: 5719891

NDA # 220152

Sarah J. Morgan, PhD

above, CYP3A4 has been identified as the major pathway of metabolism of tradipitant to
M3, with little or no additional effects; however, the production of M3 was not highest in
the high metabolizer hepatocytes, as would be expected. An alternative explanation
could be that increased production of M3 led to further conversion of M3 to M4 (by nonCYP-mediated pathways); however, again, levels of M4 were lowest in the high
metabolizer hepatocytes, contrary to that expected. While it is clear that differences in
metabolism were present among the three donors in this study, it is not clear what the
biologically significant differences were (e.g., differences in baseline or induced
activities of other CYP enzymes, differences in non-CYP pathways) or how such
differences may be clinically relevant.

Effects of Tradipitant Treatment on Hepatocytes as Part of the Four-Organ System
No significant effect of tradipitant treatment was observed on albumin production in the
four-organ system on Days 1 and 7. On Day 14, significantly increased albumin
production was observed at 3 µM in the moderate metabolizer system only compared to
control, with no significant effects observed under other conditions. No significant
effects of losartan or chlorpromazine were observed on albumin production at any
timepoint.

Cardiomyocytes
Positive controls terfenadine and doxorubicin produced substantial toxicity over the 14day study, resulting in non-viable cultures and, thus, limited datasets at later timepoints.
Results with these compounds should be interpreted with caution, especially at later
timepoints.
No significant effect of tradipitant treatment was observed on troponin-I levels on Day 1.
On Day 7, significantly increased troponin-I was observed with tradipitant at 100 µM in
the high metabolizer system compared to control, but no effects were observed with
tradipitant at 100 µM in the low or moderate metabolizer systems or in any system with
tradipitant at 3 or 30 µM. On Day 14, significantly increased troponin-I was observed
with tradipitant at 3 µM in the low metabolizer system and at 100 µM in the high
metabolizer system, but not for the other conditions. Significantly increased troponin-I
was observed with both terfenadine and doxorubicin on Day 7 but not on Day 1 or Day
14, with the latter likely due to the observed high level of cytotoxicity rather than
recovery.
Reviewer note: As previously stated, it is not clear which hepatocytes were used for the
experiments with positive controls. As terfenadine metabolism to the non-cardiotoxic
metabolite fexofenadine is primarily via CYP3A4, it is expected that treatment with
terfenadine would have different toxic effects on cardiac function between low,
moderate, and high metabolizer systems. As terfenadine did produce cardiac toxicity in
this assay, this positive control is generally considered sufficient to demonstrate the
104
Reference ID: 5719891

NDA # 220152

Sarah J. Morgan, PhD

validity of the assay (at least through Day 7, before excessive cytotoxicity was
observed). However, demonstrable differences in terfenadine toxicity via troponin-I
levels with the different metabolizer systems would have provided additional validation
of the differences in CYP3A4 activity between donors and demonstrated that this could
result in differences in drug toxicity.

11 Other Toxicology Studies
11.1 Toxicology Assessment of Tradipitant and Four Corresponding Metabolites
Performed via the TEST, ILab2, and ToxTree Software and augmented by analog
searches from the DSSTox, SuperToxic and ACToR databases – 2015-01-30
In Silico Toxicologic Assessment
Methods
Tradipitant and metabolites M2, M3, M4, and M8 were analyzed for potential toxicologic
properties using in silico methods. Tools and databases used in this analysis included
TEST (Toxicity Estimation Software Tool), ILab2, ToxTree, DSSTox, SuperToxic, and
ACToR. Various toxicological properties were assessed, including the potential for
acute toxicity (LD50 in rats), mutagenicity, carcinogenicity, developmental toxicity, hERG
inhibition, as well as skin and eye irritation.
Results
Tradipitant and its metabolites raised a number of toxicological concerns in the analysis,
including potential mutagenicity and inhibition of the hERG channel current. The
multiple polarized aryl groups in tradipitant, most notably the 3-pyridinyl group, were
flagged as potentially reactive and may result in unexpected toxicological effects.
However, the analysis concluded that tradipitant and its metabolites were considered
overall to have “a low net toxicity risk.”
Reviewer note: Although this assessment was conducted in 2015, this report was
submitted for the first time in the current NDA.46 The review team requested the study
report,1 and the report was submitted in response to the IR during the review cycle
46 Discussed in Modules 2.4 Nonclinical Overview, 2.6.6 Toxicology Written Summary, and 2.6.7

Toxicology Tabulated Summary.

108
Reference ID: 5719891

NDA # 220152

Sarah J. Morgan, PhD

(SDN 9: March 25, 2025). While the broad in silico analysis conducted here is useful for
highlighting potential toxicity risks for further analysis, it is important to confirm (or
refute) such risks experimentally. In light of the completed in vitro and in vivo toxicity
studies with tradipitant and/or its metabolites, the findings in this report are not
considered toxicologically relevant.
11.2 Chemical and structural analysis of tradipitant and its metabolites M2, M3, and M4
Response to Non-Clinical Information Request dated 01 October 2025
Methods
Updated in silico analyses were conducted by the Applicant to support the safety of
metabolites M2, M3, and M4 for this application.47,48,49,50 These analyses were intended
to assess if and how structural differences in the metabolites might affect their toxicity
profile compared to tradipitant. Briefly, these in silico analyses were conducted using a
machine learning-assisted workflow developed by xAI's Grok system, an advanced
large language model trained on scientific datasets and further validated via the
Applicant’s in-house Python scripts. Notably, the Applicant’s comparative structural
prediction data were inferred based on RDKit-computed descriptors and qualitative
thresholds aligned with best practices from consensus classifiers, rather than direct
execution of these models. It is not clear why the Applicant chose to design a workflow
using a large language model and a custom set of molecular descriptors and use
information from existing classifier techniques to ‘infer’ predictions, rather than directly
using existing (Q)SAR modeling software. Additionally, insufficient detail was provided
on the consensus classifiers used in the analyses to fully define the approach. However,
enough information was provided to facilitate review of the findings.
Results
For metabolite M2, no additional reactive groups were identified compared to tradipitant,
and no significant differences between tradipitant and M2 were predicted for PK
parameters, acute toxicity, mutagenicity, carcinogenicity, aquatic toxicity, or skin
sensitization. The potential for tradipitant and M2 to cause nephrotoxicity and
neurotoxicity was also considered comparable and low. M2 was predicted to have
slightly increased risks of hepatotoxicity and cardiotoxicity compared to tradipitant.
Metabolite M3 was shown to have structural alerts for oxidative stress, hepatotoxicity,
and genotoxicity related to the N-oxide moiety and increased covalent binding risk (e.g.,
sensitization and hepatotoxicity) related to the alpha, beta-unsaturated ketone moiety
that are not present in tradipitant. Compared to tradipitant, M3 had no significant
differences predicted for PK parameters, acute toxicity, mutagenicity, carcinogenicity,
aquatic toxicity, or skin sensitization. The potential for tradipitant and M3 to cause
nephrotoxicity and neurotoxicity was also considered comparable and low. M3 was

48 NDA 220152, SDN 23: October 15, 2025.
49 NDA 220152: Information Request. October 20, 2025.
50 NDA 220152, SDN 24: October 22, 2025.

Sarah J. Morgan, PhD

predicted to have slightly increased risk of oxidative stress, hepatotoxicity, and
cardiotoxicity compared to tradipitant.
For metabolite M4 compared to tradipitant, no significant differences were predicted for
PK parameters, acute toxicity, mutagenicity, carcinogenicity, aquatic toxicity, or skin
sensitization. The potential for tradipitant and M4 to cause nephrotoxicity and
neurotoxicity was also considered comparable and low. Compared to tradipitant, M4
was predicted to have slightly increased risk of oxidative stress, hepatotoxicity, and

Overall, the updated in silico analyses predicted that metabolites M2, M3, and M4 were
unlikely to present additional toxicity concerns compared with tradipitant. The review

for this intended use. However, in conjunction with the internally conducted (Q)SAR
analysis and following consideration of all available data (see discussion in Section
13.3.2.5 of the Integrated Review for this NDA), we generally agree with the Applicant’s
stated position.

12 Nonclinical Discussion
12.1 Assessment of Nonclinical Data to Support the Safety of Unrestricted,
Lifetime Use of Tradipitant
12.1.1 Background of the Nonclinical Program for Tradipitant
A detailed account of the regulatory history of this application is provided in the IRT for
NDA 220152. The below discussion is intended to provide the context necessary to
understand the scientific and regulatory issues that impacted the nonclinical
assessment of this NDA. As such, this background should not be considered
exhaustive.
The Applicant submitted NDA 220152 for tradipitant for the acute prevention of vomiting
induced by motion in adults on December 30, 2024. Although tradipitant will be used
intermittently51 for this indication, clinical data with tradipitant in Trial 3403 have
demonstrated that a subset of individuals are likely to use the product frequently (see
IRT Section 7.6.2.1 for a detailed presentation of the clinical data). Additionally, the
relatively long half-life of tradipitant and its major human metabolites (28+ hours)
supports that even single doses spaced days apart could lead to sustained exposure to
and even accumulation of drug-related material. Therefore, long-term nonclinical data
are warranted to support the safety of unrestricted use of tradipitant over a lifetime.
Completed repeat-dose toxicity studies with tradipitant include studies of up to 3 months
in mice, 6 months in rats, and 3 months in dogs. During clinical development, the review
team recommended that the Applicant conduct a chronic (i.e., ≥6-month) toxicity study
in a nonrodent species to adequately assess the risk of cumulative toxicity following
repeated short-term exposures to tradipitant and, thus, support the safety of tradipitant
for the indication of prevention of vomiting induced by motion.52 This is in line with
recommendations for some other drugs with repeated, short-term exposure (e.g.,
products for migraine, herpes simplex). At the time of this review, the Applicant has not
conducted a chronic toxicity study with tradipitant in a nonrodent species to assess the
risk of cumulative toxicity following repeated short-term exposures to tradipitant.
Additionally, the Applicant had not provided adequate scientific justification for why such
a study is not warranted to support the safety of tradipitant for the current indication.
The initial NDA submission and subsequent nonclinical amendments included reports of
24 nonclinical studies that had not previously been submitted to IND 141315 or crossreferenced from any other IND for tradipitant.53 Seven of these reports were of
51 Although vomiting induced by motion will resolve after exposure to motion ceases, such episodes are

expected to recur once the motion stimulus is reintroduced. Based on clinical knowledge about this
indication and the Applicant’s protocol for Trial VP-VLY-686-3403 for this intended use, tradipitant is
expected to be used repeatedly over a lifetime.
52 IND 141315: Advice Letter. February 28, 2023.
(b) (4)

Reference ID: 5719891

Sarah J. Morgan, PhD

3560, and HPTOX-3540, decreased hepatocyte cell viability was observed following
tradipitant treatment for 2 or 4 weeks at concentrations as low as 1.05 μg/mL but more
consistently in the range of 3.5-7.0 μg/mL, with no clear concentration- or timedependent effects reported. Additionally, no primary mechanism for the observed
hepatocyte toxicity was identified in these studies, although the potential for tradipitant
to induce cholestasis was noted when hepatocyte spheroids were co-cultured with bile
acids. In Study HPTOX-3610, hepatocyte toxicity was observed following tradipitant
treatment for 2 weeks at concentrations as low as 0.350 µg/mL. Hepatocyte toxicity was
also observed with tradipitant metabolites M2 (at concentrations as low as 0.350
µg/mL), M3 (as low as 1.05 μg/mL but more commonly at 10.5 or 35 μg/mL), M4 (as low
as 0.350 µg/mL), and M8 (in 2/4 donors only, as low as 0.350 and 10.5 µg/mL,
respectively).
In the study using the liver co-culture MPS, qualitative findings of mild to moderate
hepatocyte toxicity were noted with tradipitant in all donors at the highest tested
concentration (26.3 µM or 15 µg/mL) starting at 72 hours, while no toxicity was
observed in LSECs with treatment up to 168 hours (7 days). Qualitative findings of
minimal to mild toxicity were also noted sporadically in hepatocytes and LSECs treated
with metabolites M2, M3, M4, and/or M8 at the latest timepoint of 168 hours. No clear
effects of tradipitant or its metabolites were observed on isolated endpoints
representative of liver function and injury including albumin, AST, ALT, ROS generation,
CCK-18 protein expression, or miRNA-122 expression. Although four positive controls
were used in this study, no explanation was given for their selection. At least one
positive control resulted in statistically significant changes for each chosen endpoint.
However, thresholds for biologically or clinically significant changes were not prespecified for each endpoint. Additionally, no potential mechanism for the observed
hepatocyte toxicity was identified.
These studies do not rule out the potential for tradipitant and/or its major metabolites to
cause liver injury, as previously observed in the 13-week study in Han Wistar rats, and
support that the potential risk is not animal-specific (i.e., it can also occur in human
cells). As mentioned previously, the stated objective of the submitted studies using
cultured 3D human hepatocyte spheroids or human liver co-culture MPS was to quantify
the in vitro hepatotoxic potential of tradipitant and to evaluate its underlying toxicity
mechanisms; this objective was not achieved. The Applicant did not identify a primary
mechanism for potential liver toxicity in these studies. Additionally, the Applicant did not
provide any validation to support the clinical relevance of the extent or durability of drug
exposure under these study conditions (i.e., how exposure following daily dose
administration relates to constant treatment or drug refreshment every few days in
culture). Furthermore, although the Applicant referred to the tested concentrations as
multiples of the clinical Cmax, this approach does not clearly define a threshold at which
the effects may be expected to be observed clinically. Indeed, IVIVE analyses should
have been conducted to determine how the in vitro concentration relates to that
observed in humans, as direct comparison of Cmax and in vitro concentrations
versus 7.0 µg/mL) in Study HPTOX-3610 compared to Study HPTOX-3550. The source of this variability
is unknown.

113
Reference ID: 5719891

NDA # 220152

Sarah J. Morgan, PhD

“oversimplifies the complex relationship between in vitro and in vivo drug behavior and
can lead to inaccurate conclusions.”7 Ultimately, even setting aside the identified
limitations, these short-duration experiments (up to 4 weeks in hepatocyte spheroids
and 7 days in the liver co-culture chip MPS) are inadequate to inform the potential risk
of liver injury with long-term use of tradipitant.
Four-Organ System to Address Potential Multi-Organ Toxicity
The Applicant also conducted two prospective toxicology studies using a “four-organ”
MPS comprised of human liver, cardiac, renal, and neuronal cells with the objective of
evaluating the safety of tradipitant.
Based on the stated objective, these studies are in vitro nonclinical laboratory studies
intended to support the safety and approval of tradipitant under this NDA. These studies
were not conducted in compliance with US FDA GLP regulations. The validity and
reliability of the results from nonclinical safety studies depend on careful adherence to
certain standards for study conduct that help to ensure the quality, integrity, and
reliability of the data, establishing confidence in the scientific merit of the study so that
accurate interpretation of the results is possible. We do not wholly discount the findings
of these studies based on their non-GLP status; however, caution should be applied in
the interpretation of findings.
Study HSTOX-7619: 7-Day Study of Tradipitant in the Four-Organ System
According to the Applicant, no effects of tradipitant were observed on selected
biomarkers of function for any of the four cell types following repeated treatment with
tradipitant for 7 days.
Numerous limitations in study design and conduct preclude its substantive contribution
to the long-term safety profile of tradipitant.
• The evaluation of potential toxicity using the four-organ system was limited in
scope (i.e., not comprehensive).
o The MPS used human-derived cells from the heart, liver, kidney, and
brain, a small subset of tissues compared to those present in a whole
animal or human. Furthermore, there is no rationale for the specific subset
of organs chosen for this study or how an investigation using only these
organs can evaluate toxicity within fully integrated physiological systems
(i.e., including any effects of metabolism, immune responses, and
hormones) to provide a broad, tissue-agnostic assessment of toxicity and
to inform safe use of a drug.
o Within an individual “organ,” only a single cell type was cultured and
exposed to tradipitant. A single cell type only represents a portion of an
organ and thus cannot adequately or comprehensively assess potential
toxicities within that organ, precluding a recapitulation of full in vivo
physiology and pathophysiology. For example:

Liver cell types: hepatocytes, cholangiocytes, liver natural killer

o This study evaluated a small number of endpoints (one to three factors per

toxicity in each tissue.
The biological relevance of the system and chosen endpoints to characterize the
safety of tradipitant was neither justified nor established.

(i.e., CYP activity), 2) were used as biomarkers of organ injury despite a
lack of validation or clinical acceptance (i.e., KIM-158), and/or 3) were
inconsistent with the results of other studies, including the in vitro liver

toxicity in this system using the chosen endpoints.

four-organ system was significantly different than that observed in humans
or in nonclinical species.

report or provided following an information request.

tissue type. Any findings in these assays may reflect the response of an
individual donor or cell preparation and may not be predictive of the general
population.

experiments do not provide data on the chronic toxicity of the drug.

Study HSTOX-2008-002: 14-Day Study of Tradipitant in the Four-Organ System Using
Hepatocytes with Different Metabolizer Phenotypes
Evidence of potential toxicity with tradipitant was observed under select conditions at
select timepoints in all four cell types tested: hepatocytes (decreased viability),
cardiomyocytes (increased troponin-I, decreased cardiac force, increased field potential
duration), renal proximal tubular epithelial cells (increased KIM-1 release), and cortical
neurons (increased firing rate). The Applicant concluded that “chronic” exposure of
tradipitant in the four-organ system yielded only minor adverse effects in all “organ”
constructs. When comparing the safety of tradipitant across systems containing either
high, moderate, or low metabolic activity (based only on CYP3A4 activity), significant
effects could be observed, suggesting that differences in hepatic expression of
metabolizing enzymes (e.g., polymorphisms leading to activity of metabolic enzymes
that is higher or lower than that seen in the general population) may influence
physiological outcomes with tradipitant, including outcomes related to safety.
Numerous limitations in study design and conduct preclude its substantive contribution
to the long-term safety profile of tradipitant. In general, these limitations are similar to
those identified for Study HSTOX-7619, and, for brevity, only general limitations will be
repeated.
• The evaluation of potential toxicity using the four-organ system was limited in
scope (i.e., not comprehensive).
• The biological relevance of the system and chosen endpoints to characterize the
safety of tradipitant was neither justified nor established.
o While two positive controls were used for each assay, the utility of the
resulting information was limited. Several of these compounds induced
cytotoxicity that was considered significant enough to confound
interpretation of the results (i.e., terfenadine, doxorubicin, gentamicin). In
other cases, the positive control did not demonstrate the expected toxicity
in the context of the four-organ system (i.e., losartan, chlorpromazine,
nimodipine).
o For each endpoint, no definition of a biologically relevant change was
provided. Thus, the toxicological significance of any observed change
cannot be reliably determined.
o As acknowledged by the Applicant, the metabolism of tradipitant in the
four-organ system was not as expected given previous findings in humans
and nonclinical species. Although the Applicant suggested possible
explanations for these deviations (i.e., metabolite is absorbed into the
housing material, the 14-day study was not long enough for complete
metabolite detection, non-hepatic metabolism of the compound in the
body occurs in organs that were not included in this study, or a potential
enzymatic feedback loop inhibited conversion of the parent compound), no
evidence for these possibilities was provided, and there was no discussion
on how such deviations impact the clinical translatability of the reported
findings.
o A major focus of this study was the impact of differing liver metabolism on
potential toxicity with tradipitant, but this approach was not justified.

Reference ID: 5719891

NDA # 220152

Sarah J. Morgan, PhD
▪

•

•

While CYP3A4 is one mechanism of tradipitant metabolism, it is not
the only mechanism nor is it the single major pathway.
▪ No rationale was provided for the categorization of low, moderate,
and high metabolizing donors.
▪ The experimental results did not correspond to the known
metabolism of tradipitant (i.e., the high metabolizer had the lowest
levels of M3 and M4).
The experiment was conducted using a single donor or source for each primary
tissue type, with the exception of the liver construct. Any findings in this assay for
these cell types may reflect the response of an individual donor or cell
preparation and may not be predictive of the general population.
The duration of treatment in this study was only 14 days; short-duration
experiments do not provide data on the chronic toxicity of the drug.

12.1.3 Conclusions and Recommendation
As discussed in this document, the Applicant previously59 asserted that their “totality of
evidence” approach for the nonclinical program, including six (now seven) additional in
vitro assays, sufficiently demonstrates the safety of tradipitant and that a chronic toxicity
study in a nonrodent species is not justified. The Division thoroughly reviewed these
additional in vitro studies and evaluated their scientific and regulatory contribution to the
safety profile of tradipitant, taking into account all previously submitted nonclinical data
for this drug.
When evaluating novel in vitro assays for use in regulatory decisions, particularly in lieu
of a standard nonclinical toxicity study, it is important to determine that such methods
employ sound science and are appropriate to inform regulatory decision-making. For
the additional assays submitted in the current NDA, necessary considerations for their
usability in regulatory decision-making include best practices for in vitro study design
and conduct60,61,62 and the compliance (or lack thereof) with US FDA GLP regulations.
Additionally, to fully characterize the safety of a drug intended for long-term, unrestricted
administration in humans, including under conditions of repeated intermittent dosing,
any alternative information submitted in lieu of a chronic toxicity study needs to
substantively contribute to the understanding of the long-term safety of the drug.
Following review of the provided study reports, only minor limitations were identified in
the study design and conduct of the five in vitro liver safety pharmacology studies

Reference ID: 5719891

NDA # 220152

Sarah J. Morgan, PhD

(HPTOX-3540, HPTOX-3550, HPTOX-3560, HPTOX-3610, and SRV-0028), generally
involving the unclear biological relevance of the reported endpoints and the lack of
clinically actionable findings. These studies do not rule out the potential for tradipitant to
cause liver injury, as previously identified in the 13-week study in Han Wistar rats;
indeed, they provide evidence that the potential for liver injury is not animal-specific and
extends to humans. However, the utility of these studies is limited in that they did not
achieve their stated objective; that is, the studies failed to identify a primary mechanism
by which liver injury may occur, and they did not clearly define a threshold at which the
effects may be expected to be observed clinically.
After review of the four-organ studies (HSTOX-7619 and HSTOX-2008-002), the
submitted studies as designed, executed, and reported do not contribute to the
assessment of the long-term safety of tradipitant (i.e., for unrestricted use over a
lifetime). Limitations of the studies included the narrow scope of toxicity evaluations,
unjustified biological relevance of the system and chosen endpoints, lack of compliance
with US FDA GLP regulations, and short duration of treatment relative to proposed
unrestricted use over a lifetime.
Although CDER is open to alternative approaches to traditional nonclinical toxicity
studies to assess risk for clinical use, including the use of in vitro assays such as those
employed here, any alternative proposal should be scientifically sound and appropriate
to inform regulatory decision-making. The Applicant concluded63 that, “given all the
alternatives [they] have done,” a chronic toxicity study in a nonrodent species is not
justified. As discussed above, the submitted alternative in vitro assays do not
substantially contribute to the characterization of the long-term safety of tradipitant.
We acknowledge that the indication of prevention of vomiting induced by motion will
involve repeat intermittent use of tradipitant. However, the Applicant has not submitted
data to suggest that the cumulative toxicity of tradipitant will be different whether
exposure to tradipitant occurs consecutively or intermittently. As we expect repeat
intermittent dosing of tradipitant to continue over a lifetime for this indication, long-term
nonclinical toxicity studies (i.e., a 6-month toxicity study in a rodent species and a ≥ 6month toxicity study in a nonrodent species) are the most appropriate64 to support the
safety of unrestricted use of tradipitant over a lifetime for this indication. Following
careful evaluation of the entire nonclinical data package, there remains insufficient
nonclinical information to adequately characterize the safety of unrestricted use of
tradipitant over a lifetime in humans, and a chronic toxicity study in a nonrodent species
is still warranted.
We acknowledge that the clinical trial data suggest that tradipitant is effective at
preventing vomiting induced by motion, and clinical safety data with tradipitant are
available for up to 90 doses. We also acknowledge that the prescribing information will
(b) (4)

64 For drug products clinically used for longer than one month in duration, FDA’s scientific standard is that

chronic toxicity studies in a rodent and nonrodent species are the best studies to characterize the
toxicological safety of those products and support approval.

describe the limitations of the available safety data. However, the lack of adequate
nonclinical data to support the unrestricted use of tradipitant over a lifetime is an
approvability issue.
12.2 Assessment of Available Data To Support the Safety of Tradipitant
Metabolites
Based on the available clinical and nonclinical data, the four major human metabolites
of tradipitant, M2, M3, M4, and M8, should be qualified to support the safety of
tradipitant for this application. Metabolite M3 is the only metabolite qualified by the
levels present in the nonclinical studies, while M2, M4, and M8 were not measured, not
present, or present at low levels in animals and therefore not considered qualified for
safety using typical approaches. However, M2, M4, and M8 are not likely to produce
increased or novel toxicities compared to tradipitant, based on a Weight of Evidence
(WoE) analysis including structural evaluations and assessment of known on- and offtarget effects of tradipitant and the metabolites. Therefore, the levels of these
metabolites were considered qualified for safety based on a comparison of total drugrelated exposure (i.e., the sum of tradipitant, M2, M3, M4, and M8) in humans and
animals, and there are no unresolved safety concerns regarding the metabolites for this
application. Support for this determination is discussed herein.
12.2.1 Background on Nonclinical Characterization of Metabolite Safety
Characterization of the metabolic profile of a drug is an important part of the drug
development process. Nonclinical testing, including general toxicity studies, typically
focuses on adverse effects as they relate to exposure to the parent drug. However,
biotransformation of the parent drug via phase 1 and phase 2 metabolic pathways may
result in metabolites that also contribute to toxicity. It is not standard practice for drug
metabolites to be evaluated separately in a cross-species safety assessment, but
nonclinical assessment may be warranted under certain circumstances. These
circumstances are broadly discussed in FDA guidance for industry Safety Testing of
Drug Metabolites, Revision 2 (March 2020).
Three successive considerations are used to determine whether nonclinical qualification
of drug metabolites should be conducted to support the safety of a drug.
(1) To warrant qualification, a metabolite should first be shown to be a disproportionate
metabolite, meaning that the metabolite is only formed in humans and absent in the
animal test species, or, that the metabolite is present at higher levels in humans than
in the animal species used in the standard toxicology testing with the parent drug.
(2) If a disproportionate metabolite is identified, a metabolite should be present at >10%
of total drug-related exposure at steady state, typically based on AUC, to warrant
further characterization. It is not generally feasible to measure AUC of all
metabolites by nonradiolabeled methods, particularly for those drugs that have many
metabolites. In these cases, a human mass-balance PK study using a single
radiolabeled dose of drug provides a reasonable estimate of human total drug-

119
Reference ID: 5719891

NDA # 220152

Sarah J. Morgan, PhD

related exposure and may be an adequate basis for calculating whether a metabolite
exceeds 10%.65
(3) Finally, a disproportionate metabolite that is >10% of total drug-related exposure
warrants further evaluation if the levels measured in nonclinical species do not
approach human exposure (i.e., are <50% the exposure in humans65). The decision
tree for this process is shown in Figure 50.
Figure 50. Metabolite Decision Tree Flow Diagram

Figure copied directly from FDA guidance for industry M3(R2) Questions and Answers (R2), Appendix A
(March 2013).

Sponsors are encouraged to identify any differences in drug metabolism between
animals used in nonclinical safety assessments and humans as early as possible during
the drug development process, so that appropriate nonclinical studies can be conducted
to qualify the safety of these metabolites, if warranted. Such studies should generally be
completed prior to the initiation of phase 3 clinical trials and should include a general
toxicity study of appropriate duration, in vitro genotoxicity assays (evaluating the
potential for both point mutations and chromosomal aberrations), an embryo-fetal
development study, and a carcinogenicity study. Unlike the parent drug, in vivo studies
conducted in a single species are generally sufficient to support the safety of
disproportionate metabolites.
65 FDA guidance for industry M3(R2) Questions and Answers (R2), page 5. March 2013.

NDA # 220152

Sarah J. Morgan, PhD

At the time phase 3 trials with tradipitant were initiated, the Applicant’s available
nonclinical data supported that M2, M4, and M8 were likely to be present at significantly
lower levels in rats and dogs compared to humans (i.e., that these were
disproportionate metabolites).74 However, at no point during development were the
available clinical pharmacology data sufficient to definitively determine whether any
metabolite exceeded the typical threshold of 10% of drug-related exposure at steady
state at the proposed clinical doses that would warrant qualification by nonclinical data.
Total drug-related exposure at steady state was not evaluated in any clinical study
submitted under IND. Although the previously mentioned single-dose human massbalance PK study was available (Trial H8R-MC-HJAK), this study was considered to
have several limitations including:
•
•
•
•

The low dose relative to the intended clinical dose (25 mg versus 85 or 170 mg);
Tradipitant, M2, M3, and M4 only accounted for 26% of total radioactivity, based
on AUC;
Tradipitant itself did not meet the 10% threshold; and
The AUC of M8 was not reported.

Disregarding the limitations, this study on its face suggested that none of the major
human metabolites would meet the typical 10% threshold to warrant further evaluation.
Additionally, clinical trials with tradipitant have not exceeded 12 weeks or 90 dosing
days for any indication. Therefore, the lingering potential for unqualified metabolites was
not considered a safety issue for tradipitant prior to the submission of a marketing
application. However, additional data included in the marketing applications for
tradipitant led to the concern that the safety of the major human metabolites had not
been adequately addressed (see below).

Metabolite Data and Review Under NDA 220152
No new nonclinical studies, including no new studies with the metabolites, were
included in the initial submission for NDA 220152 (December 30, 2024) compared to
that submitted under NDA 218489. In addition to the previously submitted clinical
pharmacology data, NDA 220152 contained sparse human PK data for tradipitant and
its major human metabolites based on Trial 3404 in subjects with a history of motion
sickness. The population PK model previously submitted under NDA 218489 was not
initially updated with these data, nor was an integrated population PK analysis run to
characterize the PK profile of tradipitant and its metabolites in subjects with a history of
motion sickness.
Based on the data gaps identified during the review of NDA 218489 and the lack of new
information addressing those issues, concerns remained whether the metabolites had
been sufficiently characterized and/or qualified based on the available data. In the filing
letter, the Applicant was notified of the review team’s concerns that human levels of the
metabolites were not well-defined and that the safety of the metabolites may not be
supported by the submitted nonclinical data.81 In that letter, the review team requested
that the Applicant submit any additional nonclinical data that were available to support
the safety of the levels of metabolites M2, M4, and M8 (i.e., the three disproportionate
human metabolites). The review team also requested the study of M8 pharmacologic
activity that was presumed to be completed but had never been submitted either during
the review of NDA 218489 or to the current NDA.
In response to the requests made in the filing letter, the Applicant submitted the M8
activity study as well as a new liver safety pharmacology study with the metabolites
(March 25, 2025; SDN 9).
•

The M8 activity study demonstrated that M8 has pharmacologic activity at the
NK-1 receptor. This information further strengthens (but does not prove) the
review team’s previous assumption that M2, M3, and M4 are also
pharmacologically active. Notably, this study report is dated May 2, 2024, and
thus it was available both during the review of NDA 218489 and at the time of the
initial submission for NDA 220152.

•

The in vitro liver safety pharmacology study using 3D human donor-derived
hepatocyte spheroids was similar to those previously conducted with tradipitant
and submitted under NDA 218489. Importantly, unlike the previously submitted
studies that only tested tradipitant or were limited to qualitative evaluations of
hepatotoxicity with the metabolites, this new study included quantitative

81 NDA 220152: Filing Review Issues Identified Letter. March 14, 2025.

124
Reference ID: 5719891

NDA # 220152

Sarah J. Morgan, PhD

assessments of hepatotoxicity following direct treatment with the metabolites.
This study demonstrated that all four major metabolites have the potential to
induce hepatotoxicity at concentrations that could reasonably be achieved
clinically (with the caveat that proper IVIVE analyses have not been conducted to
bridge between in vitro assay concentrations and in vivo drug exposure). This
study report is dated January 19, 2025, and thus postdates the review of NDA
218489 as well as the initial submission for NDA 220152.
•

Following this submission, the clinical pharmacology team requested that the
Applicant update the population PK model and provide simulated PK profiles and
parameters for tradipitant and the major human metabolites in subjects with a
history of motion sickness under various conditions.82 The Applicant agreed to
conduct some of the additional analyses.83 Pooled population PK modeling and
simulations for tradipitant were provided on June 30, 2025,84 while modeling and
simulations of the metabolites were provided on July 22, 2025.85 While limitations
were identified with the modeling and analyses, the findings were generally
consistent with those in studies in healthy volunteers. Thus, these analyses
increased the review team’s confidence in the appropriateness of using the
healthy volunteer data to represent the motion sickness population.

Taken together, the new data submitted to NDA 220152 provided confirmation that:
•

M8 is pharmacologically active (as, presumably, are M2, M3, and M4);

•

All four major metabolites have quantifiable toxicity in an assay using human
donor-derived cells. Specifically, these compounds were toxic, evidenced by cell
death, to human donor-derived hepatocytes, with findings generally similar to
tradipitant itself. As metabolites M2, M4, and M8 are present at much lower
levels in the toxicology species compared to humans and have quantifiable
toxicity, it is scientifically possible that these metabolites can cause other
toxicities that were not adequately captured in the completed animal studies; and

•

These metabolites are present at high levels compared to tradipitant itself and at
levels that each exceed 10% of the total drug-related exposure in humans as
measured in clinical studies (i.e., where total exposure = tradipitant + M2 + M3 +
M4 ± M8).

Based on these newly identified factors, the review team concluded that qualification of
the levels of the major human metabolites is necessary to support the safety and
approval of tradipitant for the prevention of vomiting induced by motion. As such, the
adequacy of the nonclinical data to support the safety of the levels of these metabolites
was considered a review issue. These concerns were communicated to the Applicant
during the Mid-Cyle Meeting held on July 23, 2025.86

12.2.3 Clinical Characterization of Major Metabolites
The major metabolites of tradipitant were measured in three trials in healthy volunteers
and one trial in subjects with a history of motion sickness. Population PK modeling was
also conducted to provide further insight into the levels of metabolites expected in
subjects with motion sickness using tradipitant. Although these data are not derived
from nonclinical studies, understanding the human levels of tradipitant and its
metabolites cannot be separated from characterization of their safety in this application.
Thus, human PK data for tradipitant and its metabolites, delineated by various
parameters (i.e., single and multiple doses in healthy subjects in fed and fasted states,
single and multiple doses in subjects with gastroparesis and motion sickness), are
presented here as well as in clinical sections of this review, to provide additional context
for the nonclinical assessment of the safety of the metabolites.
•

At 4 hours following a single dose of 25 mg in a mass balance study with
radiolabeled tradipitant in healthy subjects (Trial H8R-MC-HJAK), tradipitant, M2,
M3, M4, and M8 accounted for 24%, 11%, 13%, 16%, and 13% of total drugrelated radioactivity in the plasma, respectively.91 The median Tmax in this trial
was 2 hours, with a range of 1 to 8 hours. Based on AUC0-inf, tradipitant, M2, M3,
and M4 accounted for approximately 9%, 4%, 5%, and 8% of total drug-related
radioactivity in plasma, respectively, while this parameter was not evaluated for
M8. Although tradipitant exposure was highly variable, the AUC of M4 was
similar to that of tradipitant in 3/5 subjects in this trial.

•

Following a single dose of 85 mg in fasted healthy subjects in a food effect study
(Trial 1002; Table 17),92 levels of metabolites M2, M3, M4, and M8 were
measured at significant levels (33% to 44%) compared to tradipitant itself, based

In the current NDA 220152, the Applicant provided updated population PK modeling
and analyses as requested by the clinical pharmacology team. Numerous limitations of
the provided information were identified by the Applicant and confirmed by the review
team, including, but not limited to:
(1) Parameter estimates were considered not reliable as the models did not
converge properly.
(2) Dosing history was unreliable due to imprecise time of doses and subject
noncompliance.
(3) The tradipitant modeling did not recapitulate the food effect previously observed
in Trial 1002.
Given these limitations, the PK parameters for subjects with motion sickness were
considered unreliable, as determined by simulating Trials 3404 and 3403 using the
updated population PK model. However, the results of the modeling and analysis
supported that PK parameters were expected to be similar between healthy volunteers
and subjects with motion sickness. Thus, the parameters of Trial 1002 were considered
most appropriate for assessing the safety of single doses of tradipitant, including the
safety of the levels of the metabolites,96 whereas PK parameters from Trial 1102, which

12.2.4 Nonclinical Characterization of Major Metabolites
Nonclinical Qualification of M3
Although definitive activity assays have not been conducted, metabolite M3 is presumed
to be pharmacologically active based on its ability to inhibit ligand binding at the NK-1
receptor ≥99.0% at 10µM and with similar potency compared to tradipitant (reported97
IC50 of 0.38nM, compared to the tradipitant IC50 of 0.40nM in a concurrent study). The
Applicant attempted to address the in vivo safety of M3 by retroactively measuring it in
the 13-week repeat-dose toxicity study in Fischer rats (mid dose only) and prospectively
measuring it in the 13-week repeat-dose toxicity study in Han Wistar rats as well as in
carcinogenicity (mice and rats), reproductive toxicity (rats only), and juvenile animal
(rats) studies; metabolites were not measured in any submitted nonrodent study.
M3 was generally observed in rats98 at exposures similar to or exceeding that expected
in humans99 under both single-dose and steady-state conditions (Table 20). Therefore,
the levels of M3 are qualified for safety and no additional studies are warranted with this
metabolite. Data from an in vitro liver safety pharmacology study using human donorderived hepatocyte spheroids and a study using a human liver co-culture MPS
(treatment with M3 directly) resulted in quantitative and qualitative effects on hepatocyte
viability, respectively. These studies support that M3 has a human-relevant risk for liver
toxicity.

Nonclinical Qualification of M2
Although definitive activity assays have not been conducted, metabolite M2 is presumed
to be pharmacologically active based on its ability to inhibit binding at the NK-1 receptor

carcinogenicity (mice and rats), reproductive toxicity (PPND in rats only), and juvenile
animal (rats) studies; metabolites were not measured in any submitted nonrodent study.

levels compared to the single-dose102 or steady-state99 levels in humans, such that
under no condition was at least 50% of human M2 exposure achieved in animals.
Findings in mice103 were similar. Thus, the levels of M2 are not qualified for safety.

(steady-state data from subjects administered 85 mg Q12H for 12 days), and Study TAJ0035 (singledose and 13-week values in female Han Wistar rats, i.e., the strain and sex with highest exposure).
Tradipitant Dose

Duration

M2 was further evaluated in one in vitro liver safety pharmacology study using human
donor-derived hepatocyte spheroids and one study using a human liver co-culture MPS
(treatment with M2 directly). In these studies, direct treatment with M2 resulted in
qualitative (liver co-culture MPS) and quantitative (hepatocyte spheroids) effects on
hepatocyte viability. These studies support that M2 has a human-relevant risk for liver
toxicity. Additionally, M2 was evaluated in a four-organ MPS (treatment with tradipitant
resulting in substantial metabolism to M2 in one study but limited metabolism to M2 in
the second study) in which changes in the chosen endpoints (i.e., potential signals of
toxicity) were observed in all organs under certain experimental conditions; the specific
contribution of M2 to these toxicities could not be determined.
Based on the available information, metabolite M2 is (1) a major human metabolite; (2)
essentially not present in rats and not measured in dogs or another nonrodent species;
and (3) presumed to be pharmacologically active given similar IC50 value to tradipitant
for inhibition of binding at the NK-1 receptor. Additionally, there is a potential for toxicity
with this metabolite in human tissues (e.g., the liver). Therefore, the levels of M2 in
humans are not qualified for safety based on the available in vivo nonclinical data, and
additional safety assessments are warranted.
Nonclinical Qualification of M4
Although definitive activity assays have not been conducted, metabolite M4 is presumed
to be pharmacologically active based on its ability to inhibit binding at the NK-1 receptor
≥99.0% at 10µM and with similar potency compared to tradipitant (reported97 IC50 value
of 2.23nM, compared to the tradipitant IC50 of 0.40nM in a concurrent study100). The
Applicant attempted to address the in vivo safety of M4 by retroactively measuring it in
the 13-week repeat-dose toxicity study in Fischer rats (mid dose only) and prospectively
measuring it in the 13-week repeat-dose toxicity study in Han Wistar rats as well as in
carcinogenicity (mice and rats), reproductive toxicity (PPND in rats only), and juvenile
animal (rats) studies; metabolites were not measured in any submitted nonrodent study.

Following repeated dosing in general toxicity and carcinogenicity studies in rats and
mice,98,101,103 M4 was generally not detected. In the single instance where it was
quantifiable (female Fisher rats treated with tradipitant for 3 months at 150 mg/kg/day),
M4 was present at ≤1% of the human AUC under the various clinical exposure
scenarios. Thus, the levels of M4 are not qualified for safety.

with this metabolite in human tissues (e.g., the liver). Therefore, the levels of M4 in
humans are not qualified for safety based on the available in vivo nonclinical data, and
additional safety assessments are warranted.
Nonclinical Qualification of M8
Metabolite M8 is a direct N-glucuronide conjugate of tradipitant that was detected in
liver slices from human and nonhuman primate but not in those from rat and dog (i.e.,
the species used in tradipitant general toxicity studies). Plasma levels of M8 were not

133
Reference ID: 5719891

NDA # 220152

Sarah J. Morgan, PhD

evaluated in any in vivo nonclinical study with tradipitant during clinical development, as
the Applicant assumed that M8 was not formed in rats and dogs following administration
of tradipitant. However, M8 was “seen at significant levels in humans.”104
“Phase 2 conjugation reactions [e.g., glucuronidation] generally render a compound
more water soluble and pharmacologically inactive, thereby eliminating the need for
further evaluation.”105 Indeed, the Applicant initially stated in NDA 218489 and reiterated
in the current NDA that “M8 […] is a direct glucuronide conjugate and therefore was
considered of lesser concern.”106 However, M8 was shown to inhibit binding to the NK-1
receptor by 99.3% at 10µM, with an IC50 of 0.49nM (compared to an IC50 of 3.3nM for
tradipitant in the same study).107 In the current NDA,108 the Applicant further
demonstrated that M8 inhibits substance P-induced calcium release in vitro, similarly to
tradipitant, with reported IC50 values of 6.3 and 0.63nM, respectively. The Cmax values
for M8 following a single dose of tradipitant of 85 or 170 mg in fasted healthy volunteers
were 13.3 and 21.7 ng/mL, respectively,109 or approximately 2.7- and 4.4-fold the IC50
for substance P-induced calcium release. Thus, M8 is a pharmacologically active
metabolite, and this activity is relevant at the proposed clinical doses.
As previously stated, M8 has not been measured in any in vivo nonclinical toxicity study
with tradipitant. M8 was evaluated in one in vitro liver safety pharmacology studies
using human donor-derived hepatocytes and one study using a human liver co-culture
MPS (treatment with M8 directly). In these studies, direct treatment with M8 resulted in
qualitative (liver co-culture MPS) and quantitative (hepatocyte spheroids) effects on
hepatocyte viability. Thus, these studies support that M8 has a human-relevant risk for
liver toxicity. M8 was not measured in the studies using the four-organ MPS.
Based on the available information, metabolite M8 is (1) a major human metabolite, (2)
not expected to be produced in nonclinical species (and was not measured in these
studies), and (3) has pharmacologic activity at the proposed clinical doses. Therefore,
the levels of M8 in humans are not qualified for safety based on the available in vivo
nonclinical data, and additional safety assessments are warranted.
12.2.5 Qualification of Metabolite Safety based on Total Drug-Related Exposure
Eli Lilly previously argued that no further nonclinical evaluation of metabolites M2, M4,
and M8 was necessary based on a WoE assessment of the nonclinical and clinical data
available at that time. Their primary argument was that each metabolite did not meet the

Sarah J. Morgan, PhD

threshold of 10% of drug-related material110 in the single-dose mass balance study. As
a secondary point, Eli Lilly argued that no idiosyncratic toxicity was expected with these
metabolites based on their structural changes compared to tradipitant, and that the ontarget safety of the metabolites was adequately covered by the completed nonclinical
studies in rats and dogs based on the pharmacological similarities between tradipitant
and the metabolites (i.e., similar binding affinities for the NK-1 receptor). As stated
(b) (4)
previously, it was agreed at the time of the 2006 meeting request (under IND
that no further nonclinical evaluation of these metabolites was necessary, although the
merits of specific arguments were not formally reviewed.68,69
We first note that the initial draft guidance for industry Safety Testing of Drug
Metabolites, as referenced by Eli Lilly in their submission, was published in 2005. Since
then, the Agency’s thinking about what defines a concerning human metabolite has
evolved, in part due to technological advances to detect, identify, and characterize
metabolites. As reflected in the most recent FDA guidance on safety testing of drug
metabolites (March 2020), the Agency considers drug metabolites to be a potential
safety concern when they (1) are shown to be disproportionate metabolites and (2)
present in humans “at greater than 10 percent of total drug-related exposure at steady
state [unless there is some justification for a different measure of exposure].” The
current thinking of the Agency now supersedes the early, draft recommendation cited by
Eli Lilly.
Despite these revisions, the level of the metabolite in humans (i.e., whether it meets the
10% threshold necessitating nonclinical qualification) remains a primary consideration,
although, as stated in the guidance, “other metabolites also can elicit safety concern.”111
Based on the points discussed previously (i.e., pharmacologic relevance, toxicologic
relevance, and relative abundance of the metabolites), we do not agree that the safety
of the metabolites has been adequately addressed simply because they do not appear
to meet the 10% threshold following a single 25 mg dose (i.e., a dose much lower than
proposed for the current indication) in the mass balance study. In other words, based on
the currently available information (i.e., including numerous studies not available at the
time of Eli Lilly’s analysis), we do not inherently agree with the 2006 determination that
no additional nonclinical information is warranted to support the safety of the
metabolites for the prevention of vomiting induced by motion. Rather, M2, M4, and M8
are disproportionate human metabolites that are not qualified for safety based on the
available in vivo nonclinical data. Notably, both Eli Lilly and the current Applicant agree
110 At the time of Eli Lilly’s submission (2006), the published guidance for industry “Safety Testing of Drug

Metabolites” was in a draft stage (publication June 2005). “This guidance defines major metabolites
primarily as those identified in human plasma that account for greater than 10 percent of drug related
material (administered dose or systemic exposure whichever is less) and that were not present at
sufficient levels to permit adequate evaluation during standard nonclinical animal studies.” Note that, in
this draft guidance, the term “major metabolite” was used instead of the currently accepted
“disproportionate metabolite.” Similarly, “drug related material” has been replaced with “total drug-related
exposure at steady state” in the current guidance. Numerous other changes have been made since 2005
to reflect FDA’s evolving understanding of metabolite safety. The principles of the most recent version of
the guidance, published in March 2020, serve as the basis for the current review.
111 FDA guidance for industry Safety Testing of Drug Metabolites Revision 2 (March 2020), footnote 12.
