the safety assessment of a drug such as tradipitant or its metabolites. M2 is not a
“naturally occurring simple […] alcohol,” or a flavoring substance with “a long history of
human experience in […] consumption from traditional foods,” nor does it have
“organoleptic and gustatory properties” limiting its use (e.g., to <1 mg/person/day).113
Thus, the safety assessment in the cited WHO report is not applicable to tradipitant or
its metabolites, and it does not support the assertion that M2 is expected to have “a low
order of oral toxicity.”112
Figure 51. Metabolism of Tradipitant to M2

Figure created by reviewer, based on Study ADME Report 01, Figure 1.

In the context of the current application, the scientific appropriateness for qualification of
M2 based on total drug-related exposure was determined using a WoE approach. The
WoE included assessment of the structural differences, pharmacological on-target
activity (including pharmacodynamics), and off-target activity of M2 compared to
tradipitant and relied on all available information for both compounds (i.e., including data
that was not available in 2006).
Structural Assessment
To assess the potential toxicological significance of the ketone reduction, a (Q)SAR
analysis was performed by CDER’s Division of Applied Regulatory Science (DARS)
Computational Toxicology Consultation Service (CTCS) to generate predictions of the
hepatobiliary toxicity of M2 compared to tradipitant and to identify new structural alerts
related to the ketone reduction.114 Briefly, tradipitant and M2 were evaluated with a
battery of statistical- and expert rule-based hepatotoxicity (Q)SAR models. The battery
utilized statistical (Q)SAR software platforms from Leadscope Model Applier (LMA) and
113 World Health Organization, 1996, Toxicological Evaluation of Certain Food Additives and

Contaminants. The 44th Meeting of the Joint FAO/WHO Expert Committee on Food Additives (JECFA).
WHO Food Additives Series 35, accessed 2025,
https://www.inchem.org/documents/jecfa/jecmono/v35je21.htm.
114 NDA 220152: Hepatobiliary Toxicity (Q)SAR analysis and Chemical Structural Similarity Search for
Metabolites of Tradipitant. CDER/OTS/OCP/DARS: Computational Toxicology Consultation Service.
October 31, 2025.

137
Reference ID: 5719891

NDA # 220152

Sarah J. Morgan, PhD

CASE Ultra (CU) that contained models for liver damage, liver enzyme abnormalities,
cholestasis, bile duct disorders, and gall bladder disorders. The battery also included
the expert rule-based software, Derek Nexus, which utilized a set of 64 hepatotoxicity
structural alerts. Structural similarity searches were conducted using Leadscope
Enterprise and Lhasa Limited’s Vitic toxicity database to identify drug substances that
contain an alcohol in a similar chemical environment to M2 to serve as comparators.
Additionally, a search of the published literature was conducted. Similarity and
substructure-based searches were focused on capturing some variant of the di-aryl
secondary alcohol group. An additional search was conducted across the drug class of
NK-1 receptor antagonists for any structurally similar drug substances containing the diaryl secondary alcohol.
The results of the in silico hepatobiliary toxicity models overall suggested no new
concerns related to the ketone reduction in M2 compared to the parent drug, tradipitant.
Most predictions for hepatobiliary toxicity with M2 exhibited the same or lower positive
prediction probabilities compared to tradipitant. An increase in the signaling strength
with the CU cholestasis model for M2 was considered nonspecific and therefore
dismissed. An increase in the signaling strength with LMA’s liver damage model with M2
compared to tradipitant was considered to represent the loss of the phenone-like
carbonyl group, identified by the model as a mitigating (negative contributing) feature in
the parent molecule; this effect was not mirrored in the results from the CU model and
was therefore considered not toxicologically significant.
No structural alerts were identified for M2 using the Derek Nexus expert rule-based
model.
No structurally relevant comparator compounds were identified containing a ketone or a
secondary alcohol in the same chemical environment as tradipitant or M2, respectively,
precluding an assessment of systemic toxicity based on data with comparator drugs.
CTCS searched broadly for substructures that had the ketone/alcohol flanked by two
aryl rings (not just specifically one phenyl and one pyridine ring, as featured in
tradipitant) but were unable to find any compounds with this moiety. CTCS also looked
for globally similar (whole molecule similarity) compounds to tradipitant and M2 and did
not find sufficiently similar analogs with available toxicity data. Additionally, other
members of the class of NK-1 receptor antagonists did not yield any useful analogs, as
the shared substructural regions of the other class members with tradipitant are in the
bis-trifluoromethylphenyl region, not the substructures of interest.
Overall, the results of the CDER CTCS (Q)SAR analyses support that no enhanced or
unanticipated toxicities are expected with M2 compared to tradipitant based on the
ketone reduction.
The Applicant conducted their own assessment of the potential toxicological
significance of the ketone reduction in parallel with the CDER analyses.47,48,49,50 Briefly,
these in silico (computational) analyses were conducted using a machine learningassisted workflow developed by xAI's Grok system, an advanced large language model

138
Reference ID: 5719891

NDA # 220152

Sarah J. Morgan, PhD

trained on scientific datasets. This workflow, validated via the Applicant’s in-house
Python scripts, leveraged RDKit version 2024.03.5 for substructure matching and
molecular property calculations, infers qualitative thresholds and probability estimates
for toxicity endpoints from RDKit-computed molecular descriptors (e.g., LogP, TPSA,
HBD, HBA). It draws on established rules and patterns synthesized from peer-reviewed
literature, (Q)SAR tool manuals (e.g., OECD QSAR Toolbox, Tox21), and manuscripts
describing consensus classifiers such as CORAL, OCHEM, and Balanced Random
Forest (BRF). These include profilers such as DNA binding by OASIS v1.5, Protein
binding by OASIS v1.5, and Carcinogenicity alerts by ISS v2.0, with attributes like
mechanistic domains and SMARTS-based pattern recognition.
Notably, the Applicant’s comparative computational prediction data were inferred based
on RDKit-computed descriptors and qualitative thresholds aligned with best practices
from consensus classifiers, rather than direct execution of (Q)SAR models. It is not
clear why the Applicant chose to design a workflow using a large language model and a
custom set of molecular descriptors and use information from existing classifier
techniques to ‘infer’ predictions, rather than directly using existing (Q)SAR modeling
software. Several times it is mentioned that this workflow is using ‘established’ rules,
patterns, and model performance metrics from peer-reviewed literature, but no literature
was cited. Validation statistics and training set attributes of these consensus models
were requested but not provided.
In the Applicant’s analyses, no additional reactive groups were identified in M2
compared to tradipitant. No significant differences between tradipitant and M2 were
predicted for PK parameters, acute toxicity, mutagenicity, carcinogenicity, aquatic
toxicity, or skin sensitization; in general, these predictions are consistent with completed
in vitro studies with M2 and tradipitant and/or in vivo nonclinical studies with tradipitant.
The potential for tradipitant and M2 to cause nephrotoxicity and neurotoxicity were also
considered comparable and low. Compared to tradipitant, M2 was predicted to have
slightly increased risk of hepatotoxicity (increased oxidation potential, potential druginduced liver injury [DILI] alerts) and cardiotoxicity (increased hERG affinity, with
predicted IC50 >5µM or approximately 29-fold the clinical Cmax,ss). Despite the slight
increase in risk presented by these analyses, the potential liver toxicity of M2 is
considered to be consistent with tradipitant based on the CDER (Q)SAR analyses and
findings in the completed in vitro liver safety pharmacology studies. The potential for
M2-related cardiotoxicity due to hERG channel binding, while not directly assessed in
nonclinical studies, has been assessed in completed clinical studies (with no risk
identified, although the high-exposure scenario has not been addressed) and will be
(b) (4)
further assessed in a clinical TQT study

Overall, the Applicant’s overall predictions are consistent with available data and with
CDER’s internal analyses supporting that M2 is unlikely to pose additional toxicity
concerns over tradipitant based on structural differences.

Sarah J. Morgan, PhD

Pharmacologic, Pharmacokinetic, and Toxicologic Comparisons
Although M2 was not adequately qualified based on in vivo nonclinical studies, as
described above, a number of other studies provide additional information on the
expected on- and off-target effects of this metabolite (see Table 23).
Based on available data, tradipitant and M2 are expected to have similar pharmacologic
(i.e., on-target) activity. Both tradipitant and M2 inhibited ligand binding at the NK-1
receptor ≥99% at 10µM, with similar IC50 values of 0.40 and 1.55nM, respectively, in
studies conducted concurrently. A functional pharmacology assay was not conducted
with M2.
The predicted off-target effects of tradipitant and M2 were generally similar based on a
typical in vitro secondary pharmacology assay using a panel of 189 receptors, channels,
and transporters. However, M2 did show significant (i.e., >50%) inhibition of the human
ERα at the tested concentration of 10µM (approximately 58 times the Cmax,ss of M2),
while tradipitant did not. Even acknowledging the large potential food effect on M2 (Cmax
4-fold higher than that observed under fasted conditions, based on Trial 1002), the risk
of M2 significantly inhibiting ligand binding to ERα is considered low at the proposed
clinical dose. Thus, no clinically relevant differences in off-target binding are expected
between tradipitant and M2, based on the secondary pharmacology screen.
In general, reduction of a ketone group to an alcohol can result in alterations in drug
pharmacokinetics, including changes in half-life and altered tissue distribution.115
However, notable differences in elimination half-life (t1/2) and Tmax between tradipitant
and M2 were not observed (t1/2: 28.2 versus 43.7 hours; Tmax: 1.5 versus 4.0 hours).116
Protein plasma binding was high for both tradipitant and M2. Reduction of the di-aryl
ketone is not expected to significantly alter the lipophilicity of tradipitant, and thus no
significant differences are expected in tissue distribution. Therefore, based on available
evidence, the pharmacokinetics of tradipitant and M2 are not expected to be
significantly different.
Tradipitant significantly inhibited transporters BCRP, OATP1B1, OATP1B3, MATE1,
and MATE2-K in vitro, with IC50 values in the micromolar range. Metabolite M2 similarly
inhibited OATP1B1 and MATE1, with no effect on the other evaluated transporters.
Tradipitant was a poor substrate for P-gp, while M2 was not a substrate for P-gp or
BCRP.
In liver safety pharmacology studies using 3D human hepatocyte spheroids, treatment
with either tradipitant or M2 resulted in quantitative, biologically significant decreases in
hepatocyte viability at concentrations as low as 0.350 µg/mL (approximately 1.1 and 3.4
times the clinical Cmax,ss values for tradipitant and M2, respectively). In a liver safety
pharmacology study using a liver co-culture MPS, tradipitant resulted in qualitative,
115 This in agreement with the Applicant’s analysis that states, “The ketone reduction pathway is

commonly used for human drugs and typically results in simple alcohols that have a chemical profile
similar to the parent, although less lipophilic and less likely to cross membranes and accumulate.” See
NDA 220152, SDN 23: October 15, 2025. Response to Non-Clinical Information Request, page 6.
116 Based on data from Trial VP-VLY-686-1002 following a single dose of 170 mg under fasted conditions.

140
Reference ID: 5719891

NDA # 220152

Sarah J. Morgan, PhD

time- and concentration-dependent, minimal to moderate findings of hepatocyte toxicity
over the concentration range of 0.772-15 µg/mL. Treatment with M2 at 1.5 µg/mL, but
not a lower concentration, produced sporadic findings of minimal to mild hepatocyte
toxicity. Based on these studies, and given that the plasma concentration of M2 in
humans is expected to be approximately 20% to 40% that of tradipitant,117 M2 is
expected to have a similar or less severe risk of hepatocyte toxicity compared to
tradipitant at the clinical dose.
Although the four-organ MPS studies in general did not elicit interpretable results, they
did not provide any data demonstrating that M2 is likely to have increased or novel
toxicities compared to tradipitant.
Finally, both tradipitant and M2 were negative for genotoxicity in the Ames assay and
the in vitro chromosomal aberration assay in human lymphocytes.
Although M2 was not adequately evaluated in general toxicity studies with tradipitant,
the WoE supports that tradipitant and M2 are expected to have similar toxicological
profiles. Therefore, it is considered scientifically acceptable to qualify the safety of M2
based on total drug-related exposure to tradipitant and its active metabolites.

Weight-of-Evidence Assessment of M4
Tradipitant is metabolized to M4 by both reduction of the di-aryl ketone (i.e., in the same
way tradipitant is metabolized to M2) and N-oxidation of the mono-substituted pyridine
ring (i.e., in the same way tradipitant is metabolized to M3); thus, M4 can be formed by
secondary metabolism of either M2 or M3. As such, a WoE assessment of M4 includes
not only evaluations of M4 itself, but also evidence generated with M2 and M3.
Figure 52. Metabolism of Tradipitant to M4

Figure created by reviewer, based on Study ADME Report 01, Figure 1.

In the context of the current application, the scientific appropriateness of a WoE
approach for qualification of M4 based on total drug-related exposure was determined
by assessing its structural differences, pharmacological on-target activity, and off-target
activity compared to tradipitant. This WoE assessment relied on all available information
for tradipitant, M2, M3, and M4 (i.e., including data that was not available in 2006).
Structural Assessment
As with M2, a (Q)SAR analysis was performed by the CDER CTCS to generate
predictions of the hepatobiliary toxicity of M4 compared to tradipitant and to identify new
structural alerts related to the ketone reduction.114 The results of the hepatobiliary
toxicity models overall suggested no new concerns related to the ketone reduction, as
most predictions with M4 exhibited the same or lower positive prediction probabilities as
compared to the parent drug, tradipitant. An increase in the signaling strength with
LMA’s liver damage model with M4 was considered to represent the loss of the
phenone-like carbonyl group, identified by the model as a mitigating (negative
contributing) feature in tradipitant; this effect was not mirrored in the results from the CU
model and was therefore considered not toxicologically significant. No structural alerts
were identified for M4 using the Derek Nexus expert rule-based model. As with M2, no
structurally relevant comparator compounds were identified containing a ketone or a
144
Reference ID: 5719891

NDA # 220152

Sarah J. Morgan, PhD

secondary alcohol in the same chemical environment as tradipitant or M4, respectively,
precluding an assessment of toxicity using data with comparator drugs. Overall, the
results of the CDER (Q)SAR analyses support that no enhanced or unanticipated
toxicities are expected with M4 compared to tradipitant based on the ketone reduction.
As described above for M2, the Applicant conducted their own assessment of the
potential toxicological significance of both the N-oxidation and ketone reduction using
comparative computational prediction data inferred based on RDKit-computed
descriptors and qualitative thresholds aligned with best practices from consensus
classifiers (e.g., CORAL, OCHEM, BRF), rather than direct execution of these
models.47,48,49,50 No significant differences between tradipitant and M4 were predicted
for PK parameters, acute toxicity, mutagenicity, carcinogenicity, aquatic toxicity, or skin
sensitization; in general, these predictions are consistent with completed in vitro and/or
in vivo nonclinical studies. The potential for tradipitant and M4 to cause nephrotoxicity
and neurotoxicity were also considered comparable and low. Compared to tradipitant,
M4 was predicted to have slightly increased risk of oxidative stress (structural alert
based on the N-oxide), hepatotoxicity (with potential DILI alerts) and cardiotoxicity
(increased hERG affinity, with predicted IC50>5µM or approximately 29-fold the clinical
Cmax,ss). Despite the slight increase in risk presented by these analyses, the potential
liver toxicity of M4 is considered to be consistent with tradipitant based on the CDER
(Q)SAR analysis and findings in the completed in vitro liver safety pharmacology
studies. The potential for M4-related cardiotoxicity due to hERG channel binding, while
not directly assessed in nonclinical studies, has been assessed in completed clinical
studies with no risk identified, although the high-exposure scenario has not been
(b) (4)
addressed, and will be further assessed in a clinical TQT study
Although a structural alert for genotoxicity
was identified based on the N-oxide, both M3 and M4 were demonstrated to be
negative for genotoxicity in the Ames assay and an in vitro chromosomal aberration
assay in human lymphocytes.
Notably, the combination of the ketone reduction and N-oxidation was not predicted to
have additive or synergistic effects in any of the Applicant’s computational analyses;
rather, the predicted effects of these two modifications were either distinct or offsetting.
Thus, for all parameters, toxicity related to M4 (combination of the two individual
modifications) was predicted to be the same or less than that of M2 or M3 (each with
one modification compared to tradipitant) and similar to tradipitant.
Overall, the Applicants predictions are consistent with available data and with CDER’s
internal analyses supporting that M4 is unlikely to pose additional toxicity concerns over
tradipitant based on structural differences. Any residual uncertainty regarding the risk of
toxicity due to the N-oxide modification are sufficiently addressed by data generated
from the completed repeat-dose toxicity studies in rats in which the levels of M3 (also
containing this modification) were adequately qualified for safety.

145
Reference ID: 5719891

NDA # 220152

Sarah J. Morgan, PhD

Pharmacologic, Pharmacokinetic, and Toxicologic Comparisons
Although M4 was not adequately characterized in vivo in nonclinical studies, as
described above, a number of other studies provide additional information on the
expected on- and off-target effects of this metabolite (see Table 24).
Based on available data, tradipitant and M4 are expected to have similar pharmacologic
(i.e., on-target) activity. Both tradipitant and M4 inhibited ligand binding at the NK-1
receptor ≥99% at 10µM, with similar IC50 values of 0.40 and 2.23nM, respectively, in
studies conducted concurrently. A functional pharmacology assay was not conducted
with M4.
M4 did not demonstrate significant off-target effects based on a typical in vitro
secondary pharmacology assay using a panel of 189 receptors, channels, and
transporters. This suggests a similar or lower potential for off-target effects with M4
compared to tradipitant.
Notable pharmacokinetic differences between tradipitant and M4 were not observed
(t1/2: 28.2 versus 28.6 hours; Tmax: 1.5 versus 4.0 hours). Protein plasma binding was
high for both tradipitant and M4. As with M2, the ketone reduction is not expected to
significantly alter the polarity of tradipitant, and thus no significant differences are
expected in tissue distribution. Therefore, based on available evidence, the
pharmacokinetics of tradipitant and M4 are not expected to be significantly different.118
While tradipitant significantly inhibited transporters BCRP, OATP1B1, OATP1B3,
MATE1, and MATE2-K in vitro, with IC50 values in the micromolar range, M4 did not
significantly inhibit any tested transporter. Tradipitant was a poor substrate for P-gp,
while M4 was a strong substrate of P-gp and weak substrate of BRCP (similar to M3).
In liver safety pharmacology studies using 3D human hepatocyte spheroids, treatment
with either tradipitant or M4 resulted in quantitative, biologically significant decreases in
hepatocyte viability at concentrations as low as 0.350 µg/mL (approximately 1.1 and 3.4
times the clinical Cmax,ss values for tradipitant and M4, respectively). In a liver safety
pharmacology study using a liver co-culture MPS, tradipitant resulted in qualitative,
time- and concentration-dependent, minimal to moderate findings of hepatocyte toxicity
over the concentration range of 0.772-15 µg/mL, while treatment with M4 at
concentrations up to 1.5 µg/mL had no effects. Based on these studies, and given that
the plasma concentration of M4 in humans is expected to be approximately 30% to 40%
that of tradipitant,117 M4 is expected to have similar or less severe risk of hepatocyte
toxicity compared to tradipitant at the clinical dose.

118 This in agreement with the Applicant’s analysis that states, “The ketone reduction pathway is

commonly used for human drugs and typically results in simple alcohols that have a chemical profile
similar to the parent, although less lipophilic and less likely to cross membranes and accumulate. The Noxide modifications make the drug less lipophilic and easier to excrete. These metabolic pathways
operate at approximately equal rates and as such, all 4 major metabolites are roughly of equal
concentrations in the plasma.” See NDA 220152, SDN 23: Response to Non-Clinical Information
Request, page 6. October 15, 2025.

146
Reference ID: 5719891

NDA # 220152

Sarah J. Morgan, PhD

In general, the four-organ MPS studies did not elicit interpretable results. Additionally,
the levels of M4 were low such that any findings (or lack of findings) in these studies
could not be attributed to M4.
Finally, both tradipitant and M4 were negative for genotoxicity in the Ames assay and
the in vitro chromosomal aberration assay in human lymphocytes.
In addition to the lack of additional concerns identified with M4 itself compared to
tradipitant, both of the individual metabolic changes (i.e., ketone reduction and Noxidation) are considered not concerning based on available data. As described above,
the WoE assessment supports that the ketone reduction on M2 compared to tradipitant
is not expected to be toxicologically significant. Furthermore, the safety of the level of
M3 was qualified based on in vivo nonclinical studies. Finally, data support that the
ketone reduction and N-oxidation modifications are independent structural changes, and
no risk of additive or synergistic effects of these modifications was identified. As M4 is
expected to be present in humans at similar levels compared to M2 and M3, these
findings further support that the metabolism of tradipitant to M4 is expected to be of
limited toxicological significance.
Although M4 was not adequately evaluated in general toxicity studies with tradipitant,
the WoE supports that tradipitant and M4 are expected to have similar toxicological
profiles. Therefore, it is considered scientifically acceptable to qualify the safety of M4
based on total drug-related exposure to tradipitant and its active metabolites.

Sarah J. Morgan, PhD

Weight-of-Evidence Assessment of M8
As discussed above, tradipitant is metabolized to M8 via direct N-glucuronide of the
mono-substituted pyridine ring. Both tradipitant and M8 inhibited ligand binding at the
NK-1 ≥99% at 10µM, with similar IC50 values of 3.3 and 0.49nM, respectively, in the
same study. In an in vitro functional assay, tradipitant and M8 inhibited substance Pmediated calcium release with IC50 values of 0.63 and 6.3nM, respectively.
Figure 53. Metabolism of Tradipitant to M8

Figure created by reviewer, based on Study ADME Report 01, Figure 1

Although M8 was not adequately characterized in vivo in nonclinical studies, as
described above, a number of other studies provide additional information on the
expected on- and off-target effects of this metabolite (see Table 25). M8 did not
demonstrate significant off-target effects based on a typical in vitro secondary
pharmacology assay using a panel of 43 receptors, channels, and transporters
(including the benzothiazepine L-type calcium channel, dihydropyridine L-type calcium
channel, ERα, and GABAA TBOB chloride channel). This suggests a similar or lower
potential for off-target effects with M8 compared to tradipitant.
M8 has altered pharmacokinetic properties compared to tradipitant, including slightly
increased t1/2 and notably later Tmax (t1/2: 28.2 hours for tradipitant versus 31.4 hours for
M8; Tmax: 1.5 versus 30 hours). Glucuronidation is expected to increase polarity of a
metabolite and, thus, water solubility, decreasing the ability of that metabolite to cross
membranes. This results in increased plasma circulation and decreased distribution of a
metabolite (M8) to tissues compared to a parent drug (tradipitant). Thus, the differences
in pharmacokinetic parameters between tradipitant and M8 are consistent with the
expected effects of glucuronidation.
In liver safety pharmacology studies using 3D human hepatocyte spheroids, treatment
with tradipitant consistently resulted in quantitative, biologically significant decreases in

150
Reference ID: 5719891

NDA # 220152

Sarah J. Morgan, PhD

hepatocyte viability at concentrations as low as 0.350 µg/mL, while treatment with M8
had biologically significant effects in only 2/4 donors (at 0.350 and 10.5 µg/mL,
respectively). In a liver safety pharmacology study using a liver co-culture MPS,
tradipitant resulted in qualitative, time- and concentration-dependent, minimal to
moderate findings of hepatocyte toxicity over the concentration range of 0.772 to
15 µg/mL. Treatment with M8 at approximately 700 µg/mL, but not a lower
concentration, produced minimal to mild hepatocyte toxicity. Based on these studies,
M8 is expected to have similar or less severe risk of hepatocyte toxicity compared to
tradipitant at the clinical dose.
M8 was not evaluated in the four-organ MPS studies. M8 was also not evaluated in
genotoxicity assays; however, N-glucuronides themselves are typically not expected to
be genotoxic in the absence of a reactive acyl group.
Although M8 was not adequately evaluated in general toxicity studies with tradipitant,
the WoE supports that M8 is expected to remain primarily in the plasma and have
similar or reduced toxicity compared to tradipitant. Therefore, it is considered
scientifically acceptable to qualify the safety of M8 based on total drug-related exposure
to tradipitant and its active metabolites.

Qualification of Metabolite Safety Based on Total Drug-Related Exposure
Although the individual levels of M2, M4, and M8 are considered not qualified based on
the completed repeat-dose toxicity studies, the WoE supports that these metabolites are
expected to have similar toxicological profiles compared to tradipitant. As such, it is
considered appropriate to qualify these metabolites based on total drug-related
exposure in animals (i.e., rats) compared to humans.
Total tradipitant-related drug exposure in rats included measurement of tradipitant, M2,
M3, and M4; M8 was not measured in these studies as it was not expected to be formed
in rodent species. Based on these measurements, total drug-related exposure (i.e.,
AUC0-24) in rats was 42,613 ng·h/mL following a single dose and 12,400 ng·h/mL
following repeated dosing.
In humans following a single dose of tradipitant at 85 or 170 mg, total tradipitant-related
drug exposure included measurement of tradipitant, M2, M3, M4, and M8. This resulted
in total drug-related exposure (AUC0-24) of 4,756 and 7,558 ng·h/mL, respectively.119
Using the previously stated rat data, this provides exposure margins following a single
dose of tradipitant at 85 or 170 mg of 9.0 and 5.6, respectively. Thus, the safety of the
levels of metabolites M2, M4, and M8 following these single doses are considered
qualified based on total drug-related exposure.
At steady state, following 12 days of dosing at 85 mg BID, total tradipitant-related drug
exposure included measurement of tradipitant, M2, M3, and M4; M8 was not measured.
This resulted in total drug-related exposure (AUC0-24) at steady state of
8,152 ng·h/mL.120 Using the previously stated rat data, this provides an exposure
margin of 1.5. It is noted that the data from Trial 1102 were derived from BID dosing and
reported as AUC0-12, then those values were doubled to approximate AUC0-24. Although
this conservative approach likely overestimates AUC0-24 following once daily dosing, this
is considered appropriate given the additional uncertainties in the clinical PK data
discussed previously. On the other hand, based on the lack of M8 data at steady state,
the human AUC0-24 is considered an underestimate of total drug-related exposure.
Including M8 in this value could conservatively be estimated to double the total steady119 The total drug-related exposure (AUC) reported here was determined by summing the reported AUC

values of tradipitant, M2, M3, M4, and M8 in humans administered tradipitant as a single dose of 85 or
170 mg in Trial 1002. It is acknowledged that this calculated total includes some uncertainty versus
calculating the total of all analytes for each pharmacokinetic timepoint and deriving the total AUC from
those values, due to the extrapolation of the terminal phase. However, data were not available at the time
of this review to enable the precise calculation of total drug-related exposure. The uncertainty or slight
inaccuracy associated with the current approach is not expected to affect the overall safety determination.
120 The total drug-related exposure (AUC) reported here was determined by summing the reported AUC
values of tradipitant, M2, M3, and M4 in humans administered tradipitant at 85 mg twice daily for 12 days
in Trial 1102. It is acknowledged that this calculated total includes some uncertainty versus calculating the
total of all analytes for each pharmacokinetic timepoint and deriving the total AUC from those values, due
to the extrapolation of the terminal phase. However, data were not available at the time of this review to
enable the precise calculation of total drug-related exposure. The uncertainty or slight inaccuracy
associated with the current approach is not expected to affect the overall safety determination.

state AUC (although, more likely, it would be closer to 1.5 times the reported value
based on available data). This would provide an exposure margin of 1.5 ÷ 2 = 0.75. This
exposure margin is considered acceptable to qualify the levels of metabolites M2, M4,
and M8 at steady state based on total drug-related exposure in the context of this
development program.

12.2.6 Conclusions and Recommendation
As discussed, the Applicant has identified four major human metabolites of tradipitant:
M2, M3, M4, and M8. Prior to submission of NDA 220152, because of limited clinical
pharmacology data, it could not be determined if these metabolites individually
represent ≥10% of total drug-related exposure at steady state, although tradipitant itself
did not seem to meet this threshold.121 However, based on all data available at the time
of this review, it was determined that adequate nonclinical assessment of these
metabolites is warranted to support the safety of tradipitant for its intended clinical use.
Based on the available data, three of the four major human metabolites (i.e., M2, M4,
and M8) are present at disproportionately higher levels in humans compared to species
used in the nonclinical toxicology studies and therefore had not been qualified based on
in vivo data. However, using a WoE approach for M2, M4, and M8, taking into
consideration data from a (Q)SAR analysis as well as primary pharmacology, secondary
pharmacology, pharmacokinetic, general toxicity, special toxicity, and genotoxicity
studies with each metabolite and/or tradipitant, the review team has determined that
each metabolite is expected to have a similar toxicological profile to that of tradipitant,
and no potential for increased or novel toxicities was identified. Therefore, it was
considered acceptable to qualify the safety of these metabolites based on total drugrelated exposure rather than on an individual basis.

121As previously discussed, tradipitant accounted for approximately 9% of total drug-related radioactivity

in the plasma, based on AUC0-inf, following a single dose of 25 mg in a mass balance study (Trial H8RMC-HJAK). Total drug-related exposure at steady state was not evaluated in any clinical study.

155
Reference ID: 5719891

NDA # 220152

Sarah J. Morgan, PhD

Based on total tradipitant-related drug exposure, the levels of M2, M4, and M8 are
considered qualified under both the single-dose and steady-state scenarios for the
prevention of vomiting induced by motion. However, as the nonclinical data supporting
the safety of unrestricted use of tradipitant over a lifetime are considered inadequate,
the safety of the metabolites is likewise not considered to be adequately supported by
nonclinical data for unrestricted use of tradipitant over a lifetime.

Appendix 14.2. Individual Study Review
By: Sanjida Mahjabeen, Ph.D.; Shen Li, Ph.D.

1. Individual PK Study Review
1.1 Trial H8R‐EW‐HJAC
Title
Single oral dose-escalating study to assess the safety and pharmacokinetics of LY686017 in
healthy subjects.
Study Design
This was a single-center, subject-blind, crossover, randomized, placebo-controlled, doseescalating study in healthy male subjects. Subjects received LY686017 dosing under fasted
conditions according to the following treatment sequence (Table 1). The washout duration
between periods within subjects was at least 9 days.
Table 1. Treatment Sequence, Study H8R-EW HJAC

Source: Table HJAC.9.2. of Study H8R-EW-HJAC CSR
Abbreviations: CSR, clinical study report; n, number of subjects in treatment group

Formulation
1 mg, 10 mg, 100 mg capsules.
Results
Tradipitant exposure [AUC0-inf and Cmax] was nonlinear with dose over the evaluated dose range.
With a 16-fold increase in dose from 50 mg to 800 mg, AUC0-inf increased by 3-fold and Cmax
increased by 4-fold (Table 2). The less than dose-proportional increase in the systemic exposure
may at least partially due to the low solubility of tradipitant.

Abbreviations: CV, coefficient of variation

Receptor Occupancy Assessment
Receptor occupancy increased with dose in the frontal cortex and the maximum occupancy in the
frontal cortex (93% ±4) was determined 24 hours after the last 100 mg dose on Day 28.
However, the clinical relevance of this receptor occupancy assessment is unclear.

Reference ID: 5719441

1.3 Trial 1002 (Food effect study)
Title
A four-period, two-way crossover open-label study evaluating the pharmacokinetics of a single
dose of tradipitant in healthy participants.
Study Design
This was an open-label, four-period, two-way crossover study to evaluate the pharmacokinetics
of a single dose of the capsule formulation of tradipitant in healthy subjects in a fed and fasted
state. The washout period (at least 11 days) is acceptable given the reported the elimination halflife (t1/2) up to 31.6 hours.


Subjects in Cohort 1 received each of the following treatments:
- Single dose of 170-mg tradipitant by two 85 mg capsules, fasted
- Single dose of 170-mg tradipitant by two 85 mg capsules + high fat/high calorie meal



Subjects in Cohort 2 received each of the following treatments:
- Single dose of 85-mg tradipitant, fasted
- Single dose of 85-mg tradipitant + high fat/high calorie meal

Based on the response to the information request dated January 5, 2024, all subjects fasted
overnight (10 hours) before dosing on Day 1. For the fed condition, a high-calorie, high-fat meal
was eaten 30 minutes or less prior to administration of study medication and finished prior to
dosing. The high-calorie, high-fat meal contained the following: 2 eggs cooked in butter, 2
pieces of buttered toast, 2 strips of bacon, 4 oz. of hash browns, and a cup of whole milk. In both
conditions, an additional meal was provided 4 hours following dosing.
Formulation
85 mg capsule.
PK Results
Geometric mean plasma concentration-time profiles for tradipitant and metabolites are presented
in Figure 1, Figure 2, and Figure 3, respectively. Concomitant high fat meal significantly
increased the systemic exposure to tradipitant and its 4 metabolites (Table 6, Table 9, and Table
10).

1.4 Trial H8R‐MC‐HJAK (Mass Balance Study)
Title
Clinical pharmacology study report: disposition after oral administration of 14C-labeled NK-1
Antagonist II (LY686017)
Study Design
Six healthy male subjects received a single oral dose of 10 mL of polyethylene glycol 400
solution containing a total of 25 mg of LY686017 (approximately 100 μCi of LY686017-14C) on
Day 1. Five subjects completed the study. One subject discontinued after screening visit due to a
personal reason.
Results
The Cumulative Excretion of Radioactivity
Following a single 25 mg (100 μCi) oral dose of LY686017-14C, the mean total recovery of
radioactivity was approximately 88% (80.11% in feces and 7.14% in urine) of the dose recovered
within 312 hours postdose (13 days). 85% of radioactivity was recovered by 168 hours postdose.
The unchanged parent drug was not detected in urine and minimum in feces. LY686017 did not
appear to distribute consistently into red blood cells. The mean percentage of radioactivity in red
blood cells was approximately 20% at 0.5 hours postdose but were either minimal or less than
zero after 1 hour.

Figure 4. Mean ± SEM Cumulative Percentages of Dose Excreted Following the Administration of a
Single 25 mg (100 μCi) Oral Dose of LY686017-14C in Healthy Subjects, Study H8R-MC-HJAK

Source: Figure HJAK.7.1. of H8R-MC-HJAK Study Report
Abbreviation: SEM, standard error of the mean

Plasma Pharmacokinetics and Total Radioactivity in Plasma
Metabolite profiling in plasma samples was conducted in samples collected up to 24 hours
postdose.
During the first 24 hours after dosing, tradipitant, M2, M3, M4, and M8 were detected as the
main identified entities in plasma samples based on radio chromatogram. Metabolite profiling in
plasma samples based on radioactivity was not conducted for plasma samples collected after 24
hours. Pharmacokinetics of LY686017 (tradipitant), M2, M3, and M4 but not M8 was further
characterized using a liquid chromatographic separation with tandem mass spectrometry (LCMS/MS) to estimate AUC0-inf. Based on AUC0-inf assessed using a validated LC-MS/MS Method
LY686017, M2, M3, and M4 accounted for approximately 9%, 4%, 5%, and 8% of radioactivity,
respectively. Compared to mean t1/2 ranged from 20 to 30 hours for LY686017, M2, M3, and
M4, the mean t1/2 of radioactivity was 113 hours, indicating that the t1/2 of radioactivity is likely
comprised of nonmonitored metabolite(s) with a substantially longer t1/2.
Of note, in Trial 1002, the mean half-life of M8 was 36.3 hours and it was 31.6 hours for
tradipitant.

Metabolic Profile of LY686017
Multiple metabolic pathways were identified and the major pathways (>5% of the dose) included
ketone reduction (M2 and M4), N-oxidation (M3 and M4) and N-glucuronidation at the
monosubstituted pyridine ring (M8). The chiral metabolites (M2 and M4) were observed in
plasma and/or urine as racemic mixtures. Metabolites M17, M35, M58, and M59 were the major
components excreted in feces. Metabolite M60 was the major urinary metabolite, but it
accounted for <2% of the dose.

15
Reference ID: 5719441

Figure 5. Proposed Major Metabolic Pathways of LY686017-14C in Healthy Subjects, Study H8RMC-HJAK

Source: Figure HJAK.7.2. of Study H8R-MC-HJAK CSR
Note: The Applicant noted in the submission that M2 was inadvertently mislabeled as present in Plasma (P) and Urine (U) and
should have been labeled as present in Plasma (P) and Feces (F).
Abbreviations: CSR, clinical study report; M, metabolite

Metabolites in Feces
Ten metabolites that were common to all five subjects were detected by radioactivity and
identified by mass spectrometry in the fecal extracts. Metabolites M17, M35, M58, and M59
were the major parent-related components in feces, accounting for 9%, 9%, 9%, and 6% of the
dose in the selected fecal samples, respectively. Each of the other metabolites accounted for <5%
of the dose in the selected fecal samples. Together, the identified metabolites in the selected fecal
samples accounted for 44.1% of the dose. A series of minor metabolites that were detected by
radioactivity but not identified constituted a total of approximately 11% of the dose in the
selected fecal samples.

16
Reference ID: 5719441

Metabolites in Urine
Ten radiolabeled metabolites that were common to all 5 subjects were detected. M60 (glycine
conjugate of chlorobenzoic acid, Compound 29263) was the major urinary metabolite, which
accounted for 1.12% of the dose in the selected urine samples. Each of the other urinary
metabolites accounted for <1% of the dose in the selected samples. Together, identified
metabolites in the selected urine samples accounted for 4.05% of the dose. Parent compound was
not found in detectable amounts in the urine.
1.5 Trial 1102 (Drug Interaction Study With Midazolam)
Title
An open-label, single-sequence study to assess the effect of multiple doses of tradipitant on
cytochrome P450 3A4 using midazolam as a substrate in healthy subjects
Study Design
This was an open-label, single-sequence, 20-day, inpatient study. The dosing schedule is as show
in Table 13. Midazolam oral solution was administered at 10 mg dose between 07:00 and 09:00.
Tradipitant was administered between 07:00 and 09:00 for the morning dosing and between
19:00 and 21:00 for the evening dosing. When the drugs were coadministered (Day 16), subjects
were administered tradipitant first and then midazolam.
Dosing
Based on the response to the information request dated January 5, 2024, subjects fasted for at
least 8 hours prior to the morning drug administration. When confined, subjects received a light
breakfast, standard lunch, standard dinner, and a light snack in the evening. No other food was
taken at any time during confinement (20 days). Meals were served at 9:00, 13:00, and 20:00.
Both the morning and evening doses of tradipitant were provided without food, prior to the
breakfast and dinner services respectively.
Table 13. Dosing Schedule, Trial 1102

Source: Table 9.1.1 of Trial VP-VLY-686-1102 CSR
Abbreviations: CSR, clinical study report; D, day; MDZ, Midazolam; Q12H, every 12 hours

17
Reference ID: 5719441

Results
Following administration of tradipitant doses of 85 mg Q12H for 12 days, when midazolam was
coadministered with tradipitant, midazolam and 1-hydroxymidazolam exposure (Cmax and AUC)
increased up to 16% as compared to when midazolam was administered alone (Table 14).
After administration of tradipitant doses of 85 mg Q12H for 12 days, the accumulation ratios for
tradipitant and its metabolites, based on AUC0-12, ranged from 4.6 to 9.3 (Table 16). The
metabolite-to-parent ratio, based on AUC0-12 on Day 16, ranged from 0.31 to 0.33 for all 3
metabolites (M2. M3, and M4). Based on Ctrough comparison and analysis, it is inconclusive
regarding whether the steady state of tradipitant was reached after 85 mg BID from Days 4 to 16
in healthy subjects due to the lack of additional PK samples after Day 16.

1.6 Trial H8R‐MC‐HJAP (Interaction Study With ethanol)
Title
A multiple dose, fixed sequence, two-period crossover, LY686017-ethanol Interaction study in
healthy volunteers.
Study Design
This was a randomized, double-blind, multiple-dose, two-way cross-over study in healthy
subjects. Briefly, the study consisted of two treatment periods with a washout period of at least 7
days. In each period, subjects received LY686017 at 50 mg once daily or placebo for 7 days. On
Day 6 subjects also received a drink of orange juice 1 hour after dosing. On Day 7 subjects
received a drink of orange juice containing alcohol 1 hour after dosing.
Results
While the Applicant reported the systemic exposure of LY686017 was slightly increased when
50 mg LY686017 was administered 1 hour prior to consumption of orange juice containing
ethanol (20% w/v). To note, the Applicant is proposing 85 mg or 170 mg single dose for current
indication which is for the acute prevention of vomiting induced by motion. Hence the
applicability of this study results to the currently proposed dose levels is unclear because the
tradipitant dose was lower and the formulation used in this study was different from the proposed
to-be-marketed formulation.

20
Reference ID: 5719441

1.7 Trial 1301 (Gastric emptying study)
Title
A single center, randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled study to evaluate the effects of
tradipitant on satiation, gastric volume, gastric accommodation and gastric emptying in healthy
volunteers
Study Design
This was a randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled study conducted in 24 healthy subjects.
Briefly, subjects received tradipitant 85 mg BID for 9 consecutive days. On Day 7, gastric
emptying was measured by scintigraphy. On Day 8, satiation with a nutrient drink test was
measured. On Day 9, gastric accommodation was measured using single photon emission
computed tomography. At the end of each visit (Days 7 to 9), blood samples were also obtained
2 hours postdose for PK assessment.
Results
The gastric emptying time and postprandial gastric volume measures were overall similar
between tradipitant, and placebo and no statistically significant differences were reported. The
clinical relevance of this study to support current indication is unclear.
1.8 Trial 3404 (Phase 3 efficacy study in subjects with history of motion sickness)
Title
A randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled study to investigate the efficacy of tradipitant in
participants affected by motion sickness during travel
Study Design
This was a randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled study to investigate the efficacy and
safety of a single oral dose of tradipitant 85 mg or 170 mg in subjects who had a history of
motion sickness on prior vehicle travel and met inclusion criteria at screening visit (Visit 1, Day
1). Subjects were randomly assigned to one of three following treatment groups stratified by port
and received single dose in efficacy assessment period at Visit 2 (Day 2):


85-mg tradipitant (n=104)



170-mg tradipitant (n=106)



Placebo (n=106)

Subjects were administered their assigned medication approximately 60 minutes prior to the start
of the vehicle travel assessment. There were 20 individual vehicle travel assessments ranging
approximately from 123 to 245 minutes in duration. Subjects had the option to undergo a PK

21
Reference ID: 5719441

assessment at Visit 3 (Day 7 following the dosing at Visit 2). Subjects who agreed were
administered a single oral dose of tradipitant 170 mg. Briefly, subjects received the first dose on
Day 2 and subsequently an optional dose for PK assessment was administered on Day 7 (i.e., 6
days after the first dose). Blood samples were collected for PK assessment at specified time
intervals, including at baseline (predose), and 1 and 2 hours following dosing.
Results
Among 316 study subjects, 171 subjects agreed on the optional PK assessment dosing. Among
these 171 subjects, 61 subjects received placebo, 55 subjects received 85 mg and 55 subjects
received 170 mg single dose of tradipitant at Visit 2 (Day 2). The sparse PK data obtained from
the optional PK assessment period at Visit 3 was grouped by treatment at Visit 2 in Table 18.

Appendix 14.5. Pharmacometrics
By: Hongshan Li, Ph.D.; Vishnu Sharma, Ph.D.

2. Pharmacometrics Assessment
2.1 Executive Summary
The Applicant’s initial population pharmacokinetic (PPK) analysis (submitted on
December 30, 2024) included no PK data from subjects with motion sickness. An information
request (IR) was sent on April 2, 2025, requesting the Applicant conduct additional PPK
modeling and simulation analysis incorporating the PK data for tradipitant and its metabolites
(M2, M3, M4, and M8) in subjects with motion sickness obtained from Trial 3404 and 3403 (if
available). In response to the IR, an updated PPK report was submitted on July 22, 2025, based
on pooled tradipitant and metabolite concentration data from 7 trials (1002, 1102, 1301, 2301,
3301, 3403, and 3404). Key highlights of the PPK models are described below:
 The Applicant’s NONMEM executions finished with unsuccessful minimization, probably
due to the over-parameterization of the models. However, the PPK analysis generally
captured the concentration data reasonably well and the predicted tradipitant and metabolite
exposures appear generally reasonable.
 The observed PK exposures of tradipitant and metabolites of Trial 3403 are higher than other
trials (including Trial 3404, another trial in motion sickness). Per the Applicant, these
differences may reflect the small number of postdose samples obtained in each trial and the
short interval of these samples relative to dosing.
 In addition, each dosing amount was derived via averaging the total dosing micrograms over
a period of time for subjects with poor compliance (as low as 46.4%) added uncertainty to
the PPK modeling and simulation analysis. It might be rational to exclude PK data from
subjects with confirmed low-compliance in the PPK analysis. The FDA reviewer’s
independent PPK analysis of tradipitant suggested one-compartmental model could also be
used to describe the PK data (with low-compliance subject and Trial 1002 excluded).
However, this model also under-predicted the elevated concentrations observed in Trial
3403.

2.2 The Applicant’s Population PK Analysis
2.2.1 Background
The Applicant originally submitted PPK analysis was based on 4126 PK samples collected from
520 subjects in 5 trials (1002, 1102, 1301, 2301, and 3301), without any PK data from subjects
with motion sickness. In response to FDA information request for tradipitant and metabolite
simulations under fed versus fasted conditions dated April 2, 2025, an updated PPK report for
motion sickness was submitted on July 22, 2025, based pooled PK data from 7 trials (1002,

23
Reference ID: 5719441

1102, 1301, 2301, 3301, 3403, and 3404). The pharmacometrics review of PPK analysis is
performed mainly based on the version submitted on July 22, 2025.
2.2.2 Objective
The objective of this analysis was to develop and validate population pharmacokinetic models to
describe exposure of tradipitant and four metabolites (M2, M3, M4, and M8) in subjects with a
history of motion sickness. This analysis included the evaluation of the potential effect of
intrinsic factors, including body size, age,
, and organ function.
2.2.4 Data
A pooled analysis on 5616 tradipitant plasma concentration (ranged 0.5 to 1510 ng/mL) samples
of 1206 subjects from 7 trials (1002, 1102, 1301, 2301, 3301, 3403, and 3404) with orally
administered tradipitant was conducted. The trial designs and population descriptions are briefly
described below:
Trial 1002
This was a two-way crossover open label PK trial of a single dose of tradipitant in healthy
subjects to evaluate the food effect and PK linearity. Subjects received either one or two 85-mg
tradipitant capsules under fasted conditions or fed conditions. The continuous variables and
categorical variables of Trial 1002 are listed in Table 19 and Table 20, respectively.

Trial 1102
This was an open-label, single sequence trial to assess the effect of multiple doses of tradipitant
on cytochrome P450 3A4 using midazolam as a substrate in healthy subjects. A single oral dose
of 10 mg midazolam was administered alone on Day 1, followed by tradipitant 85 mg once daily
on Days 3 to 15, followed by coadministration of single oral doses of tradipitant 85 mg and
midazolam 10 mg on Day 16. The continuous variables and categorical variables of Trial 1102

Trial 3404
This was a randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled trial to investigate the efficacy of
tradipitant in subjects affected by motion sickness during travel. Subjects were randomized in a
1:1:1 ratio to take tradipitant 85 mg, 170 mg, or placebo approximately 60 minutes prior to boat
travel. Subjects returned for an EOS visit within 7 days of Visit 2. PK samples were collected at
1- and 2-hours following dosing during the PK visit from whom agreed to the optional PK
assessment. The continuous variables and categorical variables of Trial 3404 are listed in Table
31 and Table 32, respectively.

2.2.5 Methods
A linear two-compartment PK model with elimination from the central compartment was
adopted. To accommodate large secondary peaks in the fasted sessions of the food effect study,

30
Reference ID: 5719441

the model included two absorption compartments for that session with the dose apportioned
between the two. Parameters were added to the model to accommodate differences in PK
characteristics between studies. Post hoc fits for each study showed minimal bias. Post hoc
parameters were then used to simulate concentration profiles for each subject. Exposure metrics
in two trials (Trials 3403, Trials 3404) were calculated for each subject, then summarized by trial
and presented graphically and in tables and text. First order conditional estimation with EtaSigma interaction was performed using the nonlinear mixed-effects modeling program
(b) (4)
NONMEM, Version 7.5.1 or later (ICON Development Solutions, Ellicott City, MD).
was used for file management and
creation of graphics. Estimates for population mean parameters (typical values), between-subject
variability, residual variability and, if possible, standard errors of the parameters were obtained.
Using NONMEM's ADVAN5, a model was developed for Trial 1002 that addressed the
secondary peaks as well as differences between the fasted and fed sessions (Figure 7). For the
fasted condition, the dose is partitioned between the proximal absorption (F1) and distant
absorption (F2) with F1 + F2 equals 1. For the fed sessions, the entire dose, scaled by Ffed, is
delivered to the central compartment. Distant absorption exists but is ignored because
bioavailability to that compartment (F2) equals 0.
The lower limit of quantitation (LOQ) was 1.00 ng/mL for tradipitant and 0.500 ng/mL for
metabolites. Predose samples with concentrations below the LOQ (BQL) were assigned a value
of 0 and excluded from the analysis. Postdose BQL samples >15 days after the final dose were
also excluded from the analysis. The remaining postdose BQL values were assigned a
concentration of LOQ/2 and included in the analysis.
Figure 7. Structural Model for Tradipitant for Trial 1002 for Food Effect

Source: Figures 6 and 7 of Applicant PPK report submitted on June 30, 2025
Abbreviations: CYP, cytochrome P450; PPK, population pharmacokinetic

In the remaining 6 trials, although subjects were instructed to take the drug on an "empty
stomach," closer to fasted condition, the fed model (right panel of Figure 7) was applied with one
difference: F1=1 (not F1 = Ffed) and F2=0. Conversion of tradipitant to each metabolite was
described by a first-order process from the central compartment of the parent (Figure 8). Datasets
31
Reference ID: 5719441

for metabolites included post hoc (individual) parameters for tradipitant obtained from the
optimal model. Formation of metabolites is driven by the central compartment concentration.
The fraction of tradipitant converted to each metabolite is fixed arbitrarily to 0.1 (described
below). Distribution of metabolite to a peripheral compartment (dashed lines) is optional.
Figure 8. Formation, Distribution, and Elimination of Metabolites of Tradipitant

Source: Figure 9 of Applicant PPK report submitted on June 30, 2025
Abbreviations: CYP, cytochrome P450; PPK, population pharmacokinetic

Metabolites were modeled independently, assuming no interaction between their formation, no
conversion between metabolites, and unidirectional formation of metabolite. Formation of each
metabolite was assumed to be a first-order process in which input to the metabolite central
compartment was driven by the central compartment (plasma) concentration of tradipitant. Two
rate constants describe irreversible movement of tradipitant from its central compartment, one
accounting for elimination, the other for conversion to metabolite. where FxFIRST is the fraction
of the dose directed to the metabolite central compartment and (1 - FxFIRST) is the fraction of
the dose directed to the tradipitant central compartment.
2.2.6 Results
Tradipitant
A two-compartment model with first-order elimination, first-order absorption, and an absorption
lag fit the data well. In the food effect study (Trial 1002), dense sampling revealed two peaks in
the fasted session (but not in the high-fat session). This was addressed with a model that included
two depots with different absorption rates. However, sampling in the other studies was more
limited and allowed only for a single absorption compartment. The estimates of the parameters
of the final PPK model for tradipitant parent are listed in Table 34 with no standard error
available for any parameters probably due to over-parameterization. Based on the final model of
tradipitant, the simulated Day 1 and Day 40 tradipitant exposures under fasted and fed conditions
are listed in Table 35.

Tradipitant Metabolites
Each metabolite was modeled separately, and it was assumed that there was no interaction or
inter-conversion between metabolites. A two-compartment model with first-order elimination fit
the data well. Input to each metabolite was the plasma concentration of tradipitant.
Pharmacokinetic parameters differed between trials. Results, including exposure metrics, are
included in the body of this report. Of note, simulated values for metabolite M8 exposures were
predicted poorly for Trial 3403, most likely because only two samples were obtained both at
early time points, with the second sample showing consistent higher concentrations compared to
the first. Based on the final models of tradipitant metabolites, the simulated Day 1 and Day 40
tradipitant exposures under fasted and fed conditions are listed in Table 36, Table 37, Table 38,
and Table 39 for M2, M3, M4, and M8, respectively.

The Applicant acknowledged that the exposure metrics for metabolite M8 in Trial 3403 should
be considered with extreme caution because the sampling regimen in that trial likely led to
meaningless estimates. For tradipitant and other metabolites, differences in the exposure metrics
for both tradipitant and metabolites between Trial 3403 and Trial 3404 were unexpected because
both trials were conducted in similar populations. These differences may reflect the small
number of postdose samples obtained in each trial and the short interval of these samples relative
to the dose.
As a result of these limitations and given that the general population of patients experiencing
motion sickness is not expected to have significantly different drug exposure (Cmax, AUC)
compared to healthy subjects, the pharmacokinetic results from healthy subjects tested in fasted
and fed conditions in a controlled clinic environment (Trial 1002) should be considered as more
representative than those from Trial 3403 and Trial 3404.

2.3 Reviewer’s Population PK Sensitivity Analysis
2.3.1 Background
The Applicant’s final PPK model did not converge properly and terminated due to rounding error
with NO. OF SIG. DIGITS UNREPORTABLE (as stated in the “lst” file of the final model on
Page 143 of the PPK report).
2.3.2 Objective
To conduct sensitivity analysis to identify a stable PPK model with minimization successful and
with covariance of each parameter reported.

38
Reference ID: 5719441

2.3.3 Data and Methods
Applicant’s final model for tradipitant used dataset “rawdata-250617-154011.csv” with total
5616 records of 1206 subjects from 7 trials. FDA reviewer’s analysis used the same dataset
excluding compliance <97.5% and >102.5% and exclude Trial 1002 where the PK behavior
appears different from other trials, result dataset is named as “fda02.csv” with 3531 records of
915 subjects from 6 trials. NONMEM 7.5 and R 4.3.3 were used. The population values of
continuous and categorical variables of the revised dataset are listed in Table 40 and Table 41,
respectively. General 1, 2, and 3 compartmental models were applied to the data to select a base
structural model, and covariates were applied the base model.

2.3.4 Results
The one compartment model (ADVAN2 TRANS2, with CL and V were different between
motion sickness studies and other studies) was chosen as the structure model for the stability
demonstrated on “fda02.csv” and covariance was available. Although the two compartmental
model (ADVAN4 TRANS4) completed the run on “fda02.csv” with minimization successful,
about 90% runs of the bootstrap terminated due to rounding error. The 3-compartmental model
didn’t finish the run due to numerical difficulty. To address under-prediction issue of high
concentrations, other models, such as Michaelis Menten model, absorption overflow model
(when drug amount in the absorption site is greater than an amount, the overflow portion follows
a different absorption rate) were tested but not successful. For the 1-compt structure model
selected, while ethnicity was identified as statistically significant covariate of some parameters,
the effect size was very small and not meaningful for implementation, so the base model was
identified as the final model. The goodness-of-fit of the final model is shown in Figure 11.
Figure 11. Goodness-of-Fit Plots for FDA PPK Model With Upper Panels for the Whole Dataset and
the Lower Panels for Trial 3403 Only

Source: FDA reviewer’s analysis

pharmacokinetic

Within motion sickness population, the observed and predicted tradipitant and metabolite
exposure in Trial 3403 is significantly higher than that of Trial 3404, and the Applicant attributed
this to random variability by stating the following on Page 22 of the PPK report submitted on
July 22, 2025: “Exposure metrics for metabolite M8 in study VP-VLY-686-3403 should be
considered with extreme caution because the sampling regimen in that study likely led to
meaningless estimates. For tradipitant and other metabolites, differences in the exposure metrics
for both tradipitant and metabolites between VP-VLY-686-3403 and VP-VLY-686-3404 were
unexpected because both studies were conducted in similar populations. These differences may

43
Reference ID: 5719441

reflect the small number of postdose samples obtained in each study and the short interval of
these samples relative to the dose. As a result of these limitations and given that the population
of patients experiencing motion sickness is not expected to result significantly different drug
exposure (Cmax, AUC) compared to healthy volunteers, the pharmacokinetic results from healthy
volunteers tested in fasted and fed conditions in a controlled clinic environment (VP-VLY-6861002) should be considered as more representative than those from VP-VLY-686-3403 and VPVLY-686-3404.”
2.4 Exposure‐Response Analysis
No exposure-response analysis has been submitted for motion sickness indication.
